holds as well, proving claim (a).

Pick any
f\in{B}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}]\setminus{P}
. From
d_{f}\leq\eta^{\prime}=\textsf{nextts}({B},d_{g})
we obtain that

C
is feasible. Inequality
d_{f}>\eta=\textsf{prevts}({P},d_{g})
and
f\not\in{P}
imply that
w_{f}\leq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},d_{g})\leq w_{g}
,
and therefore
w(C)\geq w({B})
, completing the proof of claim (b).
∎

As a corollary, we obtain that Lemma
5.5
(b) holds for
f
equal to
the earliest-deadline packet
{f^{\ast}}
in
{B}\setminus{P}
with
d_{f^{\ast}}>\textsf{prevts}({P},d_{g})
.

Corollary 5.6
.

Consider the current optimal plan
{P}
, the backup plan
{B}
, and the adversary stash
{A}
at time
t
.
Assume that invariants (InvA) and (InvB) hold. Let
g\in{A}
,
\eta=\textsf{prevts}({P},d_{g})
, and
let
{f^{\ast}}
be the earliest-deadline packet in
{B}(\eta,{T}]\setminus{P}
.
Then packet
{f^{\ast}}
exists,
the set
C={B}\setminus{\left\{{f^{\ast}}\right\}}\cup{\left\{g\right\}}
is feasible, and
w_{f^{\ast}}\leq w_{g}
(thus
w(C)\geq w({B})
).

Proof.

Applying Lemma
5.5
(a) to packet
g
and
\eta^{\prime}=\textsf{nextts}({B},d_{g})
gives
us that
{B}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}]\setminus{P}\neq\emptyset
, which in turn implies that
d_{f^{\ast}}\in(\eta,\eta^{\prime}]
.
Then, using Lemma
5.5
(b) for
f={f^{\ast}}
implies the corollary.
∎

Potential function.

We use the backup plan
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to define a potential function needed for the amortized analysis of Algorithm
PlanM
.
If
{B}
is the current snapshot of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
then the potential value at time
t
is

\textstyle\Psi({B})\;=\;\frac{1}{\phi}{\,}w^{t}({B})\,.

(5.4)

For brevity, we will use notation
{\widetilde{\Psi}^{t}}=\Psi({\widetilde{{B}}^{t}})

for the potential at the beginning of step
t
, before any packet arrives,
and
{\Psi^{t}}=\Psi({{{B}}^{t}})
for the potential just before a packet is transmitted in step
t
.

The intuition behind this definition is as follows. In order to be
\phi
-competitive, the average (per step)
profit of Algorithm
PlanM
should be at least
1/\phi
times the
adversary’s average profit. However, due to the choice of coefficients in line 1
of the algorithm, Algorithm
PlanM
tends to postpone transmitting heavy packets with large deadlines.
(For example, given just two packets, a tight packet with weight
1
and a non-tight packet
with weight
2.6<\phi^{2}
, the algorithm will transmit the tight packet in the current
step.) As a result, in tight instances,
PlanM
’s actual profit per step, throughout most of the game,
is often smaller than
1/\phi
times the adversary’s, and only near the end of the
instance, when delayed heavy packets are transmitted, the algorithm can make up for this deficit.

In our amortized analysis, if there is a deficit in a given step, we pay for it with a “loan”
that is represented by an appropriate increase of the potential function.
Eventually, of course, these loans need to be repaid – the potential eventually decreases
to
0
and this decrease must be covered by excess profit.
The formula for the potential
\Psi
is designed to guarantee such future excess profits.
To see this, imagine that no more packets arrive. Since
{{\mathcal{B}}}\cap{{\mathcal{A}}}=\emptyset
,
the packets in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
will not be transmitted in the future by the adversary.
If the algorithm does not execute any more leap steps
then it will collect all the packets in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
, which are in total heavier than the packets in
{{\mathcal{B}}}

(as
{{\mathcal{B}}}
is feasible by invariant (InvB)(i) and
each packet
f\in{{\mathcal{B}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
satisfies
w^{t}_{f}\leq{\textsf{minwt}}({{\mathcal{P}}},d^{t}_{f})
at each time step
t
).
On the other hand, if the algorithm executes a leap step, instead of a packet ousted from
{{\mathcal{P}}}
in this step,
the algorithm will collect a packet from
{{\mathcal{B}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
(or a better
packet) after it is added to the optimal plan as a substitute packet.

5.3
Overview of the Analysis

This section gives an overview of the analysis, states the main theorem,
and shows how it follows from results that will be established in the sections that follow.

Initial and final state.

At the beginning, per assumption
(A1)
,
we assume that the optimal plan is pre-filled with virtual
0
-weight packets,
each in a slot equal to its deadline, and none of them scheduled by the adversary for transmission. The adversary stash
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is empty,
i.e., before the first step (at time
0
) we have
\widetilde{{A}}^{0}=\emptyset
, and
the backup plan is the same as the optimal plan, i.e.,
\widetilde{{B}}^{0}=\widetilde{{P}}^{0}
.
Thus invariants (InvA) and (InvB) clearly hold, and
\widetilde{\Psi}^{0}=0
.
At the end, after all (non-virtual) packets expire, the potential equals
0
as well, i.e.,

\widetilde{\Psi}^{{T}+1}=0
, where
{T}
is the time horizon (the last step).

Amortized analysis.

At the core of our analysis are bounds relating amortized profits of the algorithm and the adversary in each step
t
.
For packet arrivals in step
t
, we will show the following
packet-arrivals inequality
:

{\Psi^{t}}-{\widetilde{\Psi}^{t}}\geq 0\,.

(5.5)

For the transmission event in a step
t
, we will show that the following
packet-transmission inequality
holds:

\phi{\,}w^{t}({\textsf{ALG}}[t])-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})+({\widetilde{\Psi}^{t+1}}-{\Psi^{t}})\;\geq\;\textsf{advcredit}^{t},

(5.6)

where
{\textsf{ALG}}[t]
is the packet in slot
t
in the algorithm’s schedule
ALG
(thus
w^{t}({\textsf{ALG}}[t])
is the algorithm’s profit),
and
\Delta^{t}{w}
is the total amount by which the algorithm increases the weights of its pending packets in step
t
.

We prove the packet-arrivals inequality (
5.5
) in Section
5.4

and the packet-transmission inequality (
5.6
) in Section
5.5
.
Assuming that these two inequalities hold, we now show our main result.

Theorem 5.7
.

Algorithm
PlanM
is
\phi
-competitive.

Proof.

We show that
\phi{\,}w^{0}({\textsf{ALG}})\geq w^{0}({\textsf{OPT}})
, which implies the theorem.
First, by Claim
5.1
, we have
w^{0}({\textsf{OPT}})\leq\sum_{t}\textsf{advcredit}^{t}
. Second, note that

\sum_{t}\,(\,{\widetilde{\Psi}^{t+1}}-{\widetilde{\Psi}^{t}}\,)\;=\;\widetilde{\Psi}^{T+1}-\widetilde{\Psi}^{0}\;=\;0\,,

(5.7)

where
\widetilde{\Psi}^{0}=0
is the initial potential and
\widetilde{\Psi}^{{T}+1}=0
is the final potential after the last step
{T}
.
Observe also that

\sum_{t}[\,w^{t}(\textsf{ALG}[t])-\Delta^{t}{w}\,]\;\leq\;w^{0}(\textsf{ALG})\,.

(5.8)

This follows from the observation that if the weight of
\textsf{ALG}[\tau]
was
increased by some value
\delta>0
at some step
t^{\prime}<\tau
,
then
\delta
also contributes to
\Delta^{t^{\prime}}{w}
, so such contributions cancel out in (
5.8
).
(There may be several such
\delta
’s, as the weight of a packet may have been increased multiple times.
Note that the bound (
5.8
) may not be tight if some packets with increased weights are later dropped.)

Summarizing, using the above bounds yields

\displaystyle w^{0}({\textsf{OPT}})\;

\displaystyle\leq\;\sum_{t}\textsf{advcredit}^{t}

(5.9)

\displaystyle\leq\;\sum_{t}\Big[\phi{\,}w^{t}(\textsf{ALG}[t])-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})+({\widetilde{\Psi}^{t+1}}-{\Psi^{t}})\Big]+\sum_{t}({\Psi^{t}}-{\widetilde{\Psi}^{t}})

(5.10)

\displaystyle=\;\sum_{t}\Big[\phi{\,}w^{t}(\textsf{ALG}[t])-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})\Big]+\sum_{t}({\widetilde{\Psi}^{t+1}}-{\widetilde{\Psi}^{t}})

\displaystyle\leq\;\phi{\,}w^{0}(\textsf{ALG})\,,

(5.11)

where inequality (
5.9
) is (
5.1
), inequality (
5.10
)
follows by applying (
5.5
) and (
5.6
) for each step
t
, and inequality (
5.11
)
holds by (
5.7
) and (
5.8
).
∎

5.4
Packet Arrivals

Let
t
be the current time step. Our aim in this section is to prove that invariants (InvA) and (InvB)
can be preserved as packets arrive in step
t
, using appropriate modifications of sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.
We also prove that the packet-arrivals inequality (
5.5
) holds for step
t
.
To this end, it is sufficient to show how to preserve both invariants, without decreasing the value of
w({{\mathcal{B}}})

(and thus
\Psi
as well), in response to an arrival of each individual packet.

Thus, consider the arrival event of a packet
s
at time
t
. Let
{P}
be the optimal plan just before
s
arrives and
let
{Q}
be the optimal plan just after
s
arrives.
Furthermore, let
{A}
and
{B}
be the snapshots of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
just before
s
arrives,
and let
{A}_{+s}
and
{B}_{+s}
denote, respectively, the snapshots of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}

just after
s
arrives and the changes described below are applied.
The algorithm does not change the weights and deadlines after packet arrivals, so we will omit the
superscript
t
in the notation for weights and deadlines, that is
w_{q}=w^{t}_{q}
and
d_{q}=d^{t}_{q}
, for
each packet
q
. There are two cases, depending on whether or not
s\in{Q}
.

Case A.1
:

s
is not added to the optimal plan
{{\mathcal{P}}}
, i.e.,
{Q}={P}
.
Lemma
A.2
implies that
w_{s}<{\textsf{minwt}}({P},d_{s})={\textsf{minwt}}({Q},d_{s})
.
In this case, we do not change
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, i.e.,
{A}_{+s}={A}
,
so invariant (InvA) is preserved. The backup plan
{{\mathcal{B}}}
remains the same as well, so

w({{\mathcal{B}}})
does not change and invariant (InvB) is preserved.

Case A.2
:

s
is added to the optimal plan
{{\mathcal{P}}}
. Let
u
be the lightest packet in
{P}
with
d_{u}\leq\textsf{nextts}({P},d_{s})
;
by assumption
(A1)
such
u
exists. By the choice of
u
and Lemma
A.2
, we have

{Q}={P}\cup{\left\{s\right\}}\setminus{\left\{u\right\}}
,
\textsf{nextts}({P},d_{u})\leq\textsf{nextts}({P},d_{s})
,
and
w_{s}>w_{u}={\textsf{minwt}}({P},d_{s})={\textsf{minwt}}({P},d_{u})
.

Replacing
u
by
s
in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
can also trigger changes in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.
We describe these changes in two parts:

(i) First, we show that if
u\in{A}
, then we can remove
u
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
,
preserving the invariants and not decreasing the potential.

(ii) Then, assuming that
u\notin{{\mathcal{A}}}
, we describe and analyze the remaining changes.

Let
{A}_{\mathrm{(i)}}
denote the intermediate adversary stash, after the change in (i) and before the change in (ii), where we let

{A}_{\mathrm{(i)}}={A}
if
u\notin{A}
, that is when change (i) does not apply.
We adopt the same notation for snapshots of set
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.

(i) Dealing with
u\in{A}
.

In this case we need to remove
u
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, to preserve invariant (InvA). But this also forces us
to add
u
to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, in order to preserve (InvB)(ii), which in turn requires removing some packet from
{{\mathcal{B}}}

to preserve its feasibility.

To implement these changes, we use Lemma
5.5
with
g=u
, which gives us that there
is a packet
f_{u}\in{B}\setminus{P}
such that the set
{B}\setminus{\left\{f_{u}\right\}}\cup{\left\{u\right\}}

is feasible and
w({B}\setminus{\left\{f_{u}\right\}}\cup{\left\{u\right\}})\geq w({B})
. We then let

{A}_{\mathrm{(i)}}\;=\;{A}\setminus{\left\{u\right\}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad{B}_{\mathrm{(i)}}\;=\;{B}\setminus{\left\{f_{u}\right\}}\cup{\left\{u\right\}}.

Since
u
is scheduled in
A
in interval
[t,d_{u}]
and

w_{u}={\textsf{minwt}}({P},d_{u})
, the adversary adjustment credit associated with removing
u
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is

w_{u}-{\textsf{minwt}}({P},d_{u})=0
(see Section
5.1
).
As explained above, we also have that
{B}_{\mathrm{(i)}}
is feasible and

w({B}_{\mathrm{(i)}})\geq w({B})
; thus
invariant (InvB) is preserved and the change of
w({{\mathcal{B}}})
is non-negative.

(ii) Analysis of other changes.

By (i) we can now assume that
u\notin{A}_{\mathrm{(i)}}
, and invariant (InvB)(ii) together with
u\in{P}
imply that
u\in{B}_{\mathrm{(i)}}
.
We now consider the changes of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
resulting from including
s
in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
.
We analyze two subcases, depending on whether or not
s\in{\textsf{OPT}}
.

Case A.2.a
:

s\in{\textsf{OPT}}
. We add
s
to
{{\mathcal{A}}}
in the same slot as in
OPT
. Specifically, we take

{A}_{+s}={A}_{\mathrm{(i)}}\cup{\left\{s\right\}}
and
{B}_{+s}={B}_{\mathrm{(i)}}
.
Invariant (InvA) is preserved as
s\in{A}_{+s}
,
invariant (InvB) holds as
{{\mathcal{B}}}
remains unaffected in (ii), and the value of
w({{\mathcal{B}}})
does not change.

Case A.2.b
:

s\notin{\textsf{OPT}}
. In this case, we simply let
{A}_{+s}={A}_{\mathrm{(i)}}
,
so invariant (InvA) continues to hold. However,
s
needs to get added to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to preserve invariant (InvB)(ii), so
we need to remove some packet from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to maintain its feasibility, and
this packet must be lighter than
s
to assure that the potential does not decrease.
Define
\xi=\textsf{nextts}({P},d_{s})
and
\xi_{{B}}=\textsf{nextts}({B}_{\mathrm{(i)}},d_{s})
.
We have two cases.

If
d_{u}\leq\xi_{{B}}
, we replace
u
by
s
in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
. That is, we let

{B}_{+s}={B}_{\mathrm{(i)}}\cup{\left\{s\right\}}\setminus{\left\{u\right\}}
, and this satisfies invariant (InvB)(ii).
The case condition implies that invariant (InvB)(i) continues to hold, and,
as
w_{u}<w_{s}
in Case A.2, the value of
w({{\mathcal{B}}})
does not decrease.

Next, assume that
d_{u}>\xi_{{B}}
. Note that
d_{u}\leq\xi
, by the definition of
u
.
Let
\lambda=\textsf{prevts}({P},d_{s})
. In this case we have that
\lambda<d_{s}\leq\xi_{{B}}<d_{u}\leq\xi
,
so
d_{u}
and
d_{s}
are in the same segment
(\lambda,\xi]
of
{P}
.
Since
\lambda
is a tight slot for
{P}
,
\xi_{{B}}
is a tight slot for
{B}_{\mathrm{(i)}}
, and
{\textsf{pslack}}({P},\xi_{{B}})>0
,
from Lemma
5.3
with
\eta=\lambda
and
\eta^{\prime}=\xi_{{B}}
we get that

|{B}_{\mathrm{(i)}}(\lambda,\xi_{{B}}]\setminus{P}|\;=\;{\textsf{pslack}}({B}_{\mathrm{(i)}},\lambda)+{\textsf{pslack}}({P},\xi_{{B}})+|{A}_{\mathrm{(i)}}(\lambda,\xi_{{B}}]|\;>\;0\,,

implying
{B}_{\mathrm{(i)}}(\lambda,\xi_{{B}}]\setminus{P}\neq\emptyset
.
Choose any
f\in{B}_{\mathrm{(i)}}(\lambda,\xi_{{B}}]\setminus{P}
and let

{B}_{+s}={B}_{\mathrm{(i)}}\cup{\left\{s\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f\right\}}
, preserving invariant (InvB)(ii).
Invariant (InvB)(i) holds for
{B}_{+s}
because

d_{f}\leq\xi_{{B}}=\textsf{nextts}({B}_{\mathrm{(i)}},d_{s})
.
The optimality of
{P}
and the choice of
u
and
f
imply
that
w_{f}\leq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},d_{s})=w_{u}<w_{s}
, thus
w({{\mathcal{B}}})
cannot decrease.

Summarizing, we showed that in response to the arrival of any packet
s
in step
t

we can modify
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
in such a way that the invariants (InvA) and (InvB)
will be preserved and the value of
\Psi
will not decrease.
This gives us that the packet-arrivals inequality (
5.5
) holds for step
t

and that after packet arrivals both invariants (InvA) and (InvB) will hold,
concluding the analysis of packet arrivals in step
t
.

5.5
Transmitting a Packet

Let
t
be the current step of the computation.
After all packets with release time equal to
t
arrive, the algorithm transmits its packet
p={\textsf{ALG}}[t]
. Recall that

{{{P}}^{t}}
is the optimal plan just before transmitting
p
and
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}
is the optimal plan
after the algorithm transmits
p
, possibly adjusts weights and deadlines, and after the time is incremented to
t+1
.

We split the analysis of the
transmission step into two parts, called the adversary step and the algorithm’s step, defined as follows:

Adversary step:

If packet
{{{A}}^{t}}[t]
is defined, say
{{{A}}^{t}}[t]=j
, then we need to remove it from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
. (Recall that packets in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
may have been removed
or replaced during the analysis, so
j
may not be equal to
{\textsf{OPT}}[t]
, the packet scheduled in
OPT
at time
t
.)
Removing
j
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
could trigger a change in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, but the optimal plan
{{\mathcal{P}}}
remains unchanged.
We show that these changes preserve both invariants (InvA) and (InvB). We should stress that
these changes are made without advancing the current time, which will be done in algorithm’s step.

In the analysis of the adversary step, given in Section
5.5.1
, we establish a relation (inequality (
5.12
))
between the adversary transmission credit and the change of the potential. By
{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
and
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
we will denote the snapshots of sets

{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, respectively, right after the adversary step.

Algorithm’s step:

In the algorithm’s step, the algorithm transmits
p
, the time is incremented to
t+1
, and the optimal plan
changes from
{{{P}}^{t}}
to
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}
.

The analysis of this step assumes that the changes
described in the adversary step have already been implemented.
Using the bound (
5.12
), invariants (InvA) and (InvB),
and other properties, we then show that the packet-transmission inequality (
5.6
) holds after
the sets
{{\mathcal{P}}}
,
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
are updated to reflect the changes triggered by the packet’s transmission.
We also ensure that invariants (InvA) and (InvB) are preserved.

The analysis of the algorithm’s step is given in
Sections
5.5.2
-
5.5.6
.
We first analyze the ordinary step in Section
5.5.2
.
We then give a roadmap for the analysis of a leap step in Section
5.5.3
,
as it will be divided into substeps. We analyze the particular substeps
in Section
5.5.4
, which
describes the changes in the initial segment
[t,\alpha]
of
{{{P}}^{t}}
,
and in Sections
5.5.5
-
5.5.6
,
which contain the analysis of other changes resulting from a leap step.

In Table
1
, we summarize the notation of snapshots that we use in this section.

{{\mathcal{P}}},{{\mathcal{B}}}
, and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
used in the analysis.
The particular snapshots for each substep of the analysis of a leap step will be defined later, in the respective sections.

5.5.1
Adversary Step

As defined in Section
5.1
,
if
{{{A}}^{t}}
contains a packet in slot
t
, then the adversary gains the transmission credit of
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}=w^{t}({{{A}}^{t}}[t])
.
Otherwise, the adversary’s transmission credit equals
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},t)
.
(The overall adversary credit for this step also includes the adjustment credit,
but in this section we focus only on the relation between the transmission credit and the potential change in the adversary step.)

Except for possibly removing packet
{{{A}}^{t}}[t]
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, if it is defined,
we will not make other changes to
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, so invariant (InvA) will be preserved.
Below we show that with appropriate changes invariant (InvB) will also be preserved after the adversary step.
Further, denoting by
\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi
the change of the potential in the adversary step, we prove the following auxiliary inequality:

\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}\;\geq\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w^{t}_{p}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w^{t}(\textsf{sub}^{t}(p))}\,.

(5.12)

Recall that throughout Section
5.5
we denote the packet scheduled by the algorithm in step
t
by
p
.

The proof of inequality (
5.12
) is divided into two cases, depending on whether or not
{{{A}}^{t}}[t]
is defined.
As packet weights are not changed in the adversary step, below we omit the superscript
t
in the notation for weights.

Case ADV.1
:

{{{A}}^{t}}
contains a packet in slot
t
. Let
j={{{A}}^{t}}[t]
.
We will now remove
j
from the adversary stash
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and, since by invariant (InvA) we have
j\in{{{P}}^{t}}
,
we need to add packet
j
to the backup plan
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to maintain invariant (InvB)(ii),
which in turn requires removing a packet from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to preserve its feasibility.
To this end, we apply Lemma
5.5
(with
g=j
), which implies that there is
a packet
f_{j}\in{{{B}}^{t}}\setminus{{{P}}^{t}}
such that
d_{f_{j}}>\textsf{prevts}({{{P}}^{t}},d_{j})
,
w_{f_{j}}\leq w_{j}
, and
for which set
{{{B}}^{t}}\setminus{\left\{f_{j}\right\}}\cup{\left\{j\right\}}
is feasible.

We thus set
{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}={{{A}}^{t}}\setminus{\left\{j\right\}}
and
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}={{{B}}^{t}}\setminus{\left\{f_{j}\right\}}\cup{\left\{j\right\}}
.
Invariant (InvA) is clearly preserved,
and by Lemma
5.5
, invariant (InvB) continues to hold as well. The potential change is

\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi\;=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(w({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}})-w({{{B}}^{t}})\right)\;=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}(-w_{f_{j}}+w_{j}).

(5.13)

From the definition of
\textsf{sub}^{t}(j)
,
d_{f_{j}}>\textsf{prevts}({{{P}}^{t}},d_{j})
, and
f_{j}\notin{{{P}}^{t}}
,
we have that
f_{j}
is a candidate for
\textsf{sub}^{t}(j)
, and thus
w(\textsf{sub}^{t}(j))\geq w_{f_{j}}
.
Using (
5.13
) and
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}=w_{j}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\phi{\,}(\,\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}\,)\;

\displaystyle=\;-w_{f_{j}}+w_{j}-\phi{\,}w_{j}

\displaystyle=\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{j}}-w_{f_{j}}

\displaystyle\geq\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{j}}-{w(\textsf{sub}^{t}(j))}

\displaystyle\geq\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{p}}-{w(\textsf{sub}^{t}(p))}\,,

(5.14)

where inequality (
5.14
) follows from the choice of
p

in line
1
of the algorithm’s description, using also that
j\in{{{P}}^{t}}
. This implies (
5.12
).

Case ADV.2
:
Slot
t
is empty in
{{{A}}^{t}}
. In this case, we do not change
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
,
so
{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}={{{A}}^{t}}
and
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}={{{B}}^{t}}
.
Invariants (InvA) and (InvB) are trivially preserved. Recall that

\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},t)=w_{\ell}
, where
{\ell}
denotes the lightest packet in the initial segment of
{{{P}}^{t}}
.
Note that
\textsf{sub}^{t}({\ell})={\ell}
and that
\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi=0
. Then

\displaystyle\phi{\,}(\,\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}\,)\;

\displaystyle=\;-\phi{\,}w_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}}-w(\textsf{sub}^{t}({\ell}))

\displaystyle\geq\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{p}}-w(\textsf{sub}^{t}(p))\,,

(5.15)

where inequality (
5.15
) follows from the choice of
p
again.
Thus (
5.12
) holds.

This concludes the analysis of the adversary step. In particular, we have determined the snapshots

{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
and
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
, of the adversary stash

{{\mathcal{A}}}
and the backup plan
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, respectively, resulting from the adversary step.
Next, in the following sections, we analyze the algorithm’s step.

5.5.2
Ordinary Step

We now assume that the adversary step, as described in the previous section, has already been implemented. The current optimal
plan
{{{P}}^{t}}
remains unchanged in the adversary step, and the current snapshots of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
are

{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
and
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
.
In this section we analyze the algorithm’s move at step
t
, assuming this is an ordinary step, as defined in Section
4
.

In an ordinary step a packet
p\in{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]
is transmitted,
where
\alpha=\textsf{nextts}^{t}(t)
is the first tight slot in
{{{P}}^{t}}
. The algorithm makes no changes in packet weights
and deadlines, so
\Delta^{t}{w}=0
. Thus, to simplify notation,
for any packet
q
we can write
w_{q}=w^{t}_{q}
and
d_{q}=d^{t}_{q}
, omitting the superscript
t
.
As usual,
{\ell}
denotes the lightest packet in the initial segment
{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]
.

By the algorithm,
p
is the heaviest packet in
{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]
.
Since
\textsf{sub}^{t}(p)={\ell}
, inequality (
5.12
) gives us that

\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}\;\geq\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w_{p}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w_{\ell}\,.

(5.16)

According to Lemma
A.4
,
the new optimal plan (starting at time slot
t+1
) is
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}={{{P}}^{t}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
.

We have two cases, depending on whether or not the adversary stash contains a packet
in the initial segment
[t,\alpha]
of
{{{P}}^{t}}
.

Case O.1
:

{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}[t,\alpha]=\emptyset
.
In this case,
p\not\in{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
(as
p\in{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]
) and we do not further change set
{{\mathcal{A}}}
,
i.e.,
{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}={{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
. So invariant (InvA) is preserved and
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}=\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}
.

We now show that we can preserve invariant (InvB). As
p\not\in{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
and (InvB)(ii),
we have that
p\in{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
. We modify the backup plan
{{\mathcal{B}}}
by removing
p
, that is

{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
. This immediately gives us that invariant (InvB)(ii) continues to hold.

Next, we show that invariant (InvB)(i) holds as well.
Since
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
is with respect to time
t+1
and
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
is feasible,
we have that
{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}},\tau)\geq 0
for
\tau\geq d_{p}
.
So it remains to consider slots
\tau\in[t+1,d_{p})
.
The case condition and invariant (InvB)(ii) imply that
{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]\subseteq{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
.
This, together with the feasibility of
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
and
\alpha
being a tight slot in
{{{P}}^{t}}
,
gives us that in fact
{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]={{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}[t,\alpha]
.
From this and
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)\geq 1
for
\tau\in[t+1,d_{p})

(as
\alpha\geq d_{p}
is the first tight slot), we get that

{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}},\tau)-1={\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)-1\geq 0
for all
\tau\in[t+1,d_{p})
,
completing the proof that invariant (InvB)(i) continues to hold.

The calculation showing the packet-transmission inequality (
5.6
) is quite simple. Taking into account that
the adversary credit is
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}=\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}
, and that the change of the potential in the algorithm’s step
is
\Delta_{{\mathrm{ALG}}}\Psi={\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}(w({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}})-w({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}))=-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}w_{p}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\phi{\,}w^{t}(\textsf{ALG}[t])

\displaystyle-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})+({\widetilde{\Psi}^{t+1}}-{\Psi^{t}})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}

\displaystyle=\;\phi{\,}w_{p}-\phi\cdot 0+[\,\Delta_{{\mathrm{ALG}}}\Psi+\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi\,]-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}

\displaystyle=\;\phi{\,}w_{p}+\Delta_{{\mathrm{ALG}}}\Psi+[\,\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}\,]

\displaystyle\geq\;\phi{\,}w_{p}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{p}}+\left[\,-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w_{p}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}}\,\right]

(5.17)

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{p}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}}\;\geq\;0\,,

(5.18)

where inequality (
5.17
) uses (
5.16
), and
the inequality in the last step in line (
5.18
) uses the definition of
{\ell}
,
namely that
w_{p}\geq w_{\ell}
.

Case O.2
:

{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}[t,\alpha]\neq\emptyset
. (This includes the case when
p\in{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
.)
We first describe our modifications of sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, and then argue that with these
modifications our invariants are preserved and inequality (
5.6
) is satisfied.

Changing sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.

Let
{g^{\ast}}
be the latest-deadline packet in
{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}[t,\alpha]
. Packet
{g^{\ast}}
exists, by the case condition.
Note that
d_{{g^{\ast}}}\geq t+1
, because
{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
cannot contain a packet with deadline
t

(such a packet would be removed from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
when we analyze the adversary step).
Let
{f^{\ast}}
be the earliest-deadline packet in
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\setminus{{{P}}^{t}}
.
Observation
5.2
(a) with
\eta=t-1
implies that
{f^{\ast}}
is well-defined.
Note that possibly
d_{{f^{\ast}}}=t
, in which case
{f^{\ast}}
cannot remain in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
in the next step.

If
p\in{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
, let
g=p
; otherwise let
g={g^{\ast}}
. (In either case we have
g\in{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
.)
We remove
g
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, i.e., we set
{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}={{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\setminus{\left\{g\right\}}
.
If we have
g={g^{\ast}}\neq p
, then
p\in{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
, so
p
will need to be removed from
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
,
and due to removing
g
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
we will also need to add it to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to satisfy invariant (InvB)(ii).
In either case, we remove
{f^{\ast}}
from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
. Thus the new backup plan will be

{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}\;=\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\setminus{\left\{{f^{\ast}}\right\}}&&\textrm{if\ }g=p\\
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\cup{\left\{{g^{\ast}}\right\}}\setminus{\left\{{f^{\ast}},p\right\}}&&\textrm{if\ }g={g^{\ast}}\neq p\end{array}\right.\,.

Note that in both cases it holds that
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\cup{\left\{g\right\}}\setminus{\left\{{f^{\ast}},p\right\}}
.

For a warm-up, before proving that our invariants hold, let’s verify that all packets in
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
have deadlines at least
t+1
.
Indeed, as already mentioned earlier, we have
d_{{g^{\ast}}}\geq t+1
, and if some
q\in{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}\setminus{\left\{{g^{\ast}}\right\}}
had
d_{q}=t
then,
by the definitions of
{f^{\ast}}
and
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
, this
q
would also be in
{{{P}}^{t}}
, but this implies that

\alpha=t
and
{{{P}}^{t}}[t,t]={\left\{q\right\}}
, contradicting the case condition because
q\notin{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
, by invariant (InvB)(ii).

Preserving the invariants.

We now have
p\notin{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
, so invariant (InvA) is preserved.
We next show that invariant (InvB) holds after the step.
For part (InvB)(ii), we need to show that
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}\cap{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}\setminus{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
.
This can be verified quite easily by considering how
{{\mathcal{P}}}\cap{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{{\mathcal{P}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{A}}}
change:
If
g=p
then both sets do not change, and if
g={g^{\ast}}\neq p
then
p
is replaced by
{g^{\ast}}
in both sets.

To streamline the argument for part (InvB)(i) (feasibility), we divide the process of updating and analyzing
{{\mathcal{B}}}
into
two parts: first we analyze the effects of replacing
{f^{\ast}}
by
g
in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, and then we show that we can remove
p

from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
(and increment
t
), preserving its feasibility.

So in the first part we consider the auxiliary set
{{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}={{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\cup{\left\{g\right\}}\setminus{\left\{{f^{\ast}}\right\}}
.
Directly from Corollary
5.6
, we obtain that
{{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}
is a feasible set of packets at time
t
.
Further, we also show that it has the following property:

Claim 5.8
.

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 1
for any
\tau\in[t,d_{p})
.

Proof.

We first observe that for
\tau\in[t,d_{f^{\ast}})
, by invoking Lemma
5.4
(a) with
\zeta=t-1
,

B={{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
,
A={{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
, and with the current plan
{{{P}}^{t}}
, we can obtain that

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}},\tau)\;\geq\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)+|{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}[t,\tau]|\,.

(5.19)

Now we consider three cases depending on the value of
\tau
. The first case is for
\tau\in[t,\min(d_{{f^{\ast}}},d_{g}))
.
Then

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)\;=\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}},\tau)\;\geq\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)\geq 1\,,

where the equality uses that
{{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}[t,\tau]={{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}[t,\tau]

(as
\tau<\min(d_{{f^{\ast}}},d_{g})
), the first inequality uses (
5.19
), and
the last inequality follows from the fact that
\tau
is not a tight slot of
{{{P}}^{t}}
, as
\tau\in[t,\alpha)
.

In the second case, for slots
\tau\in[d_{g},\min(d_{{f^{\ast}}},d_{p}))
, we note that
g\in{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}[t,\tau]
, so
equation (
5.19
) and
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)\geq 1
imply

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)\;=\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}},\tau)-1\;\geq\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)+|{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}[t,\tau]|-1\;\geq\;1\,.

In the third case, we deal with
\tau\in[d_{{f^{\ast}}},d_{p})
. For
\tau\in[d_{{f^{\ast}}},d_{g})
,
replacing
{f^{\ast}}
by
g
increases the value of
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{B}}},\tau)
by
1
,
implying
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}},\tau)+1\geq 1
, as
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
is feasible.
In particular, we are done if
g=p
. It thus remains to consider the sub-case when
g={g^{\ast}}\neq p
and
\tau\in[\max(d_{{f^{\ast}}},d_{{g^{\ast}}}),d_{p})
,
for which we use Lemma
5.4
(b) with
\zeta=\alpha
,
B={{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
,

A={{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
, and with the current plan
{{{P}}^{t}}
, to get

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)\;=\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}},\tau)\;\geq\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)\;\geq\;1\,,

where the equality uses that
{{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}[t,\tau]={{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}[t,\tau]\cup{\left\{g\right\}}\setminus{\left\{{f^{\ast}}\right\}}

(as
\tau\geq\max(d_{{f^{\ast}}},d_{g})
) and
the last inequality follows from the fact that
\tau
is not a tight slot of
{{{P}}^{t}}
, as
\tau\in[t,\alpha)
.
∎

Next, note that we can express
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
as
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}

— indeed, this works no matter whether
g=p
or
g={g^{\ast}}
.
It remains to show that removing
p
from
{{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}
and incrementing the current time to
t+1
preserves feasibility.
These changes decrease the value of
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{B}}},\tau)
for
\tau\in[t+1,d_{p})
, but
Claim
5.8
ensures that we still have
{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)\geq 0
for such
\tau
.
For
\tau\geq d_{p}
, the value of
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{B}}},\tau)
is not affected.
Hence, invariant (InvB)(i) holds after the step.

Deriving inequality (
5.6
).

Let
\Delta_{{\mathrm{ALG}}}\Psi
be the change of
\Psi
in the algorithm’s step, so

\Delta_{{\mathrm{ALG}}}\Psi=\frac{1}{\phi}(w({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}})-w({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}))=\frac{1}{\phi}(-w_{p}-w_{{f^{\ast}}}+w_{g})
.
The adversary adjustment credit is
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(t,\alpha]}=w_{g}-{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{g})=w_{g}-w_{\ell}
,
since we removed
g
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{g})=w_{\ell}\leq w_{g}
.
Thus, the total adversary credit for step
t
is
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}=\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}+\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(t,\alpha]}=\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}+w_{g}-w_{\ell}
.
(See Section
5.1
for the definition of
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}
.)
Note that
w_{{f^{\ast}}}\leq w_{\ell}
as
{f^{\ast}}\not\in{{{P}}^{t}}
and
that
w_{g}\leq w_{p}
as
p
is the heaviest packet in
{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]
and
g\in{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]
.
We show the packet-transmission inequality (
5.6
) by summing these changes:

\displaystyle\phi{\,}w^{t}(\textsf{ALG}[t])

\displaystyle-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})+({\widetilde{\Psi}^{t+1}}-{\Psi^{t}})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}

\displaystyle=\;\phi{\,}w_{p}-\phi\cdot 0+[\,\Delta_{{\mathrm{ALG}}}\Psi+\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi\,]-[\,\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}+w_{g}-w_{\ell}\,]

\displaystyle=\;\phi{\,}w_{p}+\Delta_{{\mathrm{ALG}}}\Psi-w_{g}+w_{\ell}+[\,\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}\,]

\displaystyle\geq\;\phi{\,}w_{p}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left[\,-{w_{p}}-{w_{{f^{\ast}}}}+{w_{g}}\,\right]-w_{g}+w_{\ell}+\left[\,-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w_{p}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}}\,\right]

(5.20)

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{p}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{{f^{\ast}}}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w_{g}}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}}

\displaystyle\geq\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{p}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w_{p}}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}}

(5.21)

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{3}}}}{\,}(\,{w_{p}}-{w_{\ell}}\,)\;\geq\;0\,,

(5.22)

where inequality (
5.20
) uses (
5.16
),
inequality (
5.21
) holds because
w_{{f^{\ast}}}\leq w_{\ell}
and
w_{g}\leq w_{p}
,
and the last inequality (
5.22
) follows from
w_{p}\geq w_{\ell}
.
This concludes the analysis of an ordinary step.

5.5.3
Leap Step: a Roadmap

In the remainder of the analysis, we focus on a leap step of Algorithm
\textsf{PlanM}(t)
, when it transmits a packet

p\in{{{P}}^{t}}(\alpha,{T}]
, where, as defined earlier,

{{{P}}^{t}}
represents the current optimal plan, already updated to take into account packet arrivals.
As in this case some packet weights change, we use notation
w^{t}_{a}
and
w^{t+1}_{a}

(or
w^{t}(a)
and
w^{t+1}(a)
) for the weights of a packet
a
before and after
p
is transmitted, respectively.
For packets whose weight does not change, we may occasionally omit the superscript
t
.
We apply the same convention to deadlines.

By Lemma
B.1
(a), the new optimal plan (starting at time
t+1
) is

{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}\;=\;{{{P}}^{t}}\setminus{\left\{p,{\ell}\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}\,,

where, as usual,
\varrho=\textsf{sub}^{t}(p)
and
{\ell}
is the lightest packet in
{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]
.

All changes in the optimal plan resulting from transmitting
p
are within two intervals of the plan: the initial segment
[t,\alpha]

and the interval
(\beta,\gamma]
, where
\beta=\textsf{prevts}^{t}(d^{t}_{p})
and
\gamma=\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d^{t}_{\varrho})

(possibly,
\alpha=\beta
). In segment
[t,\alpha]
, packet
{\ell}
is ousted.
In interval
(\beta,\gamma]
(that could consist of several segments), packet
p
gets replaced by
\varrho
, and Algorithm
PlanM

increases the weight of
\varrho
to
\mu={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{\varrho})
.
Furthermore, if this is an iterated leap step (i.e.,
k>1
in the algorithm),
PlanM
also modifies weights and deadlines of some packets
h_{i}
.
These changes in the optimal plan may change some values of
{\textsf{pslack}}(\tau)
and may force changes in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
or
{{\mathcal{B}}}
,
which might in turn trigger additional changes in these sets to restore invariants (InvA) and (InvB).

We divide the analysis of a leap step into two
substeps
, namely (i) processing the initial segment
[t,\alpha]
and (ii) processing interval
(\beta,\gamma]
.
These are outlined later in this section and described in detail in Sections
5.5.4
,

5.5.5
, and
5.5.6
.
Before proceeding to that, we introduce two key inequalities bounding the potential change in these substeps, and we show how to derive from them
the packet-transmission inequality (
5.6
).

Two key inequalities.

We now define several quantities that we will use in our estimates and in the two key inequalities below:

\Delta_{[t,\alpha]}\Psi
:
The change of the potential due to the modifications in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
implemented when processing interval
[t,\alpha]
.
These modifications include the removal of
{\ell}
from
{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]
, as well as other modifications that are triggered by it.

\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi
:
The change of the potential due to the modifications in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
implemented when processing interval
(\beta,\gamma]
.
These modifications include the replacement of
p
by
\varrho
in
(\beta,\gamma]
, the increases of weights and possible decreases of deadlines of some packets,
as well as other modifications that are triggered by these changes.

\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}
: The adversary adjustment credit
for modifications of packets in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
implemented when processing interval
(\beta,\gamma]
,
namely for removing them or replacing them by lighter packets.

\Delta^{t}{w}
:
As already defined earlier, this is the total adjustment of weights of packets in
{{{P}}^{t}}

that is done by the algorithm in step
t

(always strictly positive as the algorithm increases the weight of
\varrho
by a non-zero amount).

Note that we do not define the adversary adjustment credit for processing the initial segment
[t,\alpha]
,
as in that substep of the analysis the adversary does not need to be compensated for any change in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
.

We will derive the proof of the packet-transmission inequality (
5.6
) from the two
key inequalities below, that bound the changes of the potential resulting from processing intervals
[t,\alpha]
and
(\beta,\gamma]
,
respectively:

\displaystyle\Delta_{[t,\alpha]}\Psi

\displaystyle\geq-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}^{t}}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}

\displaystyle\geq-{w_{p}^{t}}+{w_{\varrho}^{t}}

(5.24)

Deriving the packet-transmission inequality.

We now derive (
5.6
) from (
5.23
) and (
5.24
).
We start with two simple but useful bounds. First, using inequality (
5.12
) and the definition of
\varrho
, we have

\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}\;\geq\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}w_{p}^{t}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w_{\varrho}^{t}\,.

(5.25)

Also, for any
\tau\geq t
, we have

{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w_{p}^{t}}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\varrho}^{t}}\;\geq\;w_{\ell}^{t}\;\geq\;{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)\,,

(5.26)

where the first inequality follows from the choice of
p

in line
1
of the algorithm (specifically, because the
algorithm chose
p
over
{\ell}
and because
{\ell}=\textsf{sub}({{{P}}^{t}},{\ell})
),
and the second one follows from
w_{\ell}^{t}={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},t)\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)
, that is
the monotonicity of
{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)
with respect to
\tau
.

So assume now that (
5.23
) and (
5.24
) hold. The total potential change in this step is

{\widetilde{\Psi}^{t+1}}-{\Psi^{t}}\;=\;\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi+\Delta_{[t,\alpha]}\Psi+\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi\,,

because all changes in the optimal plan and in sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
are accounted for (uniquely)
in the terms on the right-hand side. (The changes during the adversary step, accounted for in
\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi
,
were discussed in Section
5.5.1
. The changes associated with the algorithm’s step, that contribute to

\Delta_{[t,\alpha]}\Psi
and
\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi
, will be detailed in
Sections
5.5.4
-
5.5.6
.)
The total adversary credit is the sum of the transmission credit, that was analyzed in Section
5.5.1
,
and the adjustment credits for decreasing some packet weights in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, so

\textsf{advcredit}^{t}\;=\;\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}+\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}\,,

as the changes in
[t,\alpha]
will not involve any weight decreases in the adversary stash
{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Combining it all together, we have

\displaystyle\phi{\,}

\displaystyle w^{t}(\textsf{ALG}[t])-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})+({\widetilde{\Psi}^{t+1}}-{\Psi^{t}})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}

\displaystyle=\;\phi{\,}w_{p}^{t}-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})+[\,\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi+\Delta_{[t,\alpha]}\Psi+\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi\,]-[\,\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}+\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}\,]

\displaystyle=\phi{\,}{w_{p}^{t}}+[\,\Delta_{{\mathrm{adv}}}\Psi-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{t}\,]+\Delta_{[t,\alpha]}\Psi+[\,\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}\,]

\displaystyle\geq\;\phi{\,}w_{p}^{t}+\left[\,-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w_{p}^{t}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\varrho}^{t}}\,\right]+\left[\,-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}^{t}}\,\right]+\left[\,-{w_{p}^{t}}+{w_{\varrho}^{t}}\,\right]

(5.27)

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{3}}}}{\,}w_{p}^{t}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}w_{\varrho}^{t}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}^{t}}\;\geq\;0\,,

(5.28)

where in inequality (
5.27
) we use, in this order,
inequalities (
5.25
), (
5.23
), and (
5.24
),
and in line (
5.28
) the equality is obtained by
repeatedly applying the definition of
\phi
, while the last inequality follows from (
5.26
).

Therefore, to complete the analysis, it is now sufficient to show
that the two key inequalities (
5.23
) and (
5.24
) hold,
and that invariants (InvA) and (InvB) are preserved after the step.
As mentioned above, we divide the proof into two substeps, more precisely defined as follows:

Processing the initial segment
[t,\alpha]
:

In this substep, presented in Section
5.5.4
below,
we assume that the changes described in the adversary step
have already been implemented. We describe changes in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}

triggered by the removal of
{\ell}
from the optimal plan and by incrementing the time to
t+1
.
We show that inequality (
5.23
) holds and that
invariants (InvA) and (InvB) are preserved after these changes. More precisely, let
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}={{{P}}^{t}}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
be plan
{{\mathcal{P}}}

after removing
{\ell}
and incrementing the current time to
t+1
,
and let
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
be the snapshots
of sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
after implementing necessary changes resulting from modifying
{{\mathcal{P}}}
.
We prove that invariants (InvA) and (InvB) hold for plan
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}

and sets
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
. (As indicated by superscript
t+1
, these
sets are defined with respect to time
t+1
.)

Processing interval
(\beta,\gamma]
:

In this substep, we assume that the changes described in the adversary step and in the processing of
[t,\alpha]

have already been implemented.
We describe changes in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
triggered by the replacement of
p
by
\varrho
and,
for an iterated leap step, by modifications of packets
h_{i}
.
We prove that inequality (
5.24
) holds and that invariants (InvA) and (InvB)
are preserved after these changes.
In other words, we show that both invariants hold after the transmission event, i.e., for plan
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}
and sets

{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
and
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
.

The proof will be divided into two cases, depending on whether it is a simple
or an iterated leap step (see Sections
5.5.5

and
5.5.6
, respectively).
The proof for an iterated leap step is further divided into a number of smaller stages.
Each such stage consists of modifications of
{{\mathcal{P}}}
,
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and

{{\mathcal{A}}}
that involve packets with deadlines in some time interval
(\zeta,\eta]\subseteq(\beta,\gamma]
,
where
\zeta
and
\eta
are tight slots of
{{\mathcal{P}}}
.

Throughout the process, while we gradually make changes in sets
{{\mathcal{P}}}
,
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and

{{\mathcal{A}}}
, we will ensure that the following two properties hold:

Locality of changes:
For a substep or stage in which an interval
(\zeta,\eta]
of slots is processed,
let
\dot{{P}}
be the snapshot of
{{\mathcal{P}}}
just before processing interval
(\zeta,\eta]

and let
\ddot{{P}}
be the optimal plan after this subset or stage;
\zeta
and
\eta
will always be tight slots of
\dot{{P}}
.
We will maintain the property that the changes of
{{\mathcal{P}}}
are confined to the set
{{\mathcal{P}}}(\zeta,\eta]
of packets,
or more precisely,

\dot{{P}}\setminus\dot{{P}}(\zeta,\eta]=\ddot{{P}}\setminus\ddot{{P}}(\zeta,\eta]\,.

(5.29)

We also do not change the deadlines and weights of packets in
\dot{{P}}\setminus\dot{{P}}(\zeta,\eta]
.
Equation
5.29
implies that
|\dot{{P}}(\zeta,\eta]|=|\ddot{{P}}(\zeta,\eta]|
, since all plans are full.
Therefore,
\eta
and
\zeta
are tight slots of
\ddot{{P}}
and all values of
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{P}}},\tau)
remain unaffected for
\tau\notin(\zeta,\eta)
.
We remark that substeps and stages in the analysis of each case will have pairwise disjoint intervals
(\zeta,\eta]
.

Monotonicity of
{{\mathcal{B}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
:

No packets will be added to
{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha,{T}]\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
throughout the analysis.
(In fact, the only packet that may be added to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
is
{\ell}
. This could happen
when segment
[t,\alpha]
is processed.)

We remark that some “intermediate snapshots” of
{{\mathcal{P}}}
in the analysis of a leap step, such as
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, mentioned above,
are only auxiliary concepts used to capture the changes of
{{\mathcal{P}}}
after each substep or stage of the analysis.
These snapshots satisfy the definition of plans and are full, but they may not be optimal for the current set of pending packets.
As we do not rely on these intermediate plans being optimal, this presents no issue for the analysis.

5.5.4
Leap Step: Processing the Initial Segment

We now focus on the first substep of the analysis of the leap step, namely on
processing segment
[t,\alpha]
of the optimal plan
{{\mathcal{P}}}
.
As explained earlier in Section
5.5.3
,
we assume that the changes in sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
described in the adversary step
(Section
5.5.1
) have already been implemented.
Recall that
{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
and
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
represent the snapshots of sets

{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, respectively, resulting from the adversary step.
As the adversary step does not affect the optimal plan, the current plan is still
{{{P}}^{t}}
.

In this substep, we advance the time to
t+1
and remove
{\ell}
from
{{\mathcal{P}}}
;
that is, plan
{{{P}}^{t}}
is changed to
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}={{{P}}^{t}}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
.

{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
is a plan for the set of packets pending at time
t+1
, that consists of
the packets that were pending at time
t
, but excluding
{\ell}
and packets that expired at time
t
.
Since
d_{\ell}\leq\alpha=\textsf{nextts}({{{P}}^{t}},t)
,
the values of
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{P}}},\tau)
may only change for
\tau<\alpha
,
i.e.,
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)
for
\tau\geq\alpha
.
We may also need to make changes in sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, in order to preserve the invariants.
We refer to this substep as “processing the initial segment”, although some modifications of
{{\mathcal{B}}}

may involve pending packets with deadlines after
\alpha
. We divide this substep into two parts:

(i) first we show that if
{\ell}\in{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}

then we can remove it from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, preserving the invariants and not decreasing the potential,
and then

(ii) assuming that
{\ell}\notin{{\mathcal{A}}}
, we analyze the effect of
removing
{\ell}
from
{{\mathcal{P}}}
and incrementing the time.

Let
{{{A}}^{t}_{(i)}}
denote the intermediate adversary stash, after
the change in (i) and before the change in (ii), where we let

{{{A}}^{t}_{(i)}}={{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
if
{\ell}\notin{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
,
that is when change (i) does not apply. We adopt the same notation for set
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.
Note that
{{\mathcal{P}}}
does not change in (i), i.e.,
{{{P}}^{t}_{(i)}}={{{P}}^{t}}
.

(i) Dealing with the case
{\ell}\in{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
.

We now consider the case when
{\ell}\in{{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}
. Since
{\ell}\notin{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,
we need to remove
{\ell}
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
to preserve invariant (InvA).
In order to satisfy invariant (InvB), we are then forced to add
{\ell}
to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
,
which in turn requires us to remove some packet from
{{\mathcal{B}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
to restore feasibility of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.
To identify this packet we use Lemma
5.5
(with
g={\ell}
),
which shows that there is a packet
f_{\ell}\in{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\setminus{{{P}}^{t}}
for which the
set
{{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\setminus{\left\{f_{\ell}\right\}}\cup{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}

is feasible and satisfies
w({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\setminus{\left\{f_{\ell}\right\}}\cup{\left\{{\ell}\right\}})\geq w({{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}})
.

We thus set
{{{A}}^{t}_{(i)}}={{{A}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}

and
{{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}={{{B}}^{t}_{{\mathrm{adv}}}}\setminus{\left\{f_{\ell}\right\}}\cup{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
,
and this preserves both invariants (InvA) and (InvB).
Note that the adversary does not get any adjustment credit for the removal of
{\ell}
,
as
w_{\ell}={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d_{\ell})
, by the definition of
{\ell}
.
Further, since the contribution of this change of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to
\Delta_{[t,\alpha]}\Psi
is positive, when estimating
\Delta_{[t,\alpha]}\Psi

we will account for this contribution without an explicit reference.

(ii) Removing
{\ell}
from
{{\mathcal{P}}}
and incrementing the time.

By (i) we have
{\ell}\notin{{{A}}^{t}_{(i)}}
, and thus also
{\ell}\in{{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}
, by invariant (InvB)(ii).
Now, the plan is changed from
{{{P}}^{t}}
to
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}={{{P}}^{t}}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
.
As we do not further change
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, i.e.,
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}={{{A}}^{t}_{(i)}}
, invariant (InvA) holds.
(Note that after the adversary step, analyzed in Section
5.5.1
,

{{{A}}^{t}_{(i)}}
cannot contain a packet that expires at time
t
.) However, we need to preserve invariant (InvB).
Denoting by
f_{1}
the earliest-deadline packet in
{{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}\setminus{{{P}}^{t}}
, we consider two cases.

Case L.InSeg.1
:

d_{f_{1}}\geq d_{\ell}
.
We let
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}={{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
. Inequality (
5.23
) holds as

\Delta_{[t,\alpha]}\Psi\geq-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}^{t}}
.
(This inequality could be strict, due to the contribution from replacing
f_{\ell}
by
{\ell}
, as discussed in part (i) above.)
Invariant (InvB)(ii) holds because
{\ell}\notin{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
does not change in (ii).
To show that (InvB)(i), i.e., the feasibility of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, is preserved,
note that
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 0
for
\tau\geq d_{\ell}
.
Furthermore,
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)\geq 1

for
\tau\in[t,d_{\ell})
, where the first inequality follows from the definition of
f_{1}
,

d_{f_{1}}\geq d_{\ell}>\tau
, and Lemma
5.4
(a) with
\zeta=t-1
,
and the second inequality follows from the fact that
\tau
is before the first tight slot
\alpha
of
{{{P}}^{t}}
.
Hence, for
\tau\in[t+1,d_{\ell})
we have
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)-1\geq 0
,
concluding the proof that invariant (InvB) is maintained.

Case L.InSeg.2
:

d_{f_{1}}<d_{\ell}
. In this case, we
let
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}={{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}}\setminus{\left\{f_{1}\right\}}
.
Then
\Delta_{[t,\alpha]}\Psi\geq-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{f_{1}}^{t}}\geq-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w_{\ell}^{t}}
,
where the second inequality follows from
{\ell}\in{{{P}}^{t}}[t,\alpha]
and
f_{1}\notin{{{P}}^{t}}
.
Thus inequality (
5.23
) holds. Regarding invariant (InvB),
its part (ii) follows from the changes of sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.
To show invariant (InvB)(i),
note that
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 0
for
\tau\geq d_{f_{1}}
.
For
\tau\in[t,d_{f_{1}})
, we first get
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t}},\tau)\geq 1

by Lemma
5.4
(a) with
\zeta=t-1
, using the fact that
\tau
is before the first tight slot
\alpha
of
{{{P}}^{t}}
,
as
\tau<d_{f_{1}}<d_{\ell}\leq\alpha
(here, we use the case condition in the second inequality).
Then, for
\tau\in[t+1,d_{f_{1}})
, we have
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t}_{(i)}},\tau)-1\geq 0
.
Thus, invariant (InvB) is preserved after processing the initial segment
[t,\alpha]
.

5.5.5
Processing
(\beta,\gamma]
in a Simple Leap Step

We now address the second substep of analyzing a leap step, namely processing the interval
(\beta,\gamma]
, focusing on
the case of a simple leap step. (The case of an iterated leap step will be handled separately,
in Section
5.5.6
.) Recall that
the algorithm replaces
p
by
\varrho
in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
, and that in a simple leap step we have
k=0
,
that is
(\beta,\gamma]
is a segment of
{{{P}}^{t}}
and
d_{p},d_{\varrho}\in(\beta,\gamma]
(see Section
4
).

We assume that the changes in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
and sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
described in Sections
5.5.1

and
5.5.4
have already been implemented.
(We note that these changes might have involved some packets considered in this section; for example, it is possible that

\varrho
is the same as packet
f_{j}
removed from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
when processing the adversary step in Section
5.5.1
.)
In particular, the current time is already advanced to
t+1
, and
we assume that both invariants (InvA) and (InvB) hold for sets
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,

{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, and
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, all three defined with respect to time
t+1
.
It now remains to describe how we process the interval
(\beta,\gamma]
. In this substep, the plan changes from

{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
to
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
and the weight of
\varrho
is increased.
The replacement of
p
by
\varrho
may trigger modifications in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, in order to restore the invariants.

{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
only differs from
{{{P}}^{t}}
in that in
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
the current time is
incremented to
t+1
, packet
{\ell}
is removed, and the interval
[t+1,\alpha]
of
{{{P}}^{t}}
may be split into multiple segments.
Slot
\alpha=\textsf{nextts}({{{P}}^{t}},t)
is also tight in
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, and there were no changes in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
after slot
\alpha
;
that is
{{{P}}^{t}}(\alpha,{T}]={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\alpha,{T}]
.
Therefore
d_{\varrho}
and
d_{p}
are still in the same segment
(\beta,\gamma]
of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,
so
\textsf{prevts}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},d_{\varrho})=\textsf{prevts}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},d_{p})=\beta
and

\textsf{nextts}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},d_{\varrho})=\textsf{nextts}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},d_{p})=\gamma
.

In order to build some intuition behind our modifications of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, before we give a formal argument,
let us consider a few illustrative scenarios, focusing on maintaining invariant (InvB).
The simplest case is when
p\notin{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and
\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,
as then we can simply let
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.

Suppose next that
p\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and
\varrho\notin{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
In this case we are forced to remove
p
from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, and to add
\varrho
to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.
If
d_{\varrho}\geq d_{p}
, then we don’t need to make any more changes,
that is we can let
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
, and

{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
will be feasible.
In fact, even when
d_{\varrho}<d_{p}
, using the properties of
{{\mathcal{B}}}

established in Section
5.2
,
we can take
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
as long as
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]=\emptyset
.

In the remaining situations, some additional
modifications of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
are needed. In essence, what will happen is this: If
p\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, we will
replace it in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
by the latest-deadline packet
{g^{\ast}}
in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}[t+1,\gamma]
, that will
be removed from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Similarly, if
\varrho\notin{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
then
\varrho
will replace in
{{\mathcal{B}}}

the earliest-deadline packet
{f^{\ast}}
in
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,T]\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
Using the results from Section
5.2
, we show that
such packets exist and that these changes will indeed preserve the feasibility of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.

We now give a formal argument. The proof is organized into
two subcases, depending on whether some changes in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
are needed or not.

Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.S.1
:

\varrho\notin{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]=\emptyset
.
(In particular, the case condition implies that
p\not\in{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, as
p\in{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]
,
and that
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]\subseteq{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
; it is possible though that this
inclusion is strict.) Then we do not further change set
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, i.e.,
{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}={{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
Since
p\not\in{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
and deadlines of all packets remain unchanged, invariant (InvA) holds.

Regarding
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, note that
\varrho\notin{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
by invariant (InvA), and

p\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, by invariant (InvB)(ii) and
p\notin{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
Thus, we let
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
,
which preserves invariant (InvB)(ii).
Next, we need to show the feasibility of
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
, i.e., invariant (InvB)(i).
If
d_{\varrho}\geq d_{p}
, then replacing
p
by
\varrho
in
{{\mathcal{B}}}

preserves feasibility and thus, as
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
is feasible,
the new backup plan
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
is feasible as well.
Otherwise,
d_{\varrho}<d_{p}
. For
\tau\notin[d_{\varrho},d_{p})
, it holds that

{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\geq 0
,
by the feasibility of
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
. Next, consider some
\tau\in[d_{\varrho},d_{p})\subseteq(\beta,\gamma)
.
By the case condition,
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]=\emptyset
,
thus using Lemma
5.4
(b) with
\zeta=\gamma
we get that

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\geq 1
,
where the second inequality holds because
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma)
is not a tight slot in
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
Hence,
{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)-1\geq 0

for
\tau\in[d_{\varrho},d_{p})
, and we can conclude that invariant (InvB) holds after the step.

Finally, we prove inequality (
5.24
). According to the algorithm, we have
w^{t+1}_{\varrho}={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{\varrho})
.
Then the terms on the left-hand side of (
5.24
) are

\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi={\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}(-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t+1}_{\varrho})\;,\quad\Delta^{t}{w}=w^{t+1}_{\varrho}-w^{t}_{\varrho}\;,\quad\textrm{and}\quad\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}=0\;.

Plugging these in, we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}\;

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}(-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t+1}_{\varrho})-\phi{\,}(w^{t+1}_{\varrho}-w^{t}_{\varrho})-0

\displaystyle=\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{p}-w^{t+1}_{\varrho}+\phi{\,}w^{t}_{\varrho}

\displaystyle\geq\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{p}-({\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}w^{t}_{p}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{\varrho})+\phi{\,}w^{t}_{\varrho}

(5.30)

\displaystyle=\;-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t}_{\varrho}\,,

where inequality (
5.30
) follows from

w^{t+1}_{\varrho}={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{\varrho})\leq{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}w^{t}_{p}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}w^{t}_{\varrho}
,
by (
5.26
). This shows that (
5.24
) holds.

Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.S.2
:

\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
or
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]\neq\emptyset
.
(Thus, there is a packet in
({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}})\cup{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}

which has deadline in
(\beta,\gamma]
.)

Changing sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.

Let
{g^{\ast}}
be the latest-deadline packet in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}[t+1,\gamma]
.
Packet
{g^{\ast}}
is well-defined: this is trivially true if the second condition of the case is
satisfied, and otherwise we have
\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,
in which case Observation
5.2
(b) with
\eta=\gamma
implies that

{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}[t+1,\gamma]\neq\emptyset
.
(We remark that
d_{{g^{\ast}}}\leq\beta
if
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]=\emptyset
.)
Similarly, let
{f^{\ast}}
be the earliest-deadline packet in
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,T]\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
We show that packet
{f^{\ast}}
is well-defined. This is trivially true if
\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,
while otherwise it holds that
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]\neq\emptyset
, and then Observation
5.2
(a)
with
\eta=\beta
gives us that
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,T]\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\neq\emptyset
.
(It is possible that
d_{f^{\ast}}>\gamma
if
\varrho\notin{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.)

We now define packets
g
and
f
, and we modify
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
as follows.
If
p\in{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, let
g=p
; otherwise let
g={g^{\ast}}
.
If
\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, let
f=\varrho
; otherwise let
f={f^{\ast}}
.
We remove
g
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, i.e., we set
{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}={{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{\left\{g\right\}}
.
To preserve invariant (InvB)(ii), we must add
\varrho
to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
(if it’s not there already) because
\varrho\in{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}\setminus{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
. Hence, we let

{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\cup{\left\{g\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f,p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
;
this definition applies no matter whether
g=p
or
g={g^{\ast}}
and whether
f=\varrho
or
f={f^{\ast}}
.
(In particular, if
g=p
and
f=\varrho
then
{{\mathcal{B}}}
will not change.)

Deriving inequality (
5.24
).

We first claim that
w_{f}^{t}\leq w_{\varrho}^{t}
. As
f\in{\left\{{f^{\ast}},\varrho\right\}}
, it is enough to prove that
w_{f^{\ast}}^{t}\leq w_{\varrho}^{t}
.
We have
d_{f^{\ast}}>\beta\geq\alpha
and, by the monotonicity property of
{{\mathcal{B}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}

(see Section
5.5.3
), no packets were added to
{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha,{T}]\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}

when processing segment
[t,\alpha]
, implying that
{f^{\ast}}\in{{{B}}^{t}}(\beta,T]\setminus{{{P}}^{t}}
.
Then the inequality
w_{f^{\ast}}^{t}\leq w_{\varrho}^{t}
follows from the choice of

\varrho=\textsf{sub}^{t}(p)
as the heaviest pending packet not in
{{{P}}^{t}}
with deadline after slot
\beta
.

We also claim that
w_{g}^{t}\leq w_{p}^{t}
. As
g\in{\left\{{g^{\ast}},p\right\}}
, it is enough to prove that
w_{g^{\ast}}^{t}\leq w_{p}^{t}
.
Indeed, as we do not add any packet to
{{\mathcal{A}}}
when processing segment
[t,\alpha]
,
it holds that
{g^{\ast}}\in{{{A}}^{t}}
and thus,
{g^{\ast}}\in{{{P}}^{t}}
by invariant (InvA).
The choice of
{g^{\ast}}
implies that
\textsf{prevts}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{{g^{\ast}}})\leq\textsf{prevts}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{p})
, which
in turn implies that
w(\textsf{sub}({{{P}}^{t}},{g^{\ast}}))\geq w_{\varrho}^{t}
. Therefore, if we had
w_{g^{\ast}}^{t}>w_{p}^{t}
then
the algorithm would transmit
{g^{\ast}}
instead of
p
.
(Strictly speaking, here were rely on the “locality of changes” invariant from Section
5.5.3
, namely that
{{\mathcal{P}}}(\alpha,T]
does not change when processing segment
[t,\alpha]
.)

According to the algorithm,
w^{t+1}_{\varrho}={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{\varrho})
.
Then the terms on the left-hand side of (
5.24
) are

\displaystyle\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi\;

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}(w_{g}^{t}-w^{t}_{p}-w_{f}^{t}+w^{t+1}_{\varrho})\,,

\displaystyle\Delta^{t}{w}\;

\displaystyle=\;w^{t+1}_{\varrho}-w^{t}_{\varrho}\,,\;\textrm{and}

\displaystyle\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}\;

\displaystyle=\;w^{t}_{g}-{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{g})

\displaystyle\leq\;w^{t}_{g}-{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{\varrho})\;=\;w^{t}_{g}-w^{t+1}_{\varrho},

(5.31)

where the inequality in line (
5.31
) follows
from
\textsf{nextts}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{g})\leq\gamma=\textsf{nextts}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{\varrho})
.
(For simplicity, the estimate for
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}
is given above
with respect to
{{{P}}^{t}}
, as described in Section
5.1
.
To be completely formal, in this substep this credit would be with respect to the intermediate plan
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
However, using
{{{P}}^{t}}
instead of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
cannot make the adversary credit smaller
since
{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},d^{t}_{g})\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{g})
, which holds as
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}={{{P}}^{t}}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}

and as any tight slot of
{{{P}}^{t}}
is also tight in
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.) Thus the changes described above give us that

\displaystyle\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi

\displaystyle-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}

\displaystyle\geq\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}(w_{g}^{t}-w^{t}_{p}-w_{f}^{t}+w^{t+1}_{\varrho})-\phi{\,}(w^{t+1}_{\varrho}-w^{t}_{\varrho})-(w^{t}_{g}-w^{t+1}_{\varrho})

\displaystyle=\;-(\,{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w^{t}_{g}}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w^{t}_{p}}\,)+(\,-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w^{t}_{f}}+\phi{\,}{w^{t}_{\varrho}}\,)

\displaystyle\geq\;-{w^{t}_{p}}+{w^{t}_{\varrho}}\,,

(5.32)

using bounds
w_{f}^{t}\leq w_{\varrho}^{t}
and
w_{g}^{t}\leq w_{p}^{t}
in step (
5.32
).
This shows (
5.24
).

Preserving the invariants.

Since after the changes it holds that
p\notin{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
, invariant (InvA) is maintained.
We now show that invariant (InvB) holds. That condition (InvB)(ii) is true follows
directly from the way we update
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
. Therefore, we will focus on
condition (InvB)(i), that is the feasibility of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.
If we have both
f=\varrho
and
g=p
then
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, and
the feasibility of
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
is trivial. Hence, from now on we assume that

f={f^{\ast}}\neq\varrho
or
g={g^{\ast}}\neq p
(or both).

We split the changes in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
into two parts (see Figure
5.3
):

(i)

First, we show that invariant (InvB) holds for the intermediate
backup plan
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\cup{\left\{g\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f\right\}}
,
which we consider with respect to adversary stash
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}={{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{\left\{g\right\}}

and plan
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
In other words, we first implement the removal of
g
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}

together with the corresponding changes in
{{\mathcal{B}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
, but postpone the change in the plan.

(ii)

Second, we replace
p
by
\varrho
in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
, so
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}

and
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
,
and we show that
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
is feasible as well.

Figure 5.3:
Changing
{{\mathcal{B}}}
in two parts.

Claim 5.9
.

Assume that
f={f^{\ast}}\neq\varrho
or
g={g^{\ast}}\neq p
.
Set
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\cup{\left\{g\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f\right\}}
is a feasible
set of packets with respect to time
t+1
.

Proof.

The claim clearly holds if
d_{f}\leq d_{g}
, so for the rest of the proof we assume that
d_{g}<d_{f}
.
Recall that, according to the assumptions of our Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.S.2, we have that either

\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
or
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]\neq\emptyset
holds.

It is sufficient to show that
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\geq 1
for any
\tau\in[d_{g},d_{f})
,
as this will imply that
d_{f}\leq\textsf{nextts}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},d_{g})
, which in turn implies
the feasibility of
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
. We consider two cases.

First, assume that
\varrho\notin{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
. This implies
f={f^{\ast}}
and
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]\neq\emptyset
,
which means that
d_{g}\in(\beta,\gamma]
, no matter whether
g=p
or
g={g^{\ast}}
.
Then, for
\tau\in[d_{g},d_{f})
, Lemma
5.4
(a) with
\zeta=\beta
gives us

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\;\geq\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)+|{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\tau]|\;\geq\;1,

since
g\in{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\tau]
and
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\geq 0
.

In the second case we have
\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, so
f=\varrho
.
We then have
g={g^{\ast}}
(as we assume that
f\neq\varrho
or
g\neq p
).
For
\tau\in[d_{g},d_{f})
, we use Lemma
5.4
(b) with
\zeta=\gamma
to obtain

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\;\geq\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)+|{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\tau,\gamma]\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}|\;\geq\;1,

where we use
f=\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\tau,\gamma]\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
in the second inequality.
∎

From the definitions of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
,
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
, and
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
, and using
Claim
5.9
, invariants (InvA) and (InvB) hold for these three
intermediate sets.

Before replacing
p
by
\varrho
in the optimal plan and in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, we prove the following claim:

Claim 5.10
.

Assume that
f={f^{\ast}}\neq\varrho
or
g={g^{\ast}}\neq p
.
If
d_{\varrho}<d_{p}
, then
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 1
for any
\tau\in[d_{\varrho},d_{p})
.

Proof.

Let
{\hat{f}}
be the earliest-deadline packet in
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}(\beta,T]\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
and
let
{\hat{g}}
be the latest-deadline packet in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}[t+1,\gamma]
.
These definitions are similar to the definitions of
{f^{\ast}}
and
{g^{\ast}}
, but they are not equivalent
because in part (i) we could have removed
{f^{\ast}}
from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{g^{\ast}}
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
For this reason,
packets
{\hat{f}}
and
{\hat{g}}
may not actually exist, in which case, abusing notation, we will introduce artificial notations
for their deadlines:
if
{\hat{f}}
does not exist, we let
d_{\hat{f}}=T
and, similarly, if
{\hat{g}}
does not exist, we let
d_{\hat{g}}=t+1
.
We now observe that

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 1\quad\textrm{for}\;\tau\in(\beta,\min(d_{\hat{f}},\gamma))\cup[\max(d_{\hat{g}},\beta+1),\gamma).

(5.33)

Indeed, the first inequality follows from Lemma
5.4
, which we can apply here
because both invariants (InvA) and (InvB) are true for
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
,
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
,
and
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
. Specifically, apply Lemma
5.4
(a) with
\zeta=\beta
,
obtaining the first inequality for
\tau\in(\beta,d_{\hat{f}})
, and
Lemma
5.4
(b) with
\zeta=\gamma
, to obtain the first inequality
for
\tau\in(d_{\hat{g}},\gamma)
. To justify the second inequality in (
5.33
),
note that, as
(\beta,\gamma]
is a segment of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, we have

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 1
for
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma)
.

Since
[d_{\varrho},d_{p})\subseteq(\beta,\gamma)
,
inequality (
5.33
) directly implies Claim
5.10

when
d_{\hat{f}}\geq\gamma
or
d_{\hat{g}}\leq\beta
.
Therefore, for the rest of the argument, we will assume that
d_{\hat{f}}<\gamma
and
d_{\hat{g}}>\beta
.

Next, observe that, since
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
is obtained from
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
by replacing

f
by
g
, we have

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)+1\geq 1\quad\textrm{for}\;\tau\in[d_{f},d_{g}),

(5.34)

where the inequality follows from the feasibility of
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
. (It is possible that
d_{g}\leq d_{f}
, in which case this interval is empty.)

From these two observations, (
5.33
) and (
5.34
),
it is now sufficient to show that

[d_{\varrho},d_{p})\;\subseteq\;(\beta,d_{\hat{f}})\cup[d_{f},d_{g})\cup[d_{\hat{g}},\gamma).

(5.35)

Note that
d_{\hat{g}}\leq d_{g^{\ast}}

and
d_{\hat{f}}\geq d_{f^{\ast}}
, by the definitions of these packets and of
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
and
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}

(note that
g
is not a candidate for
{\hat{f}}
as
g\in{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
).
Now we consider three cases.

If
f=\varrho
, then it holds that
g={g^{\ast}}
by the claim’s assumption, and interval
[d_{\varrho},d_{p})
is covered by

[d_{f},d_{g})=[d_{\varrho},d_{g^{\ast}})
and
[d_{\hat{g}},\gamma)
, because
d_{\hat{g}}\leq d_{g^{\ast}}
and
d_{p}\leq\gamma
.

Similarly, if
g=p
, then we have
f={f^{\ast}}
, and interval
[d_{\varrho},d_{p})
is covered by

[d_{f},d_{g})=[d_{f^{\ast}},d_{p})
and
(\beta,d_{\hat{f}})
, because
d_{\hat{f}}\geq d_{f^{\ast}}
.

The last case is when
f={f^{\ast}}
and
g={g^{\ast}}
. Then interval
[d_{\varrho},d_{p})\subseteq(\beta,\gamma)
is covered by

(\beta,d_{f^{\ast}})\cup[d_{f^{\ast}},d_{g^{\ast}})\cup[d_{g^{\ast}},\gamma)
,
which implies (
5.35
)
because
d_{\hat{f}}\geq d_{f^{\ast}}
and
d_{\hat{g}}\leq d_{g^{\ast}}
.
∎

Finally, we show that the new backup plan
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}

is feasible. If
d_{\varrho}\geq d_{p}
, then the feasibility of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
is clearly preserved
after replacing
p
by
\varrho
. Otherwise,
d_{\varrho}<d_{p}
, and for
\tau\in[d_{\varrho},d_{p})

we have
{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)-1\geq 0
by Claim
5.10
,
while
{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 0
for
\tau\notin[d_{\varrho},d_{p})

by Claim
5.9
. Hence, invariant (InvB) holds after the step.

5.5.6
Processing
(\beta,\gamma]
in an Iterated Leap Step

Here we address the last (and by far most involved) part of our argument, namely
the analysis of an iterated leap step. As a reminder, this step occurs when
k\geq 1
in the algorithm, or,
equivalently, when
d_{\varrho}
is in a later segment of
{{{P}}^{t}}
than
d_{p}
.
The initial comments in Section
5.5.5

apply here as well. To recap: We assume that
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
have already been modified as
described in Sections
5.5.1
(the adversary step)
and
5.5.4
(processing segment
[t,\alpha]
).
In particular, the time step has already been incremented to
t+1
and the
current snapshots of
{{{\mathcal{P}}}}
,
{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
and
{{{\mathcal{A}}}}

are denoted
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, respectively.
Recall that
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}={{{P}}^{t}}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
.
We assume that both invariants (InvA) and (InvB) hold for these sets (with respect to time step
t+1
).

As before,
\beta=\textsf{prevts}^{t}(d_{p})
and
\gamma=\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d_{\varrho})
.
As this is an iterated leap step, we have
d_{\varrho}>\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d_{p})
, so the
interval
(\beta,\gamma]
of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
is a union of two or more consecutive segments of
