{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
(The content of this interval is identical to the same interval in
{{{P}}^{t}}
; in fact,
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and
{{{P}}^{t}}
are
identical starting right after
\alpha=\textsf{nextts}^{t}(t)
, the first tight slot of
{{{P}}^{t}}
.)
Let
h_{0}=p,h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
be the packets from Algorithm
PlanM
(line
9
)
and let
h_{k+1}=\varrho
. All packets
h_{0},h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
are in different segments of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, not necessarily
consecutive, while
h_{k+1}=\varrho
has deadline in the same segment as
h_{k}
, but
\varrho\notin{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
The deadlines and weights of packets
\varrho
and
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
are still unchanged before processing
(\beta,\gamma]

(that is, they are the same as in step
t
).

We need to estimate the potential change due to weight increases and the changes
triggered by the replacement of
p
by
\varrho
and by
the “shifting” of
h_{i}
’s. We also need to prove key inequality (
5.24
)
and that invariants (InvA) and (InvB) hold after the step.

The overall structure of the argument in this case is similar to
Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.S (simple leap step), with the analysis split according to
whether changes in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
are needed (Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.I.2) or not (Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.I.1).
In Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.I.2, the analysis will be divided into several stages corresponding to
processing of different groups of segments.

For
i=0,\dots,k
, let
\mu_{i}={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{h_{i}})
; note that

w^{t}_{\ell}\geq\mu_{0}\geq\mu_{1}\geq\dots\geq\mu_{k}={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d_{\varrho})
and
the algorithm ensures that
w^{t+1}_{h_{i}}\geq\mu_{i-1}
for
i=1,\dots,k+1
.
In both cases below, we use the following simple bound on the change of weights of
h_{i}
’s.

Lemma 5.11
.

For any
1\leq a^{\prime}\leq b^{\prime}\leq k
, let
\Delta^{t}w({h_{a^{\prime}}},\dots,{h_{b^{\prime}}})

be the total amount by which the algorithm increases the weights of packets
h_{a^{\prime}},\dots,h_{b^{\prime}}
in step
t
.
Suppose that there exists
i\in[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]
such that
w^{t}_{h_{i}}<\mu_{i-1}
, i.e.,
the algorithm increases the weight of
h_{i}
in Line 11 (and thus
\Delta^{t}w({h_{a^{\prime}}},\dots,{h_{b^{\prime}}})>0
).
Then
\Delta^{t}w({h_{a^{\prime}}},\dots,{h_{b^{\prime}}})\leq\mu_{a^{\prime}-1}-w^{t}_{h_{b^{\prime}}}
.

Proof.

Let
c\in[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]
be the maximum index such that
w^{t}_{h_{c}}<\mu_{c-1}
;
such
c
exists by the assumption of the lemma.
We show the claim as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta^{t}w({h_{a^{\prime}}},\dots,{h_{b^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=a^{\prime}}^{c}\max(\mu_{i-1}-w^{t}_{h_{i}},0)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=a^{\prime}}^{c-1}\max(\mu_{i-1}-\mu_{i},0)+\max(\mu_{c-1}-w^{t}_{h_{c}},0)

(5.36)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=a^{\prime}}^{c-1}(\mu_{i-1}-\mu_{i})+\mu_{c-1}-w^{t}_{h_{c}}

(5.37)

\displaystyle=\mu_{a^{\prime}-1}-w^{t}_{h_{c}}

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{a^{\prime}-1}-w^{t}_{h_{b^{\prime}}}\,,

(5.38)

where inequality (
5.36
) follows from
w^{t}_{h_{i}}\geq\mu_{i}
,
equality (
5.37
) from
\mu_{i-1}\geq\mu_{i}
and
from
\mu_{c-1}>w^{t}_{h_{c}}
(by the choice of
c
),
and inequality (
5.38
) from
w^{t}_{h_{c}}\geq w^{t}_{h_{b^{\prime}}}
by Lemma
B.1
(b) and
c\leq b^{\prime}
.
∎

Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.I.1
:

\varrho\notin{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]=\emptyset
.
In this case, we do not make any changes in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, i.e.
{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}={{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
From the case condition,
p=h_{0},h_{1},...,h_{k}\notin{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, because each
h_{i}
is in
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]
.
It follows that
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is indeed not affected by the decrease of the deadlines of
h_{i}
’s.
Thus invariant (InvA) holds. Note that
\varrho\notin{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
by invariant (InvA).
From the case condition and
p\in{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]
, we have
p\notin{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
;
therefore
p\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
by invariant (InvB)(ii). This means that in order to preserve invariant (InvB)(ii) we
need to remove
p
from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and add
\varrho
to it.
Thus we set
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
.

Deriving inequality (
5.24
).

To prove this inequality, we first show that

\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{k}})\;\leq\;\mu_{0}-\mu_{k}.

(5.39)

(The weight increase
\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{k}})
was defined in Lemma
5.11
.)
Indeed, if there is no
i\in[1,k]
such that
w^{t}_{h_{i}}<\mu_{i-1}
,
then
\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{k}})=0\leq\mu_{0}-\mu_{k}
as
\mu_{0}\geq\mu_{k}
.
Otherwise, we use Lemma
5.11
with
a^{\prime}=1
and
b^{\prime}=k
to get

\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{k}})\leq\mu_{0}-w^{t}_{h_{k}}\leq\mu_{0}-\mu_{k}
, where the last inequality follows from
w^{t}_{h_{k}}\geq\mu_{k}
.

In this case, the terms on the left-hand side of inequality (
5.24
) are

\displaystyle\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi\;

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\big(-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t+1}_{\varrho}+\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{k}})\big)

\displaystyle\Delta^{t}{w}\;

\displaystyle=\;w^{t+1}_{\varrho}-w^{t}_{\varrho}+\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{k}})

\displaystyle\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}\;

\displaystyle=\;0

The equation for
\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi
is true because
all packets
h_{1},...,h_{k}
are both in
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and in
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}
, by Lemma
B.1
(a),
and none of them is in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}={\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
, so we keep all of them in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
to preserve invariant (InvB)(ii).
The last equation holds because we have not changed the adversary stash.
Plugging in these formulas, taking into account that
w_{\varrho}^{t+1}=\mu_{k}
, and using inequality (
5.39
), we can obtain inequality (
5.24
) with an easy calculation:

\displaystyle\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi

\displaystyle-\phi{\,}(\Delta^{t}{w})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}(-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t+1}_{\varrho}+\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{k}}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\phi{\,}(w^{t+1}_{\varrho}-w^{t}_{\varrho}+\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{k}}))-0

\displaystyle=\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{p}-\mu_{k}+\phi{\,}w^{t}_{\varrho}-\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{k}})

\displaystyle\geq\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{p}-\mu_{k}+\phi{\,}w^{t}_{\varrho}-(\mu_{0}-\mu_{k})

\displaystyle=\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{p}+\phi w^{t}_{\varrho}-\mu_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{p}+\phi{\,}w^{t}_{\varrho}-({\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}w_{p}^{t}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w_{\varrho}^{t})

(5.40)

\displaystyle=\;-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t}_{\varrho}\,,

where inequality (
5.40
) follows from

{\mu_{0}}={\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d_{p})\leq{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}w_{p}^{t}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w_{\varrho}^{t}
,
which is inequality (
5.26
) with
\tau=d_{p}
.

Preserving invariant (InvB).

That invariant (InvB)(ii) is preserved follows immediately from the definitions of
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}
,

{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
, and
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
.
Next, we show invariant (InvB)(i), that is the feasibility of
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
,
and we split the argument into two parts. First, we
show that the invariant holds for the intermediate backup plan resulting from replacing
p
by
\varrho
,
and then later we show that modifying the deadlines of the
h_{i}
’s preserves the invariant as well.

So consider an intermediate plan
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}

and backup plan
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
,
both with respect to time
t+1
but with the deadlines of packets
h_{1},h_{2},...,h_{k}
still unchanged.
Since this is an iterated leap step, we have that
d_{\varrho}>d_{p}
, so
replacing
p
by
\varrho
in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
preserves feasibility. Therefore
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
is feasible.

Now, we consider
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
, that is the backup plan at step
t+1
, which
is obtained from
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
by decreasing the deadlines of
h_{i}
’s, as in the algorithm.
As slots outside
(\beta,\gamma)
are not affected, we have

{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 0
for
\tau\notin(\beta,\gamma)
.
So it remains to consider slots
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma)
.

Recall that packets
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
belong to both
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
.
We also have that
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 1
for any
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma)
,
which holds as replacing
p
by
\varrho
merges all segments between
\beta=\textsf{prevts}^{t}(d_{p})

and
\gamma=\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d_{\varrho})
. Since
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}(\beta,\gamma]={{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]=\emptyset
,
Lemma
5.4
(b) with
\zeta=\gamma
gives us that for any
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma)
,

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 1\,.

(5.41)

Next, we claim that for
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma)
,

{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)-1\,.

(5.42)

To justify this inequality, we observe that
d^{t+1}_{h_{i}}=\tau_{i-1}\geq d^{t}_{h_{i-1}}
for
i=1,\dots,k
,
which gives us that intervals
[d^{t+1}_{h_{i}},d^{t}_{h_{i}})\subset(\beta,\gamma)
are disjoint. This implies in turn
that, for
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma)
, decreasing the deadlines of all
h_{i}
’s can decrease
{\textsf{pslack}}({\mathcal{B}},\tau)
at most by one,
which yields inequality (
5.42
).

Inequalities (
5.41
) and (
5.42
) yield

{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)-1\geq 0
for
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma)
,
completing the proof that
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
satisfies invariant (InvB)(i).

Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.I.2
:

\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
or
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]\neq\emptyset
.
In this case, both sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
will be changed. We focus on the segments of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
which
contain the packets
h_{0}=p,h_{1},...,h_{k}
. These are the segments of the current optimal plan that are modified by the algorithm
when
p
is transmitted.
Specifically, for
i=0,\dots,k
, let
S_{i}
be the segment of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
that ends at
\tau_{i}=\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d^{t}_{h_{i}})
,
that is the segment containing
d^{t}_{h_{i}}
. (See Figure
4.1
.)
Recall that
\textsf{prevts}^{t}(d_{p})=\beta
,
\tau_{k}=\gamma
, and that we defined
h_{k+1}=\varrho
.
We first organize segments
S_{i}
into groups and then split the sequence of changes in this case into stages, having one stage for each group.

Groups.

We start by defining a packet
g\in{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
Let
{g^{\ast}}
be the latest-deadline packet in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}[t+1,\gamma]
.
Observe that packet
{g^{\ast}}
is well-defined. This is trivially true
if the second part of the case condition holds, while in the case when
\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,
we use Observation
5.2
(b) with
\eta=\gamma
to get that

{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}[t+1,\gamma]\neq\emptyset
. (It is possible that
d_{{g^{\ast}}}\leq\beta
in the second case.)

We now define
g
as follows: If
d^{t}_{{g^{\ast}}}
is in a segment
S_{i}
for which
h_{i}\in{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, then let
g=h_{i}
;
otherwise let
g={g^{\ast}}
. Observe that, in particular, if
h_{k}\in{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
then
g=h_{k}
.

We will process segments
S_{i}
in
groups
, where each group is specified by some non-empty
interval of indices
[a,b]\subseteq{\left\{0,\dots,k\right\}}
of segments
S_{i}
.
We will denote such a group by
{\langle a,b\rangle}
(to avoid confusion with the notation for segments and time
intervals).
These intervals will be chosen so that they partition the set of all indices
{\left\{0,\dots,k\right\}}
.
Roughly, we have a group for each
h_{i}\in{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
(that needs to be
replaced in or removed from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
because its deadline needs to be decreased), a special last group,
and possibly a special group at the beginning.
Let
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{l}
be the indices of those packets
h_{0}=p,h_{1},...,h_{k}
that are in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
Note that, if
l>0
, then
d^{t}_{g}\in[d^{t}(h_{i_{l}}),\gamma]
, because
h_{i_{l}}\in{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
is a
candidate for
{g^{\ast}}
. In particular, since
g\in{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, we have that

g\notin{\left\{h_{0},...,h_{k}\right\}}\setminus{\left\{h_{i_{l}}\right\}}
; that is, among

all
packets
h_{0},...,h_{k}
,
g
may be possibly equal only to
h_{i_{l}}
.
The definition of these groups depends on whether some packet
h_{i}
is in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
(that is
l>0
)
or none of them is in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
(that is
l=0
):

Figure 5.4:
Two examples that illustrate assigning segments into groups. In both examples we have
k=8
.
Segments are separated by vertical bars.
Segments
S_{i}
are colored (shaded). Green (or dark shaded) segments
S_{i}
are those for which packet
h_{i}
is in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
In the first example, we have
l=3
,
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})=(2,3,6)
,
g={g^{\ast}}
(as
{g^{\ast}}
is not in
S_{i}
for any
i
),
and
{{\tilde{a}}}=7
. We have 5 groups: the initial group
{\langle 0,1\rangle}
, middle groups

{\langle 2,2\rangle}
,
{\langle 3,5\rangle}
, and
{\langle 6,6\rangle}
,
and the terminal group
{\langle 7,8\rangle}
.
In the second example we have
l=4
,
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})=(0,2,3,6)
, and
g=h_{6}
(because
{g^{\ast}}
is in
S_{6}
and

h_{6}
is in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
). Since
i_{1}=0
there is no
initial group. We have 4 groups: middle groups
{\langle 0,1\rangle}
,
{\langle 2,2\rangle}
,
{\langle 3,5\rangle}
, and the terminal group
{\langle 6,8\rangle}
.

Case
l>0
:
If
l>1
, then for each
c=1,\dots,l-1
, the interval
{\langle i_{c},i_{c+1}-1\rangle}
is a
middle group
.
If
i_{1}>0
(meaning that
h_{0}=p\not\in{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
),
then there is a special
initial group

{\langle 0,i_{1}-1\rangle}
. If
i_{1}=0
then there is no initial group.
Next, we assign the indices in
[i_{l},k]
to one or two groups and define index
{{\tilde{a}}}
.
If
g=h_{i_{l}}
(in particular, if
i_{l}=k
), then
{\langle i_{l},k\rangle}
is the
terminal group
and we let
{{\tilde{a}}}=i_{l}
.
Otherwise,
g\neq h_{i_{l}}
and
we let
{{\tilde{a}}}
be the smallest index in
0,...,k
for which
\tau_{{\tilde{a}}}\geq d^{t}_{g}
.
The assumption that
g\neq h_{i_{l}}
implies that
{{\tilde{a}}}>i_{l}
.
Then
{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}
is the terminal group and
{\langle i_{l},{{\tilde{a}}}-1\rangle}
is a new middle group.
(See Figure
5.4
for two examples that illustrate the case when
l>0
.)

Case
l=0
:
We define one or two groups only.
There is the terminal group
{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}
, where
{{\tilde{a}}}
is again the smallest index
in
0,...,k
with
\tau_{{\tilde{a}}}\geq d^{t}_{g}
. If
{{\tilde{a}}}>0
, we also have the initial group
{\langle 0,{{\tilde{a}}}-1\rangle}
.

Observe that we always define
{{\tilde{a}}}
so that
{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}
is the terminal group.
We further define index
{{\tilde{b}}}
so that the first group is
{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}
;
for example, if
l>0
and
i_{1}>0
, then
{{\tilde{b}}}=i_{1}-1
.
Note that in almost every group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
packet
h_{a}
is in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
; the only
two possible exceptions are (i) the initial group, and (ii) the terminal group in case when
either
l=0
or
l\geq 1
and
{{\tilde{a}}}\neq i_{l}
.
On the other hand, for any group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b}
are never in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}

and thus these packets are in
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
by invariant (InvB)(ii).

Stages.

The changes implemented during the processing of interval
(\beta,\gamma]
involve the
replacement of
p
by
\varrho
in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
, weight increases of
\varrho
and
h_{i}
’s, and
deadline decreases of
h_{i}
’s, as described in the algorithm.
These changes will also trigger changes of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
necessary to preserve the invariants.
We will organize all these changes (of
{{\mathcal{P}}}
,
{{\mathcal{B}}}
,
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, and the instance itself)
into a sequence of
stages
, with one stage for each group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
.
The stages will be implemented in the reverse order of the groups,
that is, starting from the stage for the terminal group (which is defined in all cases),
continuing with middle groups in the reverse order of their indices,
and ending by the group containing index
0
, which may be of any type.

To understand better the partition into stages, as described below,
it helps to think of the substitution of
p
by
\varrho=h_{k+1}
in the optimal
plan as a chain of substitutions: first replace
h_{k}
by
\varrho
, then replace
h_{k-1}
by
h_{k}
, and so on, and
eventually replace
p=h_{0}
by
h_{1}
. In our process here, rather than processing these substitutions individually,
we process the substitutions within each group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
simultaneously as one stage, in which
h_{a}
is replaced by
h_{b+1}
.
(See Figure
5.5
for illustration.)

Figure 5.5:
An illustration of the changes in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
in the stage when a middle group
{\langle a,b\rangle}={\langle 4,9\rangle}

is processed.

Formally, let
{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
be the snapshot of the intermediate plan
{{\mathcal{P}}}
just before the stage
for group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
and let
{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
be the snapshot of the intermediate plan
{{\mathcal{P}}}
just after the stage
for group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
. (We remark that
{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
is not necessarily an optimal plan. It is only meant to be
an “intermediate plan” used to capture gradual changes in the optimal plan as stages are executed.
It is, however, feasible and it will satisfy
appropriate invariants.) Naturally, if
{\langle a,b\rangle}
and
{\langle b+1,c\rangle}
are two consecutive groups, then

{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle b+1,c\rangle}}}={\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
.
We adopt the same conventions for sets
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
. In this notation
(see Figure
5.6
for illustration):

•

In the first stage, that corresponds to the terminal group
{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}
, we have

{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,

{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and
{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
Note that in this stage, it holds that
h_{k+1}=\varrho\notin{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
.

•

When describing the stage for each group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
, we assume that the changes in stages
for groups
{\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rangle}
with
a^{\prime}>b
have already been implemented.
As an invariant we will ensure that
h_{b+1}\notin{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}

(for
b=k
, this holds by
h_{k+1}=\varrho\notin{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
).
In this stage, the plan changes from
{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
to

{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}={\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{h_{a}\right\}}\cup{\left\{h_{b+1}\right\}}
, which
will imply that
h_{a}\notin{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
just after this stage is processed.
Further, the weights and deadlines of packets
h_{a+1},...,h_{b+1}
are adjusted according to the algorithm
(for
b=k
, the deadline of
h_{k+1}=\varrho
remains the same).
This stage also involves changes in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
triggered by the changes in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
and by the
adjustments of weights and deadlines. These changes will convert

{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
and
{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
to
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
and
{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
,
respectively.

•

After the last stage, corresponding to the first group
{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}
(which could be an initial, middle, or terminal group),
we have
{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}={\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}
,
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}={\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
,
and
{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}={\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
.

Figure 5.6:
Notation for stages.

By the results of Section
5.5.4
, invariants (InvA) and (InvB) hold for

{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
,
{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
, and
{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
.
In the stage for each group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
, we will show that these invariants are
preserved and that an appropriate bound on amortized profits holds. This will give us
that inequality (
5.24
) holds when processing interval
(\beta,\gamma]

and that invariants (InvA) and (InvB) hold afterwards, that is for
sets
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}
,
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
, and
{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
.

Note that the changes of
{{\mathcal{P}}}
implemented by the algorithm
in the stage for group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
satisfy

{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\setminus{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}(\eta,\tau_{b}]={\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\setminus{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}(\eta,\tau_{b}]

for
\eta=\textsf{prevts}({\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}},d^{t}_{h_{a}})
,
which is the “locality of changes” invariant (
5.29
) from Section
5.5.3
. The
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{P}}},\tau)

values are actually affected only for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{h_{a}},\tau_{b})
.
(We remark that changes of
{{\mathcal{P}}}
involve packet
h_{b+1}
with
d^{t}_{h_{b+1}}>\tau_{b}
(if
b<k
),
however,
h_{b+1}\notin{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
and we change the deadline of
h_{b+1}
to
\tau_{b}

during the stage for group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
.)

Finally, we will maintain the property that
after the stage for each group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
, packet
h_{a}
will not be in
{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
—
even though it may have been in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
earlier. This is necessary for invariant (InvA), because

h_{a}\notin{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
. As also
\varrho=h_{k+1}\notin{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, this will give us that

h_{b+1}\notin{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
for any group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
.
Recall that, importantly, packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b}
are also not in
{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
.

Deriving inequality (
5.24
).

We split the potential changes and the adversary credit for replacing packets in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
among groups in a natural way.
Let
\Delta_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}\Psi
be the total change of the potential due to the modifications
implemented in the stage for group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
, and let
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}
be the adversary adjustment credit
for the changes of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
implemented in the stage for group
{\langle a,b\rangle}

(that is, for removing a packet or for replacing
h_{a}
by a lighter packet).
Recall that
\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})

is the total amount by which the algorithm increases the weights of
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b+1}
.
Our goal is to prove for each group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
that

\Delta_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}\Psi-\phi{\,}\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}\geq-w^{t}_{h_{a}}+w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}\,.

(5.43)

(Note that the right-hand side of (
5.43
) is negative, by Lemma
B.1
(b).)

It remains to observe that the sum of (
5.43
) over all groups gives us exactly the
key inequality (
5.24
). Indeed, the right-hand sides sum to

-w^{t}_{h_{0}}+w^{t}_{h_{k+1}}=-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t}_{\varrho}
.
Regarding the left-hand side of (
5.43
),
\Delta_{(\beta,\gamma]}\Psi
equals the sum of
\Delta_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}\Psi

over all groups
{\langle a,b\rangle}
, and similarly for
\Delta^{t}{w}
and
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{(\beta,\gamma]}
.
(Note that the increase of the weight of
\varrho=h_{k+1}
is accounted for in inequality (
5.43
)
for the terminal group
{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}
.)

Stage for the terminal group.

Let
{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}
be the terminal group of segments.
In this stage, we change plan
{{\mathcal{P}}}
by removing
h_{{\tilde{a}}}
and adding
\varrho
,
we increase the weights of packets
h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1},\dots,h_{k+1}=\varrho
, and we decrease the deadlines of
packets
h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1},\dots,h_{k}
.
We thus have
{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{h_{{\tilde{a}}}\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
.

Let
{f^{\ast}}
be the earliest-deadline packet in
{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}(\beta,{T}]\setminus{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
.
We argue that packet
{f^{\ast}}
is well-defined, which is trivially true if

\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,{T}]={\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}(\beta,{T}]
.
Otherwise,
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]={\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}(\beta,\gamma]\neq\emptyset

and then Observation
5.2
(a) with
\eta=\beta
implies that

{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}(\beta,{T}]\setminus{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}\neq\emptyset
.
(Possibly,
d_{f^{\ast}}>\gamma
in the second case.)

We then define a packet
f\in{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}\setminus{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
, letting
f=\varrho
if

\varrho\in{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
, and
f={f^{\ast}}
otherwise.

Next, we modify sets
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.
We remove
g
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, i.e.,
{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{g\right\}}
. This
ensures that invariant (InvA) is preserved,
because
h_{{\tilde{a}}}\notin{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
and
none of packets
h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1},\dots,h_{k}
is in
{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
(by the definition of groups), thus
the decrease of their deadlines, implemented in this stage, does not affect
{{\mathcal{A}}}
.

Regarding
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, note that
\varrho\in{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}\setminus{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
, so we need
to include
\varrho
in
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
to preserve invariant (InvB)(ii). Hence, we let

{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\cup{\left\{g\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f,h_{{\tilde{a}}}\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
;
this definition applies no matter whether
g=h_{{\tilde{a}}}
or
g={g^{\ast}}
and whether
f=\varrho
or
f={f^{\ast}}
.

Deriving (
5.43
) for the terminal group.

Apart from the changes in the paragraph above,
the algorithm changes the weights of packets
h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1},\dots,h_{k+1}=\varrho
, which results in the
increase of the weight of the optimal plan. Taking all these changes into account, the terms
on the left-hand side of (
5.43
) are:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}\Psi\;

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(w^{t}_{g}-w^{t}_{f}-w^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}+w^{t+1}_{\varrho}+\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}})\right)

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(w^{t}_{g}-w^{t}_{f}-w^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}+\mu_{k}+\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}})\right)

(5.44)

\displaystyle\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k+1}})\;

\displaystyle=\;\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}})+w^{t+1}_{\varrho}-w^{t}_{\varrho}

\displaystyle=\;\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}})+\mu_{k}-w^{t}_{\varrho}

(5.45)

\displaystyle\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}\;

\displaystyle=\;w^{t}_{g}-{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{g})

(5.46)

Equation (
5.44
) follows from the definition of
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
,
as
\varrho
is added to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
with its new weight
w^{k+1}_{\varrho}=\mu_{k}
and
packets
{h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}}
remain in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, although their weights are changed.
(Recall that for each group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
, packets
h_{a+1},...,h_{b}
are always in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.)
Equation (
5.45
) holds because
w^{k+1}_{\varrho}=\mu_{k}
.
In
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, we just removed
g
, implying (
5.46
).
(See Section
5.1
for the definition of the adversary adjustment credit associated with the
removal of packets from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
.)

We further claim that the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}})\;

\displaystyle\leq\;\mu_{{\tilde{a}}}-\mu_{k}

(5.47)

\displaystyle w^{t}_{g}\;

\displaystyle\leq\;w^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}

(5.48)

\displaystyle w^{t}_{f}\;

\displaystyle\leq\;w^{t}_{\varrho}

(5.49)

\displaystyle{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{g})\;

\displaystyle\geq\;\mu_{{\tilde{a}}}

(5.50)

To show (
5.47
), we consider two
simple cases. If there is no
i\in{\langle{{\tilde{a}}}+1,k\rangle}
such that
w^{t}_{h_{i}}<\mu_{i-1}
,
then
\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}})=0\leq\mu_{{\tilde{a}}}-\mu_{k}
as
\mu_{{\tilde{a}}}\geq\mu_{k}
.
Otherwise, we use Lemma
5.11
with
a^{\prime}={{\tilde{a}}}+1
and
b^{\prime}=k
to get

\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}})\leq\mu_{{\tilde{a}}}-w^{t}_{h_{k}}\leq\mu_{{\tilde{a}}}-\mu_{k}
,
where the last inequality follows from
w^{t}_{h_{k}}\geq\mu_{k}
.

Inequality (
5.48
) is trivial if
g=h_{i_{l}}
, in which case also
{{\tilde{a}}}=i_{l}
.
Otherwise,
{{\tilde{a}}}>i_{l}
. By the definition of the terminal group,

{{\tilde{a}}}
is the smallest index with
\tau_{{\tilde{a}}}\geq d^{t}_{g}
, and thus
d^{t}_{g}>\tau_{{{\tilde{a}}}-1}
.
We also have that
h_{{\tilde{a}}}
is the heaviest packet in plan
{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
with deadline in
(\tau_{{{\tilde{a}}}-1},\gamma]
.
(This property holds for plan
{{{P}}^{t}}
, by the algorithm. However,
the changes in Section
5.5.4
, where we process
[t,\alpha]
,
do not affect packets in the optimal plan after the first tight slot
\alpha\leq\beta
;
see also the “locality of changes” invariant in Section
5.5.3
.)
As
g\in{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
and
d^{t}_{g}\in(\tau_{{{\tilde{a}}}-1},\gamma]

(by the definition of
{{\tilde{a}}}
), we conclude that
w^{t}_{g}\leq w^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}
.

Next, we justify (
5.49
). Indeed, since
d^{t}_{f}>\beta

and the only packet possibly added to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
in the analysis of the transmission step
is
{\ell}
with
d^{t}_{\ell}\leq\alpha\leq\beta
,
we have that
f\notin{{{P}}^{t}}
and then the inequality holds by the definition of
\varrho
.

Finally, inequality (
5.50
) follows from
\tau_{{\tilde{a}}}\geq d^{t}_{g}

and the monotonicity of the
{\textsf{minwt}}()
function.

Using the above equations and inequalities, we derive inequality (
5.43
) as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}\Psi\;

\displaystyle-\phi{\,}\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,h_{k+1})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(w^{t}_{g}-w^{t}_{f}-w^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}+\mu_{k}+\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\phi{\,}(\,\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}})+\mu_{k}-w^{t}_{\varrho}\,)-(\,w^{t}_{g}-{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{g})\,)

\displaystyle=\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w^{t}_{g}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w^{t}_{f}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}-\Delta^{t}w({h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}},\dots,{h_{k}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\mu_{k}+\phi{\,}w^{t}_{\varrho}+{\textsf{minwt}}({{{P}}^{t}},d^{t}_{g})

\displaystyle\geq\;-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}{w^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}{w^{t}_{\varrho}}-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}-(\mu_{{\tilde{a}}}-\mu_{k})-\mu_{k}+\phi{\,}w^{t}_{\varrho}+\mu_{{\tilde{a}}}

(5.51)

\displaystyle=\;-w^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}+w^{t}_{h_{k+1}}\,,

(5.52)

where in inequality (
5.51
) we use (
5.47
),
(
5.48
), (
5.49
),
and (
5.50
), and in step (
5.52
) we substituted
w^{t}_{\varrho}=w^{t}_{h_{k+1}}
.

Invariant (InvB) after the stage for the terminal group

We claim that after the stage for the terminal group, invariant (InvB) holds for backup plan
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
.
(The proof given here is an extension of the one for the case L.
[\beta,\gamma)
.S.2
in Section
5.5.5
.)
To show this, we split all changes of this stage into three parts (see Figure
5.7
):

(i)

First, we implement the changes in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
, but postpone
changes in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
, that is, we consider sets

{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
,

{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}\cup{\left\{g\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f\right\}}
, and

{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{g\right\}}={\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
.
The deadlines of packets
h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1},\dots,h_{k}
are yet unchanged.

(ii)

Second, we replace
h_{{\tilde{a}}}
by
\varrho
in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
, but still not implement the deadline changes,
so
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}\setminus{\left\{h_{{\tilde{a}}}\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
,

{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}\setminus{\left\{h_{{\tilde{a}}}\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
,
and
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}={{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
.

(iii)

Third, we decrease the deadlines of packets
h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1},\dots,h_{k}
, ending up with plan
{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}

and backup plan
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
.
(Note that
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
are not affected by the deadline decreases,
since packets
h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1},\dots,h_{k}
are in
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}\setminus{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}
.)

Figure 5.7:
Partition of the stage for the terminal group into three parts in the
proof that invariant (InvB) is preserved.

In the argument for the first two parts, we can assume that
f={f^{\ast}}\neq\varrho
or
g={g^{\ast}}\neq h_{{\tilde{a}}}
(or both),
since if both
f=\varrho
and
g=h_{{\tilde{a}}}
then

{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}={\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
,
in which case invariant (InvB) holds after part (ii), as (InvB)(i) is trivial and (InvB)(ii)
follows from the way
{{\mathcal{P}}}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
are modified in parts (i) and (ii).
(Recall that
g\in{\left\{{g^{\ast}},h_{{{\tilde{a}}}}\right\}}
and that
g=h_{{{\tilde{a}}}}
iff
{{\tilde{a}}}=i_{l}
.)

Claim 5.12
.

Assume that
f={f^{\ast}}\neq\varrho
or
g={g^{\ast}}\neq h_{{\tilde{a}}}
.
Then set
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}\cup{\left\{g\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f\right\}}
is a feasible
set of packets pending at time
t+1
.

Proof.

Recall that
{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
and that the same relations hold for
sets
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
. Also, we are currently in Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.I.2, in which
either
\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
or
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]\neq\emptyset
.

The claim clearly holds if
d^{t}_{f}\leq d^{t}_{g}
.
Otherwise, by the definition of tight slots, it suffices to prove
d^{t}_{f}\leq\textsf{nextts}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},d^{t}_{g})
, from which
the feasibility of
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
follows.
In particular, we show that
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\geq 1
for any
\tau\in[d^{t}_{g},d^{t}_{f})
.

We now consider two cases.
First, assume that
\varrho\notin{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
This implies
f={f^{\ast}}
and
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]\neq\emptyset
,
which means that
d^{t}_{g}\in(\beta,\gamma]
, no matter whether
g=h_{{\tilde{a}}}
or
g={g^{\ast}}
.
Then, for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{g},d^{t}_{f})
, Lemma
5.4
(a) with
\zeta=\beta
gives us

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\;\geq\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)+|{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\tau]|\;\geq\;1,

since
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\tau]
contains
g
and
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\geq 0
.

Otherwise,
\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
, so
f=\varrho
.
In this case,
g={g^{\ast}}
holds (as we assume in this part that
f={f^{\ast}}\neq\varrho
or
g={g^{\ast}}\neq h_{{\tilde{a}}}
).
For
\tau\in[d^{t}_{g},d^{t}_{f})
, we use Lemma
5.4
(b) with
\zeta=\gamma
to obtain

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)\;\geq\;{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)+|{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\tau,\gamma]\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}|\;\geq\;1,

where we use
f=\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\tau,\gamma]\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
in the second inequality.
∎

From the definitions of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
,
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
, and
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
, and using
Claim
5.12
, invariants (InvA) and (InvB) hold for these three
intermediate sets.

Before considering part (ii) (that is, replacing
h_{{\tilde{a}}}
by
\varrho
in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
),
we prove the following claim:

Claim 5.13
.

Assume that
f={f^{\ast}}\neq\varrho
or
g={g^{\ast}}\neq h_{{\tilde{a}}}
. If
d^{t}_{\varrho}<d^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}
, then
{{\tilde{a}}}=k
and

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 1
for any
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}})
.

Proof.

Let
\chi=\textsf{prevts}^{t}(d^{t}_{\varrho})
.
By the definitions of
\gamma
and
\varrho
and since the tight slots of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
are the same as those of
{{{P}}^{t}}

starting with slot
\alpha
,
d^{t}_{\varrho}
is in the segment
(\chi,\gamma]
of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
;
consequently,
d^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}
also belongs to this segment. So
{{\tilde{a}}}=k
, proving the first part of the claim.
Moreover, it follows that
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 1
for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{k}})
.

We now make two observations that will be used later to prove Claim
5.13
.
First, observe that replacing
f
by
g
in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
results in

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}},\tau)+1\geq 1\quad\textrm{for}\;\tau\in[d^{t}_{f},d^{t}_{g})\,,

(5.53)

where the inequality follows from the feasibility of
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
.
(It is possible that
d^{t}_{g}\leq d^{t}_{f}
, in which case this interval is empty.)

Next, let
{\hat{f}}
be the earliest-deadline packet in
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}(\chi,T]\setminus{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
and
let
{\hat{g}}
be the latest-deadline packet in
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}[t+1,\gamma]
;
if
{\hat{f}}
is undefined, we let
d^{t}_{\hat{f}}=T
, and similarly if
{\hat{g}}
is undefined, we let
d^{t}_{\hat{g}}=t+1
.
By Lemma
5.4

(which we can apply because invariants (InvA) and (InvB) are true for
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
,
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
, and
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
),
it holds that

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 1\quad\textrm{for}\;\tau\in(\chi,\min(d^{t}_{\hat{f}},\gamma))\cup[\max(d^{t}_{\hat{g}},\chi+1),\gamma).

(5.54)

Specifically,
we apply Lemma
5.4
(a) with
\zeta=\chi
to derive the first inequality for
\tau\in(\chi,d^{t}_{\hat{f}})
,
and Lemma
5.4
(b) with
\zeta=\gamma
to derive the first inequality for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\hat{g}},\gamma)
.
The second inequality holds because
(\chi,\gamma]
is a segment of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
.

If
d^{t}_{\hat{f}}\geq\gamma
or
d^{t}_{\hat{g}}\leq\chi
, then inequality (
5.54
) directly
implies the lemma, because
[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{k}})\subseteq(\chi,\gamma)
.
Therefore, for the rest of the proof, we will assume that
d^{t}_{\hat{f}}<\gamma
and
d^{t}_{\hat{g}}>\chi
.

To complete the proof,
it is sufficient to show that inequalities (
5.53
) and (
5.54
) cover the whole
range of
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{k}})
, that is

[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{k}})\subseteq(\chi,d^{t}_{\hat{f}})\cup[d^{t}_{f},d^{t}_{g})\cup[d^{t}_{\hat{g}},\gamma).

(5.55)

Note that
d^{t}_{\hat{g}}\leq d^{t}_{g^{\ast}}

and
d^{t}_{\hat{f}}\geq d^{t}_{f^{\ast}}
by the definitions of these packets and of
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
and
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
(note that
g
is not a candidate for
{\hat{f}}
as
g\in{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
).
If
f=\varrho
, then it holds that
g={g^{\ast}}
by the assumption, and the interval
[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{k}})
is covered by

[d^{t}_{f},d^{t}_{g})=[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{g^{\ast}})
and
[d^{t}_{\hat{g}},\gamma)
, because
d^{t}_{\hat{g}}\leq d^{t}_{g^{\ast}}
and
d^{t}_{h_{k}}\leq\gamma
.
Similarly, if
g=h_{k}
, we have
f={f^{\ast}}
and
[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{k}})
is covered by

[d^{t}_{f},d^{t}_{g})=[d^{t}_{f^{\ast}},d^{t}_{h_{k}})
and
(\chi,d^{t}_{\hat{f}})
, since
d^{t}_{\hat{f}}\geq d^{t}_{f^{\ast}}
.
Finally, consider the case of
f={f^{\ast}}
and
g={g^{\ast}}
. Then
the interval
[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{k}})\subseteq(\chi,\gamma)
is covered by

(\chi,d^{t}_{f^{\ast}})\cup[d^{t}_{f^{\ast}},d^{t}_{g^{\ast}})\cup[d^{t}_{g^{\ast}},\gamma)
,
which implies (
5.55
), because

d^{t}_{\hat{f}}\geq d^{t}_{f^{\ast}}
and
d^{t}_{\hat{g}}\leq d^{t}_{g^{\ast}}
.
∎

We now address the second part, where
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
is changed into

{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}\setminus{\left\{h_{{\tilde{a}}}\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
,
and we show that
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}
satisfies invariant (InvB).
We consider the feasibility of
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}
first.
Recall that in this part we assume
f={f^{\ast}}
or
g={g^{\ast}}
.
If
d^{t}_{\varrho}\geq d^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}
, then the feasibility of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
is clearly preserved after replacing
h_{{\tilde{a}}}
by
\varrho
.
Otherwise,
d^{t}_{\varrho}<d^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}}
, in which case, by Claim
5.13
,
we have
{{\tilde{a}}}=k
and
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)-1\geq 0
for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}})
;
while
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)\geq 0
for
\tau\notin[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}})
,
by Claim
5.12
. Therefore,
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}
is indeed feasible.
In this part, we replace
h_{\tilde{a}}
by
\varrho
in both
{{\mathcal{P}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is unchanged,
so invariant (InvB)(ii) is preserved as well.

Finally, we consider the third part of this stage
where the deadline decreases of
h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1},\dots,h_{k}
are implemented,
converting
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}
into
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
.
(Unlike in the first two parts, we perform this part also in the case
f=\varrho
and
g=h_{{\tilde{a}}}
.)
We only need to show that
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}}
remains feasible, since
invariant (InvB)(ii) is not affected.

There is no deadline decrease in this part if
{{\tilde{a}}}=k
, so it remains to deal with the case when
{{\tilde{a}}}<k
.
Recall that the algorithm will change the deadline
d^{t}_{h_{i}}
to
d^{t+1}_{h_{i}}=\tau_{i-1}
, for each
i={{\tilde{a}}}+1,...,k
.
These changes result in decreasing
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{B}}})

by one in each interval
[\tau_{{\tilde{a}}},d^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{a}}}+1}}),\dots,[\tau_{k-1},d^{t}_{h_{k}})
. Other slots in

{{\mathcal{B}}}
are not affected.
These intervals are disjoint, because
d^{t}_{h_{i}}\leq\tau_{i}
for
i=0,\dots,k
. Thus, it suffices to show the following claim.

Claim 5.14
.

Assume that
{{\tilde{a}}}<k
. Then

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)\geq 1
for any
\tau\in[\tau_{{\tilde{a}}},\gamma)
.

Proof.

We first show that
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)\geq 1
for any
\tau\in[\tau_{{\tilde{a}}},\gamma)
.
To this end, we consider two cases.

The first case is when
\tau\in[\tau_{{\tilde{a}}},d^{t}_{\varrho})
. For such
\tau
we have

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)+1\geq 1
,
because replacing
h_{{\tilde{a}}}
by
\varrho
causes
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{P}}},\tau)
to increase by 1 for

\tau\in[d^{t}_{h_{{\tilde{a}}}},d^{t}_{\varrho})\supseteq[\tau_{{\tilde{a}}},d^{t}_{\varrho})
, and because
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
is feasible.

The second case is when
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\varrho},\gamma)
. In this case we argue as follows.
Since for
{{\tilde{a}}}<k
replacing
h_{{\tilde{a}}}
by
\varrho
in
{{\mathcal{P}}}

does not decrease any value
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{P}}},\tau)
(i.e., there are no new tight slots in
{{\mathcal{P}}}
)
and each slot
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\varrho},\gamma)
belongs to the segment of
{{{P}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}
ending at
\gamma
,
we indeed have
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)\geq 1
for any
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\varrho},\gamma)
.

We thus have
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)\geq 1
for all
\tau\in[\tau_{{\tilde{a}}},\gamma)
, as claimed.
To finish the proof,
note that
d^{t}_{g^{\ast}}\leq\tau_{{\tilde{a}}}
by the definitions of
{g^{\ast}}
and the terminal group
{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}
,
and that
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}(\tau_{{\tilde{a}}},\gamma]=\emptyset
. Thus
for
\tau\in[\tau_{{\tilde{a}}},\gamma)
, we can apply Lemma
5.4
(b) with
\zeta=\gamma

to obtain
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{P}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)\geq 1
,
completing the proof.
∎

Putting it all together and using Claim
5.14
, we obtain that

{\textsf{pslack}}({\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)-1\geq 0

for any
\tau\in[\tau_{{\tilde{a}}},\gamma)
, while

{\textsf{pslack}}({\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle{{\tilde{a}}},k\rangle}}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)\geq 0

for
\tau\notin[\tau_{{\tilde{a}}},\gamma)
. This concludes the proof that invariant (InvB) holds after
the stage for the terminal group.

Stage for a middle group.

We now analyze the stage in which the segments represented by a middle group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
of indices are processed.
The current intermediate plan, backup plan, and adversary stash are
{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
,

{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
, and
{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
, respectively, and we assume that they satisfy
invariants (InvA) and (InvB). At this point, it holds that:

(a)

h_{a}\in{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
by the definition of the middle group,

(b)

h_{b+1}\notin{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
as a result of the previous stage (recall that
b<k
for a middle group),
which implies that
h_{b+1}\notin{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
by invariant (InvA), and

(c)

h_{b+1}\notin{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
, because
h_{b+1}
was removed from
{\mathcal{P}}
in the previous stage
(that is, we had
h_{b+1}\in{{{P}}^{t}}(\alpha,{T}]
, but now
h_{b+1}\notin{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
),
and because we do not add any packets to
{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha,{T}]\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
while processing a leap step
(see the “monotonicity of
{{\mathcal{B}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
” invariant in Section
5.5.3
).

The changes of plan
{{\mathcal{P}}}
in this stage involve removing
h_{a}
and adding back
h_{b+1}
;
that is
{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
is changed to
{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}={\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{h_{a}\right\}}\cup{\left\{h_{b+1}\right\}}
.
We also decrease the deadlines of packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b+1}
and
increase the weights of a subset of packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b+1}
, according to the algorithm.
Since
h_{a}
is removed from
{{\mathcal{P}}}
, we also need to remove it from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
to preserve invariant (InvA).
Similarly, as
h_{b+1}
is added back to
{{\mathcal{P}}}
(and is not in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
), it needs to be added
to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
or to
{{\mathcal{A}}}
so that invariant (InvB)(ii) continues to hold.
The changes of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
depend on two cases, which are described below.

Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.M.1
:

There is an index
i\in[a+1,b+1]
such that
w^{t}_{h_{i}}<\mu_{i-1}
, i.e., the algorithm (in line 11) increases the weight of
h_{i}
.
We will remove
h_{a}
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and add
h_{b+1}
to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.
To restore the feasibility of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
,
we apply Lemma
5.5
for packet
g=h_{a}

to identify a packet
f_{a}\in{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\setminus{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
for which set

{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{f_{a}\right\}}\cup{\left\{h_{a}\right\}}
is feasible
and
d^{t}_{f_{a}}>\textsf{prevts}({\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}},d^{t}_{h_{a}})
. We then let

{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\;=\;{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{h_{a}\right\}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\;=\;{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{f_{a}\right\}}\cup{\left\{h_{b+1}\right\}}\,.

With these changes, (InvA) is preserved, since also none of packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b+1}
,
whose deadlines are decreased, is in
{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
.
We will show below that
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
is feasible,
even with the deadlines of
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b+1}
decreased by the algorithm.

Deriving (
5.43
) in Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.M.1.

We need to take into account the addition of
h_{b+1}
in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
possible change of weights of some packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b+1}
. Taking these changes into account, the terms
on the left-hand side of (
5.43
) can be expressed or estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}\Psi\;

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})+w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}-w^{t}_{f_{a}}\right)

(5.56)

\displaystyle\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})\;

\displaystyle\leq\;\mu_{a}-w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}

(5.57)

\displaystyle\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}\;

\displaystyle=\;w^{t}_{h_{a}}-{\textsf{minwt}}({\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}},d^{t}_{h_{a}})\;\leq\;w^{t}_{h_{a}}-\mu_{a}

(5.58)

To justify (
5.56
), note that while

h_{b+1}\notin{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
is added to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
with its new weight
w^{t+1}_{h_{b+1}}
, its
weight increase of
w^{t+1}_{h_{b+1}}-w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}
is already accounted for in
\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})
.

Inequality (
5.57
) can be derived from the case condition,
which says that there is
i\in[a+1,b+1]
such that
w^{t}_{h_{i}}<\mu_{i-1}
, so we just
need to apply Lemma
5.11
with
a^{\prime}=a+1
and
b^{\prime}=b+1

to get (
5.57
).

Inequality (
5.58
) follows from the definition of
\mu_{a}
as

\mu_{a}={\textsf{minwt}}({P}^{t},d^{t}_{h_{a}})
and from the observation that

{\textsf{minwt}}({\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}},d^{t}_{h_{a}})\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P}^{t},d^{t}_{h_{a}})
, which in turn follows from

\textsf{nextts}({\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}},d^{t}_{h_{a}})=\textsf{nextts}({P}^{t},d^{t}_{h_{a}})

and
{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}[t+1,\eta]={P}^{t}[t,\eta]\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
,
where
\eta=\textsf{nextts}({\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}},d^{t}_{h_{a}})
.
(The equality
\textsf{nextts}({\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}},d^{t}_{h_{a}})=\textsf{nextts}({P}^{t},d^{t}_{h_{a}})
holds
because of the “locality of changes” invariant from Section
5.5.3
).

Next, we claim that

w^{t}_{f_{a}}\;\leq\;w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}.

(5.59)

Indeed,
f_{a}
is not in
{P}^{t}
since, by its definition,
f_{a}\notin{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
and

d^{t}_{f_{a}}>\textsf{prevts}({\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}d^{t}_{h_{a}})\geq\beta
, so
f_{a}
could not have been
removed from
{{\mathcal{P}}}
in this step. (Note that
h_{b+1}
is temporarily removed from
{{\mathcal{P}}}
, but it
cannot be equal
f_{a}
because
h_{b+1}\notin{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
).
Thus
w^{t}_{f_{a}}\leq w^{t}_{\varrho}
, because
\varrho
is the heaviest pending packet not in
{{{P}}^{t}}
with deadline after
\beta

and
f_{a}\notin{{{P}}^{t}}
is pending in step
t
.
By Lemma
B.1
(b) we have
w^{t}_{\varrho}\leq w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}
, which implies that

w^{t}_{f_{a}}\leq w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}
. (As an additional clarification, the argument in this paragraph relies tacitly on two properties
of earlier changes in processing the transmission event: One, no packets with deadlines after

\alpha
are added to
{{\mathcal{B}}}\setminus{{\mathcal{P}}}
, and two,
the tight slots of
{\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
in the interval
(\beta,\tau_{b}]
are the same as tight slots in
{{{P}}^{t}}
;
cf. Section
5.5.3
.)

Then we prove (
5.43
) as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}\Psi

\displaystyle-\phi{\,}\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}

\displaystyle\geq\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})+w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}-w^{t}_{f_{a}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\phi{\,}\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})-(w^{t}_{h_{a}}-\mu_{a})

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}-w^{t}_{f_{a}}\right)-\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})-w^{t}_{h_{a}}+\mu_{a}

\displaystyle\geq\;-(\mu_{a}-w^{t}_{h_{b+1}})-w^{t}_{h_{a}}+\mu_{a}

(5.60)

\displaystyle=\;-w^{t}_{h_{a}}+w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}\,,

where inequality (
5.60
) follows from (
5.59
) and (
5.57
).

Invariant (InvB) in Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.M.1.

We claim that after the stage for the middle group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
, invariant (InvB) holds,
which we show by partitioning this stage into three parts:

(i) We first let
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{f_{a}\right\}}\cup{\left\{h_{a}\right\}}
.

(ii) Second, we modify
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
to obtain set
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}={{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}\setminus{\left\{h_{a}\right\}}\cup{\left\{h_{b+1}\right\}}
.

(iii) Finally, we implement the decrease of the deadlines of packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b+1}
,
changing
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}
to
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
.

In each part, we also appropriately modify the sets
{{\mathcal{P}}}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, as illustrated in
Figure
5.8
.

Figure 5.8:
Partition of the stage for the middle group in Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.M.1 into three parts.

That
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
is feasible follows from the choice of
f_{a}
and Lemma
5.5
.
Since
d^{t}_{h_{a}}<d^{t}_{h_{b+1}}
,
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}}
is also a feasible set of packets,
and moreover,
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)+1\geq 1
for any
\tau\in[d^{t}_{h_{a}},d^{t}_{h_{b+1}})
, by the feasibility of
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
.
Finally, we argue that
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
remains feasible. The deadline decreases for packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b+1}

decrease
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{B}}})
by one for slots
in each of the disjoint intervals
[\tau_{a},d^{t}_{h_{a+1}}),\dots,[\tau_{b},d^{t}_{h_{b+1}})
.
As
d^{t}_{h_{a}}\leq\tau_{a}
, all these intervals are contained in
[d^{t}_{h_{a}},d^{t}_{h_{b+1}})
; hence, by the earlier observation,
it holds that
{\textsf{pslack}}({\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(ii)}},\tau)-1\geq 0

for any
\tau\in[d^{t}_{h_{a}},d^{t}_{h_{b+1}})
. For
\tau\notin[d^{t}_{h_{a}},d^{t}_{h_{b+1}})
, the values of
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{B}}})
do not change.
It follows that
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
is feasible; that is, invariant (InvB)(i) holds after the stage.
That invariant (InvB)(ii) holds as well follows directly from the definitions of

{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
,
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
, and
{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
.

Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.M.2
:

For all indices
i\in[a+1,b+1]
we have
w^{t}_{h_{i}}\geq\mu_{i-1}
. Thus in this case
the algorithm does not increase the weights of packets
h_{a+1},...,h_{b+1}
, i.e.,

\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})=0
.

Before describing the changes in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, we argue that
h_{a+1}\notin{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
.
This is trivial if
b>a
, because then packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b}
are not in
{{\mathcal{A}}}

before processing the groups. Otherwise, we have
a=b
. Since in the stages, we
process the groups backwards with respect to time slots,
the stage for the group
{\langle b+1,b^{\prime}\rangle}
containing index
b+1
is already completed, and
it guarantees that
h_{b+1}
is not in
{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle b+1,b^{\prime}\rangle}}}={\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}

and thus not in
{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle b+1,b^{\prime}\rangle}}}={\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
by invariant (InvA), as claimed.
(See also the overview of the partition into stages, earlier in the description of Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.I.2.)

We now change
{{\mathcal{A}}}
as follows:
we remove
h_{a}
from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, and we put
h_{a+1}
in the former slot of
h_{a}
in
{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
,
that is
{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}={\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{h_{a}\right\}}\cup{\left\{h_{a+1}\right\}}
.
This is valid because, as we showed above,
h_{a+1}\notin{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
, and
the new deadline of
h_{a+1}
is
\tau_{a}
.
Then
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}=w^{t}_{h_{a}}-w^{t}_{h_{a+1}}
, as the weight of
h_{a+1}
does not change.
Since packets
h_{a+2},\dots,h_{b+1}
are not in
{\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
,
the decreases of their deadlines do not affect
{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
After these changes, invariant (InvA) will be preserved.

Regarding
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, recall that
h_{b+1}\notin{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
,

h_{a}\in{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
, and
h_{a}\notin{\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
. Also,
if
b>a
then
h_{a+1}\in{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
.
Then, since
h_{a+1}
is newly added to
{{\mathcal{A}}}
,
we let
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}={\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\cup{\left\{h_{b+1}\right\}}\setminus{\left\{h_{a+1}\right\}}
;
it is possible here that
h_{a+1}=h_{b+1}
, in which case
{{\mathcal{B}}}
does not change.

Deriving (
5.43
) in Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.M.2.

In this case, the terms on the left-hand side of (
5.43
) are

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}\Psi\;

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(-w^{t}_{h_{a+1}}+w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}\right)

\displaystyle\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})\;

\displaystyle=\;0

\displaystyle\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}\;

\displaystyle=\;w^{t}_{h_{a}}-w^{t}_{h_{a+1}}

with all formulas following directly from the definition and the fact that
the weights remain unchanged. Plugging these in, we obtain:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}\Psi

\displaystyle-\phi{\,}\Delta^{t}w({h_{a+1}},\dots,{h_{b+1}})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(-w^{t}_{h_{a+1}}+w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}\right)-\phi\cdot 0-(w^{t}_{h_{a}}-w^{t}_{h_{a+1}})

\displaystyle=\;-w^{t}_{h_{a}}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}w^{t}_{h_{a+1}}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}

\displaystyle\geq\;-w^{t}_{h_{a}}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}

(5.61)

\displaystyle=\;-w^{t}_{h_{a}}+w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}\,,

where inequality (
5.61
) follows from
w^{t}_{h_{a+1}}\geq w^{t}_{h_{b+1}}
, by Lemma
B.1
(b) and
a\leq b
.

Invariant (InvB) in Case L.
(\beta,\gamma]
.M.2

We show that
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
is feasible by considering first an intermediate backup plan

{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}\cup{\left\{h_{b+1}\right\}}\setminus{\left\{h_{a+1}\right\}}
,
computed with packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b+1}
having their deadlines unchanged. Since

{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
is obtained by replacing
h_{a+1}
by
h_{b+1}
in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
,
and
d^{t}_{h_{a+1}}\leq d^{t}_{h_{b+1}}
,
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
is feasible;
moreover, it satisfies
{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}},\tau)+1\geq 1

for any
\tau\in[d^{t}_{h_{a+1}},d^{t}_{h_{b+1}})
. Next, to prove the feasibility of
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
,
we decrease the deadlines of packets
h_{a+1},\dots,h_{b+1}
.
This decreases
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{B}}})
by one for slots in disjoint intervals

[\tau_{a+1},d^{t}_{h_{a+2}}),\dots,[\tau_{b},d^{t}_{h_{b+1}})
.
(We do not consider
h_{a+1}
here because this packet was already removed from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.)
These intervals are contained in
[d^{t}_{h_{a+1}},d^{t}_{h_{b+1}})
,
and we thus have
{\textsf{pslack}}({\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)-1\geq 0

for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{h_{a+1}},d^{t}_{h_{b+1}})
. For
\tau\notin[d^{t}_{h_{a+1}},d^{t}_{h_{b+1}})
, the values of

{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{B}}})
do not change. Therefore
{\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle a,b\rangle}}}
is feasible, showing (InvB)(i).
Part (ii) of invariant (InvB) is implied by the changes of sets
{{\mathcal{P}}},{{\mathcal{A}}}
, and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
.

This completes the proof that invariants (InvA) and (InvB) are
preserved after the stage for the middle group
{\langle a,b\rangle}
, and that
inequality (
5.43
) holds for this stage.

Stage for the initial group.

The initial group
{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}
is defined when either
l>0
and
i_{1}>0
or if
l=0
and
{{\tilde{a}}}>0
. In either case,
we have
h_{0},...,h_{{\tilde{b}}}\not\in{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}
.

In the stage in which this group is processed, we change plan
{{\mathcal{P}}}
by removing
h_{0}=p
and adding
h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}
,
so
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}={\dot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}\right\}}
.
(Recall that
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}={\ddot{{P}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}
and the corresponding relation holds
for
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, since we process the stage for the initial group after all other groups.)
We also decrease the deadlines of packets
h_{1},\dots,h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}
, and for some
we increase their weights, according to the algorithm. We do not change
{{\mathcal{A}}}
;
thus
{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}={\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}
and
\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}=0
.
Since packets
h_{1},\dots,h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}
are not in
{\dot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}
, it follows that
invariant (InvA) is preserved. To update
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, we let

{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}\right\}}

(recall that
h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}\notin{\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}
).

Deriving (
5.43
) for the initial group.

The terms on the left-hand side of (
5.43
) can be expressed or estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}\Psi\;

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}}\right)

(5.62)

\displaystyle\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})\;

\displaystyle\leq\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}(w^{t}_{p}-w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})

(5.63)

\displaystyle\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}\;

\displaystyle=\;0

(5.64)

Equations (
5.62
) and (
5.64
) follow directly from the
definitions and the case assumption.

To justify (
5.63
), we consider two cases.
If the algorithm does not increase the weight of any of the packets
h_{1},\dots,h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}
,
then
\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})=0\leq{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}(w^{t}_{p}-{\,}w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})
,
by Lemma
B.1
(b). Otherwise, there is
i\in[1,{{\tilde{b}}}+1]
such that
w^{t}_{h_{i}}<\mu_{i-1}
,
and applying Lemma
5.11
with
a^{\prime}=1
and
b^{\prime}={{\tilde{b}}}+1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})\;

\displaystyle\leq\;\mu_{0}-w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}w^{t}_{p}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{\varrho}-w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}}

(5.65)

\displaystyle\leq\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}(w^{t}_{p}-w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})\,,

(5.66)

where inequality (
5.65
) uses the bound

\mu_{0}\leq{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}w^{t}_{p}+{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}w^{t}_{\varrho}
, that follows from (
5.26
),
and inequality (
5.66
) uses
w^{t}_{\varrho}\leq w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}}
, that follows from Lemma
B.1
(b).
Thus (
5.63
) holds.

The calculation showing (
5.43
) is now quite simple:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}\Psi

\displaystyle-\phi{\,}\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})-\textsf{advcredit}^{t}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}\left(\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}}\right)-\phi{\,}\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})-0

\displaystyle=\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}(-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})-\Delta^{t}w({h_{1}},\dots,{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})

\displaystyle\geq\;{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi}}}{\,}(-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})-{\textstyle{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}}}{\,}(w^{t}_{p}-w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})

(5.67)

\displaystyle=\;-w^{t}_{p}+w^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}}\,,

where inequality(
5.67
) holds by (
5.63
).

Invariant (InvB) after the stage for the initial group.

That invariant (InvB)(ii) is preserved follows directly from the formulas for
{\widetilde{{P}}^{t+1}}
,

{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
, and
{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}
.

To show invariant (InvB)(i), that is the feasibility of
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
,
we consider an intermediate backup plan
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}={\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}\right\}}
,
where the deadlines of packets
h_{1},\dots,h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}
remain unchanged.
Replacing
p
by
h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}
in
{{\mathcal{B}}}
preserves feasibility as
d^{t}_{p}<d^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}}
.
So
{{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}}
is feasible; moreover, it satisfies

{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({\dot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}},\tau)+1\geq 1

for any
\tau\in[d^{t}_{p},d^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})
.

We now decrease the deadlines of packets
h_{1},\dots,h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}
, as in the algorithm.
This decreases
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{B}}},\tau)
by one for slots
\tau
in disjoint intervals

[\tau_{0},d^{t}_{h_{1}}),\dots,[\tau_{{\tilde{b}}},d^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})
. These intervals are contained in
[d^{t}_{p},d^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})
,
and we thus have
{\textsf{pslack}}({\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({{{B}}^{t+1}_{(i)}},\tau)-1\geq 0
for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{p},d^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})
.
The values of
{\textsf{pslack}}({{\mathcal{B}}})
are not affected for slots
\tau\notin[d^{t}_{p},d^{t}_{h_{{{\tilde{b}}}+1}})
.
Therefore
{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}
is feasible;
that is, invariant (InvB)(i) holds after the stage for the initial group and after the algorithm’s step as well.

Summary of Case L.
\boldsymbol{(\beta,\gamma]}
.I.2.

In this case, we processed changes in the interval
(\beta,\gamma]
when

\varrho\in{{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}
or
{{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\mathrm{ins}}}}(\beta,\gamma]\neq\emptyset
.
We divided the segments in this interval that contain packets
h_{i}
into groups,
and each group was processed in a separate stage, in the reverse order (i.e., backwards with respect to time slots).
For each stage, we proved that (i) invariants (InvA) and (InvB) are preserved,
and that (ii) inequality (
5.43
) holds for this stage.
The last stage corresponds to a group
{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}
(that may be of any type),
and we proved that after this last stage the backup plan

{\widetilde{{B}}^{t+1}}={\ddot{{B}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}
satisfies invariant (InvB),
and that the adversary stash
{\widetilde{{A}}^{t+1}}={\ddot{{A}}^{t+1}_{{\langle 0,{{\tilde{b}}}\rangle}}}
satisfies invariant (InvA).
All inequalities (
5.43
) (one for each group) imply the key
inequality (
5.24
), needed for amortized analysis.

This concludes the analysis of an iterated leap step and thus also the analysis of an
event of packet transmission. Together with the analysis of packet arrivals given in Section
5.4
,
this completes the proof of
\phi
-competitiveness of Algorithm
PlanM
, as described in Section
5.3
.

6
Final Comments

Our result establishes a tight bound of
\phi
on the competitive ratio of

PacketSchD
in the deterministic case, settling a long-standing open problem.
There is still a number of intriguing open problems about online
packet scheduling that deserve further study.

Of these open problems, the most prominent one is to establish tight bounds for randomized algorithms
for
PacketSchD
. The best known upper bound to date is

\mathrm{e}/(\mathrm{e}-1)\approx 1.582

[
4
,
9
,
7
,
25
]
.
This ratio is achieved by a memoryless algorithm and it holds
even against an adaptive adversary. No better upper bound for the oblivious adversary is known.
(In fact, against the oblivious adversary, the same ratio can be attained for a more general problem of
online vertex-weighted bipartite matching
[
1
,
13
]
.)
The best lower bounds, to our knowledge,
are
4/3\approx 1.333

[
7
]
against the
adaptive adversary, and
1.25

[
10
]
against the oblivious one,
respectively. (Both lower bounds use
2
-bounded instances and are in fact
tight for
2
-bounded instances.)
Closing these gaps would provide insight into the power of randomization in packet scheduling.

In practical settings, the choice of the packet to transmit needs to be made at speed matching the link’s rate,
so the running time and simplicity of the scheduling algorithm are important factors.
This motivates the study of
memoryless
algorithms for
PacketSchD
, as those algorithms
tend to be easy to implement and fast. All known upper bounds for competitive
randomized algorithms we are aware of are achieved by memoryless algorithms (see
[
15
]
).
For deterministic algorithms, the only memoryless one that beats ratio
2
is the

1.893
-competitive algorithm in
[
14
]
. The main question here is whether the ratio of
\phi
can be achieved by a memoryless algorithm.

Among other models for packet scheduling, optimal competitiveness for the FIFO model is
still wide open, both in the deterministic and randomized cases. We refer the reader to
the (still mostly current) survey of Goldwasser
[
15
]
, who provides a thorough
discussion of various models for online packet scheduling and related open problems.

Appendix A
Appendix: Properties of Optimal Plans

A computation of an online algorithm can be thought of as a sequence
of events, with each event being either a packet arrival or transmitting a packet (which includes
incrementing the current time). In this appendix, we give formal statements of “plan-update lemmas”,
that explain how the structure of the optimal plan changes in response to these events.

The proofs are based on analyzing Algorithm
ComputePlan
, given in Section
3
,
for computing the optimal plan in a greedy fashion. In the proofs, we will refer to
the packets
j
added to the plan in line 5 of this algorithm as
admitted
to the plan,
and to the remaining packets as
rejected
.

We will use notation
{{\mathcal{U}}}
for the current set of pending packets,
which is the default argument for Algorithm
ComputePlan
.
For a set
Z\subseteq{{\mathcal{U}}}
of pending packets and a
packet
j
, by
Z_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
we will denote the set of packets in
Z

that are (strictly) heavier than
j
and by
Z_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
the set of packets in
Z

that are at least as heavy as
j
. (In most cases we will use this notation when

Z
is the optimal plan and
j\in{{\mathcal{U}}}
, but occasionally we apply it in other contexts.)
If
{P}
is an optimal plan, then
{P}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
can be thought of, equivalently, as the set of
all packets that are admitted to set
X
in Algorithm
ComputePlan

when all packets heavier than
j
have already been considered (and similarly for
{P}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
).

In the condition of admitting
j\in{{\mathcal{U}}}
in Algorithm
ComputePlan
(line 4) it is
sufficient to check if
{\textsf{pslack}}(X\cup{\left\{j\right\}},\tau)\geq 0
only for
\tau\geq d_{j}
, because
for smaller values of
\tau
the addition of
j
does not change the value of
{\textsf{pslack}}(\tau)
.
This condition can be equivalently stated as “
{\textsf{pslack}}(X,\tau)\geq 1
for each
\tau\geq d_{j}
”.
Note also that, letting
{P}
be the computed optimal plan, right before processing
j
we have
X=P_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
,
and right after processing
j
we have
X=P_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
.

The following observation summarizes some simple but useful properties of plans.

Observation A.1
.

Let
{{\mathcal{U}}}
be the set of packets pending at time
t
and let
{P}
be the optimal plan at time
t
. Then

(a)

{P}
depends only on the ordering of packets in
{{\mathcal{U}}}
with respect to weights
(not on the actual values of the weights).

(b)

{P}
does not change if the weight of any packet in
{P}
is increased.
It also does not change if the weight of any packet in
{{\mathcal{U}}}\setminus{P}

is decreased.

(c)

For a packet
j\in{P}
, let
\beta=\textsf{prevts}({P},d_{j})
.
Then for any slot
\xi\in(\beta,d_{j}]
there is a schedule of
{P}
in
which
j
is scheduled in slot
\xi
.

(d)

If
j\in{{\mathcal{U}}}
is a packet and
\zeta
a slot such that
{\textsf{pslack}}({{P}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\zeta)=0
,
then
{{P}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}[t,\zeta]={{P}}[t,\zeta]
, and consequently

{\textsf{pslack}}({{P}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({{P}},\tau)
for all
\tau\in[t,\zeta]
.

Proof.

Part (a) follows directly from the correctness of Algorithm
ComputePlan
that computes
{P}
.
Part (b) is also straightforward:
{P}
is heavier than any other plan (feasible set of pending packets) for
{{\mathcal{U}}}
.
If the weight of
j\in{P}
is increased by

\delta>0
, the weight of
{P}
will increase by
\delta
,
and as the weight of any other plan
X
increases by at most
\delta
, plan
{P}

remains to be optimal.
The proof for the second part of (b) is similar.

To show (c), compute the desired schedule as follows. First, schedule packets in
{P}

in the canonical order. In this schedule,
j
will be scheduled at some slot
\xi^{\prime}\in(\beta,d_{j}]
.
If
\xi^{\prime}=\xi
, we are done.
If
\xi^{\prime}\in(\beta,\xi)
, shift the packets in
[\xi^{\prime}+1,\xi]
to the left by one slot and schedule
j
at
\xi
.
The last case is when
\xi^{\prime}\in(\xi,d_{j}]
. In this case, we use the fact that

{\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)\geq 1
for all
\tau\in(\beta,d_{j})
, which means that for any
\tau\in(\beta,d_{j})

the number of packets in
{P}
with deadline at most
\tau
is strictly less than
\tau-t+1
,
the number of slots in
[t,\tau]
. Then, by the canonical ordering, we have that
none of the packets scheduled in
[\xi,\xi^{\prime}-1]
is scheduled at its deadline.
Therefore, we can shift these packets to the right by one slot, which allows us to schedule
j
at slot
\xi
.

Part (d) also follows from the correctness of
ComputePlan
: Consider its run that produces
{P}
.
Immediately after packet
j
is processed we have
{\textsf{pslack}}({X}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\zeta)=0
,
i.e., slot
\zeta
is already tight.
Then no packet lighter than
j
with deadline at most
\zeta
will be later admitted to
X
.
Thus,
{P}[t,\zeta]=X[t,\zeta]
is already fixed at that point, and so are its slack values.
∎

In the rest of this section we now deal with plan-update lemmas.
First, in Section
A.1
we explain how the optimal
plan changes in response to packet arrivals.
In Section
A.2
we detail how
to update the optimal plan after some arbitrary packet from the plan is transmitted. The
plan properties in these two sections are not specific to our algorithm – they address general
properties of optimal plans. Later in Section
B
,
we give the plan-update lemma specifically for a leap step of Algorithm
PlanM
.

A.1
Updating the Optimal Plan after a Packet Arrival

In this section, we address the question of how the optimal plan changes when a new packet is
released. So consider some step
t
, and let
{{\mathcal{U}}}
be the current set of pending
packets and
{P}
the optimal plan for
{{\mathcal{U}}}
. Suppose that now
another packet
s
is released, and let
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{U}}}\cup{\left\{s\right\}}
be
the new set of pending packets. Denote by
{Q}
the optimal plan for
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
.
The lemma below explains how to determine
{Q}
from
{P}
and
it gives its important properties. (See Figure
A.1
for illustration.)

Lemma A.2
.

(The Plan-Update Lemma for Packet Arrival.)
Consider the scenario above, involving the arrival of a new packet
s
.
Let
\gamma=\textsf{nextts}({P},d_{s})
and define
f
to be the packet in
{P}[t,\gamma]
with
w_{f}={\textsf{minwt}}({P},d_{s})
;
in other words, the minimum-weight packet in
{P}[t,\gamma]
. Also, let
\beta=\textsf{prevts}({P},d_{f})
.
By definition, we have
\beta<\gamma
and
d_{f},d_{s}\in(\beta,\gamma]
.

If
w_{s}<w_{f}
, then
{Q}={P}
, i.e.,
s
is not added to the optimal plan.

If
w_{s}>w_{f}
, then
{Q}={P}\cup{\left\{s\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f\right\}}
, i.e.,

s
is added to the optimal plan replacing
f
. In this case the following properties hold:

(a)

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)
for

\tau\in[t,\min(d_{f},d_{s}))\cup[\max(d_{f},d_{s}),{T}]
.

(b)

For slots
\tau\in[\min(d_{f},d_{s}),\max(d_{f},d_{s}))
, there are two cases:

(b1)

If
d_{s}>d_{f}
, then

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)+1
for
\tau\in[d_{f},d_{s})
. It follows that all segments of
{P}
in the interval
(\beta,\gamma]

get merged into one segment
(\beta,\gamma]
of
{Q}
.

(b2)

If
d_{s}\leq d_{f}
,
then
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)-1
for
\tau\in[d_{s},d_{f})
,
and thus there might be new tight slots in
[d_{s},d_{f})
.
This case happens only when
(\beta,\gamma]
is itself a segment of
{P}
and it contains
both
d_{f}
and
d_{s}
. This segment may get split into multiple segments of
{Q}
.

(c)

For any slot
\tau\geq t
,
{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},\tau)\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
.

We remark that packet
f
satisfies
