w_{f}\neq w_{s}
, by assumption
(A2)
.

Figure A.1:
An
illustration of changes of tight slots and segments
in Case (b1) of Lemma
A.2
on the left and in Case (b2) on the right.
The segments are separated by vertical dark-red line segments.

Proof.

We start by proving how the set of packets in the optimal plan changes.
Let
{{\mathcal{U}}}
be the set of packets pending before
s
is released and

{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{U}}}\cup{\left\{s\right\}}
be the set of pending packets after the release of
s
.
We consider two “parallel” runs of Algorithm
ComputePlan
, one on
{{\mathcal{U}}}
to compute
{P}

and the other on
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
to compute
{Q}
.

Suppose first that
w_{s}<w_{f}
. Then both runs will be identical until right after the time
when
f
is considered. Thus
{P}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}f}={Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}f}
.
Moreover, as
f
is the minimum-weight packet in
{P}

with
d_{f}\leq\gamma=\textsf{nextts}({P},d_{s})
, we have
{P}[t,\gamma]\subseteq{P}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}f}
, that is,
all packets in
{P}
with deadline at most
\gamma
are in
{P}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}f}
.
Thus
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}f},\gamma)={\textsf{pslack}}({P}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}f},\gamma)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\gamma)=0
.
Therefore, as
d_{s}\leq\gamma
and as
s
is considered
after
f
, packet
s
will not be admitted, which implies that both runs produce the same plan
{P}={Q}
.

For the rest of the proof, we assume that
w_{s}>w_{f}
. To prove that
{Q}={P}\cup{\left\{s\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f\right\}}
,
based on Observation
A.1
(b), without loss of generality we can assume that

w_{s}=w_{f}+\varepsilon
for a tiny
\varepsilon>0
,
so that
s
immediately precedes
f
in the ordering of
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
by decreasing weights.
Specifically, since
f\in{P}
,
{P}
does not change if
f
’s weight is increased to

w_{s}-\varepsilon
. We will show that
{Q}={P}\cup{\left\{s\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f\right\}}

with this increased weight of
f
. But as then
f\notin{Q}
, decreasing
f
’s weight
back to its original value does not change
Q
.

With the assumption about
w_{f}
, the run on
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
before
s
is considered will be identical to the run on
{{\mathcal{U}}}

before
f
is considered, thus
{Q}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}s}={P}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}f}
. In the next step of these
runs, we consider packet
f
for
{P}
and
s
for
{Q}
. We now have two cases.

Suppose that
d_{s}>d_{f}
. Then,
since
f
is admitted to
{P}
, we have
{\textsf{pslack}}({P}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}f},\tau)\geq 1
for
\tau\geq d_{f}
,
which, by the paragraph above, implies that
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}s},\tau)\geq 1
for
\tau\geq d_{s}
; and
thus
s
will be admitted to
{Q}
. As
d_{f},d_{s}\leq\gamma
, we also have

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}s},\gamma)={\textsf{pslack}}({P}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}f},\gamma)=0
. This means that in the
run on
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
packet
f
, which is considered next, will be rejected.
From this point on, both runs are again the same, because no packet with deadline at most
\gamma

is admitted, and because for
\tau>\gamma
and any packet
g
lighter than
f
we have

{\textsf{pslack}}({P}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}g},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}g},\tau)
, so

g
gets admitted to
{Q}
if and only if
g
is admitted to
{P}
.

Next, consider the case when
d_{s}\leq d_{f}
. In this case, since also
d_{f}\leq\gamma
, we have

\textsf{nextts}({P},d_{f})=\gamma
, so the interval
(\beta,\gamma]
is in fact a single segment of
{P}
.
(This also proves the second claim in Case (b2), namely that
(\beta,\gamma]
is a segment of
{P}
.)
This gives us that
{\textsf{pslack}}({P}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}f},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)\geq 1
for all
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma)
.
Since
{P}
admits
f
, we also have
{\textsf{pslack}}({P}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}f},\tau)\geq 1
for all
\tau\geq d_{f}
.
Therefore
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}s},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}f},\tau)\geq 1
for all
\tau\geq d_{s}
,
which implies that
{Q}
will admit
s
. As in the previous case, we will then have

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}s},\gamma)={\textsf{pslack}}({P}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}f},\gamma)=0
, so the run for
{Q}

will not admit
f
(which is next in the ordering), and the remainders of both runs will be identical.

Next, to prove parts (a)–(c) in the case
w_{s}>w_{f}
, we
analyze the changes of
{\textsf{pslack}}()
and
{\textsf{minwt}}()
caused by the arrival of
s
.

(a) This part is straightforward, as replacing
f
by
s
in the plan does not change any value of

|{P}[t,\tau]|
for slots
\tau\in[t,\min(d_{f},d_{s}))\cup[\max(d_{f},d_{s}),{T}]
.
In particular,
\beta
and
\gamma
remain tight in
{Q}
, and
slots in
(\beta,\min(d_{f},d_{s}))\cup[\max(d_{f},d_{s}),\gamma)
are not tight in
{Q}
.

(b)
Recall that
{Q}={P}\cup{\left\{s\right\}}\setminus{\left\{f\right\}}
.
Suppose that
d_{s}>d_{f}
. Then
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)+1

for
\tau\in[d_{f},d_{s})
as
|{Q}[t,\tau]|=|{P}[t,\tau]|-1
for such
\tau
.
So there are no tight slots in the interval
[d_{f},d_{s})
in
{Q}
. This shows (b1).

The argument for (b2) is similar. Suppose that
d_{s}\leq d_{f}
. As already justified
earlier,
(\beta,\gamma]
is a segment of
{P}
. Hence, as
\beta<d_{s}\leq d_{f}\leq\gamma
,
the slack values for
\tau\in[d_{s},d_{f})
are strictly positive in
{P}
.
Further,
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)-1\geq 0
for
\tau\in[d_{s},d_{f})
.
This means that in
{Q}
there may appear additional tight slots in
[d_{s},d_{f})
.

(c) For
\tau\in[t,\beta]
, the set of packets with deadline at most
\tau

does not change and tight slots up to
\beta
remain the same.
Hence,
{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
for such
\tau
.
For
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma]
, it holds that
{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)=w_{f}

and, as
s
replaces
f
and
\gamma
remains a tight slot,
all packets in
{Q}
with deadlines at most
\textsf{nextts}({Q},\tau)\leq\gamma
are heavier than
f
, so
we actually have
{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},\tau)>{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
.
Finally, consider
\tau>\gamma
. Since tight slots after
\gamma
are unchanged,
the set of packets considered in the definition of

{\textsf{minwt}}(\tau)
changes only by replacing
f
by
s
with
w_{s}>w_{f}
, which implies
{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},\tau)\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
.
∎

The observation below provides a characterization of optimal plans in terms of the
{\textsf{minwt}}()
function.
Its
(\Rightarrow)
implication follows from the first claim of Lemma
A.2
.
The
(\Leftarrow)
implication follows by noting that if a plan
{P}
satisfies the condition
in the observation then running Algorithm
ComputePlan
on
{{\mathcal{U}}}
will produce
exactly the set of packets in
{P}
. (The formal proof proceeds by induction, showing
that packets from
{P}
will be admitted to
X
because
{P}
is feasible, but
packets from
{{\mathcal{U}}}\setminus{P}
will not, as the condition in line (4) will be violated
at the time they are considered.)

Observation A.3
.

A plan
{P}
is optimal for a set
{{\mathcal{U}}}
of pending packets if and only if

w_{j}<{\textsf{minwt}}({P},d_{j})
for all packets
j\in{{\mathcal{U}}}\setminus{P}
.

A.2
Updating the Optimal Plan after Transmitting a Packet

Next, we analyze how the optimal plan evolves after an algorithm transmits a packet from the current
optimal plan. (Only such transmissions are relevant to our analysis as in our algorithm
PlanM

packets from outside the optimal plan are not considered for transmission.)
We stress that in this section we only analyze the effects of the event of a packet being transmitted, and the
properties we establish are independent of the algorithm.
In Appendix
B
we will deal with
the changes in the plan specific to
PlanM
, namely with packet transmissions that
also involve adjustments of the instance.

So let
{{\mathcal{U}}}
be the set of packets pending at a time
t
and denote by

{P}={P}^{t}
the optimal plan for
{{\mathcal{U}}}
. Suppose that we choose some
packet
p\in{P}
, transmit it at time
t
, and advance the time to
t+1
.
The set
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
of packets pending at time
t+1
is obtained from

{{\mathcal{U}}}
by removing
p
and all packets that expire at time
t
.
In this section,
{Q}
will denote the new optimal plan for
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
and time step
t+1
;
that is
{Q}={\widetilde{{P}}}^{t+1}
in the notation from Section
5.5
.

We now explain how to obtain
{Q}
from
{P}
.
There are two cases, depending on whether or not
p
is in the initial segment
[t,\alpha]
of
{P}
,
where
\alpha=\textsf{nextts}({P},t)
.
We start with the case when
p
is in the initial segment, which is quite straightforward.
See Figure
A.2
for an illustration.

Lemma A.4
.

(The Plan-Update Lemma for Transmitting
p\in{P}[t,\alpha]
.)
Suppose that at time
t
we transmit a packet
p\in{P}[t,\alpha]
,
where
\alpha=\textsf{nextts}({P},t)
. Then:

(a)

{Q}={P}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
. Thus the new optimal plan
{Q}
is obtained from
{P}

simply by removing
p
.

(b)

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)-1
for
\tau\in[t+1,d_{p})

and
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)
for
\tau\geq d_{p}
.
Thus new tight slots may appear in
[t+1,d_{p})
.

(c)

For any slot
\tau\geq t+1
,
{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},\tau)\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
.

Figure A.2:
An illustration of changes of tight slots
and segments in Lemma
A.4
.

Note that part (b) is meaningful in the special case when
d_{p}=t
, as according to our definition
of
{\textsf{pslack}}()
,
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},t)
is defined and equal to
0
, that is slot
t
is considered tight at time
t+1
.

Proof.

Observe first that
{P}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\subseteq{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
. Indeed, if some

q\in{P}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
had
d_{q}=t
, it would mean that the first tight slot
\alpha
is
t
,
so
q
would have to be equal to
p
.

(a) According to Observation
A.1
(c),
{P}
has a schedule where
p
is scheduled in slot
t
.
This implies directly that
{P}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
is a feasible set of packets at time
t+1
. If
{Q}^{\prime}\neq{P}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
is any other
feasible subset of
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
then
{Q}^{\prime}
would have a schedule starting at time
t+1
, so

{P}^{\prime}={Q}^{\prime}\cup{\left\{p\right\}}
would have a schedule starting at time
t
, and thus would be feasible at time
t
.
Since
{P}
is heavier than
{P}^{\prime}
,
{P}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
is heavier than
{Q}^{\prime}
; thus proving that the optimal plan
at time
t+1
is indeed
{Q}={P}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
.

(b)
For
\tau\in[t+1,d_{p})
,
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)-1
as in
{Q}
the time is incremented
and
|{Q}[t+1,\tau]|=|{P}[t+1,\tau]|
.
For
\tau\geq d_{p}
, incrementing the time for
{Q}
is compensated by
p
not contributing to
{Q}[t+1,\tau]

(that is
|{Q}[t+1,\tau]|=|{P}[t+1,\tau]|-1
), and thus
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)
.

(c) By (b), the value of
\textsf{nextts}({P},\tau)
may only decrease.
Since also no packet is added to the plan, in the definition of
{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},\tau)

we consider a subset of packets used to define
{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
, which shows (c).
∎

For the case
p\in{P}(\alpha,{T}]
, recall that the substitute packet
\textsf{sub}({P},p)
for
p

is the heaviest pending packet
\varrho\in{{\mathcal{U}}}\setminus{P}
satisfying
d_{\varrho}>\textsf{prevts}({P},d_{p})
.
We prove that
\textsf{sub}({P},p)
really appears in the plan when
p
is transmitted.
See Figure
A.3
for an illustration.

Lemma A.5
.

(The Plan-Update Lemma for Transmitting
p\ \in{P}(\alpha,{T}]
.)
Suppose that at time
t
we transmit a packet
p\in{P}(\alpha,{T}]
, where
\alpha=\textsf{nextts}({P},t)
.
Let
\varrho=\textsf{sub}({P},p)
,
\beta=\textsf{prevts}({P},d_{p})
,
\gamma=\textsf{nextts}({P},d_{\varrho})
,
and denote by
{\ell}
the lightest packet in
{P}[t,\alpha]
, i.e., in the initial segment of
{P}
. Then:

(a)

{Q}={P}\setminus{\left\{p,{\ell}\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
.
Thus the new optimal plan
{Q}
is obtained from
{P}
by removing
{\ell}
and
replacing
p
by
\varrho
.

(b)

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)-1
for
\tau\in[t+1,d_{\ell})
.

(c)

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)

for
\tau\in[d_{\ell},\min(d_{p},d_{\varrho}))\cup[\max(d_{p},d_{\varrho}),{T}]
.

(d)

For
\tau\in[\min(d_{p},d_{\varrho}),\max(d_{p},d_{\varrho}))
there are two cases:

(d1)

If
d_{\varrho}>d_{p}
, then
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)+1
for
\tau\in[d_{p},d_{\varrho})
.
This means that all segments of
{P}
in
(\beta,\gamma]
get merged into one segment
(\beta,\gamma]
of
{Q}
.

(d2)

If
d_{\varrho}<d_{p}
, then

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)-1
for
\tau\in[d_{\varrho},d_{p})
.
In this case
(\beta,\gamma]
is itself a segment of
{P}
, and in
{Q}

there might be new tight slots in
[d_{\varrho},d_{p})
that partition
(\beta,\gamma]
into smaller segments.

Figure A.3:
An illustration of changes of tight slots
and segments, in Case (d1) of Lemma
A.5
on the top
and in Case (d2) on the bottom.

Proof.

(a) Let
{{\mathcal{U}}}
be the set of packets pending at time
t
before transmitting
p
and

{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
be the packets that remain pending at time
t+1
after
p
is transmitted;
that is,
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
is obtained from
{{\mathcal{U}}}
by removing
p
and all packets that expire at time
t
.
We observe first that
{P}\setminus{\left\{p,{\ell}\right\}}\subseteq{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
.
Indeed, if there was some
q\in{P}\setminus{\left\{p,{\ell}\right\}}
with
d_{q}=t
, this would
imply that
\alpha=t
, so
d_{\ell}=t
and thus
{\ell}=q
, contradicting the choice of
q
.
Obviously,
p\notin{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
.
As for
{\ell}
, it may or may not be in
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
, depending on whether or not
d_{\ell}>t
.
We also have
\varrho\in{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
, because
d_{\varrho}>\beta\geq t
.

We prove that
{Q}={P}\setminus{\left\{p,{\ell}\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
in two substeps:

•

First, we increment the time to
t+1
but without transmitting
p
. After this substep the set
of pending packets is
{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}={\left\{a\in{{\mathcal{U}}}{\;:\;}d_{a}\geq t+1\right\}}
, i.e.,
we exclude packets that expire at time
t
. We show that the optimal plan for
{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}
is

{\overline{{Q}}}={P}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
.

•

In the second substep we remove

p
from
{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}
(and transmit it in time slot
t
), so the new set of pending packets
will be
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}={\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
, and we prove that the new optimal plan
will be
{Q}={\overline{{Q}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
.
(Recall that we have
p\neq{\ell}

as
p\not\in{P}[t,\alpha]
and
{\ell}\in{P}[t,\alpha]
.)

The intuition is that these two substeps are largely independent: incrementing
t
to
t+1
will
squeeze out
{\ell}
from
{P}
, no matter what packets with deadlines after
\alpha

are removed from
{{\mathcal{U}}}
.

First substep.

For the first substep, we use an argument similar to the one in Lemma
A.4
.
Observe that
{P}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
is a feasible set of packets at time
t+1
,
since
{P}
has a schedule where
{\ell}
is in slot
t
, by Observation
A.1
(c).
Let
{Q}^{\prime}\neq{P}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
be any other subset of
{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}
that is feasible at time
t+1
;
we show that
w({Q}^{\prime})<w({P}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}})
.
Since
{P}
contains exactly
\alpha-t+1
packets with deadline at most
\alpha
,
there is a packet
q^{\prime}\in{P}[t,\alpha]
such that
q^{\prime}\notin{Q}^{\prime}

(possibly
q^{\prime}={\ell}
). As
{Q}^{\prime}
has a schedule starting at time
t+1
,

{P}^{\prime}={Q}^{\prime}\cup{\left\{q^{\prime}\right\}}
has a schedule starting at time
t
and thus is feasible at time
t
.
Since
{P}
is at least as heavy as
{P}^{\prime}
and
{\ell}
is the lightest packet in
{P}[t,\alpha]
,
implying
w_{\ell}\leq w_{q^{\prime}}
, we conclude that indeed
w({P}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}})\geq w({Q}^{\prime})
.
Hence, the plan after incrementing the time is indeed
{\overline{{Q}}}={P}\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
.

Thus we can think of
{\overline{{Q}}}
as being obtained from
{P}
by advancing the time to
t+1
and removing
{\ell}
.
It follows that
{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)
for
\tau\geq d_{\ell}
.
In particular,
\beta
is a tight slot of
{\overline{{Q}}}
and
\beta=\textsf{prevts}({\overline{{Q}}},d_{p})
.
Further, as
{{\mathcal{U}}}
and
{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}
contain the same packets with deadline after
\beta

and as
\beta\geq d_{\ell}
, we get that

\varrho
is the heaviest packet in
{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}\setminus{\overline{{Q}}}
with deadline greater than
\beta
.
Summarizing, we have

\displaystyle\beta\;

\displaystyle=\;\textsf{prevts}({\overline{{Q}}},d_{p})

(A.1)

\displaystyle\varrho\;

\displaystyle=\;{\textrm{argmax}}_{j}{\left\{w_{j}{\;:\;}j\in{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}\setminus{\overline{{Q}}}\;\&\;d_{j}>\beta\right\}}

(A.2)

\displaystyle\gamma\;

\displaystyle=\;\textsf{nextts}({\overline{{Q}}},d_{\varrho})

(A.3)

Second substep.

In the second substep we establish a relationship between
{\overline{{Q}}}
and
{Q}
.
To this end, we consider two “parallel” runs of the greedy algorithm to compute plans: one run
of
{\textsf{ComputePlan}}({\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}},t+1)
which yields
{\overline{{Q}}}
and another run
of
{\textsf{ComputePlan}}({{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime},t+1)
which yields
{Q}
. (Recall that
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}={\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
.)
The contents of the set
X
in these two runs after processing packets heavier than some packet
j
is equal to

{\overline{{Q}}}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
and
{{Q}}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
, respectively. To avoid ambiguity, we will
be using these notations when referring to the contents of
X
during these runs.

We divide these two runs into three phases. In the first phase we consider the two runs for packets
j
with
w_{j}>w_{p}

(that is, before
p
gets admitted to
{\overline{{Q}}}
). Since
{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}p}={{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}p}
,
in this phase the two runs are exactly the same. This gives us that

{\overline{{Q}}}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}p}\;=\;{Q}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}p}.

The second phase involves packets
j
with
w_{p}\geq w_{j}>w_{\varrho}
.
We claim that during this phase the following invariants will be maintained:

\displaystyle{Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}\;

\displaystyle=\;{{\overline{{Q}}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}

(A.4)

\displaystyle{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\tau)\;

\displaystyle=\;\begin{cases}{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\tau)&\quad\text{for }\tau\in[t+1,\beta]\\
{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\tau)\geq 1&\quad\text{for }\tau\in(\beta,d_{p})\\
{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\tau)+1&\quad\text{for }\tau\geq d_{p}\\
\end{cases}

(A.5)

We first show that these properties hold for
j=p
. We have
p\in{\overline{{Q}}}
and
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}={\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}
;
this means that
p
will be admitted to
{\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}p}
in the run for
{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}

but it will not be considered in the run for
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\prime}
. Thus Property (
A.4
) follows.
Invariant (
A.4
) and the definition of
{\textsf{pslack}}()
imply all relations
between
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}p},\tau)
and
{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}p},\tau)

in invariant (
A.5
), and the inequality

{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}p},\tau)\geq 1
for
\tau\in(\beta,d_{p})
follows immediately from (
A.1
).
(Note that
{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}a},\tau)\geq{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}},\tau)
holds for any packet
a
and slot
\tau
.)

Next, consider packets
j
with
w_{p}>w_{j}>w_{\varrho}
. If
j
gets admitted to

{\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
then invariant (
A.5
) implies that
it also gets admitted to
{{Q}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
, preserving (
A.4
).
That (
A.5
) is preserved follows from the same argument as for
j=p
above.

So consider the second case, when
j
does not get admitted to
{\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
.
We claim that
j
will also not be admitted to
{{Q}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
. To justify this, we argue as follows.
By (
A.2
),
\varrho
is the heaviest packet in
{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}
with deadline after
\beta

that will not be admitted to
{\overline{{Q}}}
. Therefore, since
w_{j}>w_{\varrho}
, we have
d_{j}\leq\beta
.
Suppose for a contradiction that
j
is admitted to
{{Q}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
.
As
j
is not admitted to
{\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
, there must be
\tau\geq d_{j}
such that

{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\tau)=0
, although
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\tau)>0
as
j
is admitted to
{{Q}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
.
Due to invariant (
A.5
) for
\tau\in[t+1,\beta]
,
we must have
\tau>\beta
. As
\tau
is already tight, by Observation
A.1
(d)
we get that
{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\beta)={\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}},\beta)=0
,
where the second step holds by (
A.1
).
Using invariant (
A.5
) for
\tau\in[t+1,\beta]
,
we obtain
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\beta)={\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\beta)=0
,
contradicting that we must have
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\beta)>0

as
j
is admitted to
{{Q}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
and
d_{j}\leq\beta
.
Thus we indeed have that
j
will not be admitted to
{{Q}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
.
As in this case
j
is not admitted to both
{\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
and
{{Q}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
,
both invariants (
A.4
) and (
A.5
) are obviously preserved.

In the third phase we analyze both runs for packets
j
with
w_{j}\leq w_{\varrho}
. We claim that for these
packets the following invariants hold:

\displaystyle{Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}\;

\displaystyle=\;{{\overline{{Q}}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}\setminus{\left\{p\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}

(A.6)

\displaystyle{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\tau)\;

\displaystyle=\;{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\tau)\quad\text{for }\tau\geq\gamma

(A.7)

\displaystyle{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\gamma)\;

\displaystyle=\;{\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j},\gamma)\;=\;0

(A.8)

Note that property (
A.7
) follows directly from (
A.6
), because

d_{p},d_{\varrho}\leq\gamma
. Thus we only need to show that (
A.6
) and (
A.8
)
hold for all
j
in this phase.

We first consider
j=\varrho
. As
\varrho\notin{\overline{{Q}}}
,
\varrho
will not be admitted to
{\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}\varrho}
.
From (
A.5
) and (
A.2
) we have that
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\triangleright{\hskip 0.5pt}}\varrho},\tau)\geq 1
for

\tau\geq d_{\varrho}
, and thus
\varrho
will be admitted to
{Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}\varrho}
. This gives us (
A.6
) and (
A.7
).
Since
\varrho\notin{\overline{{Q}}}
, all packets in
{\overline{{Q}}}[t+1,\gamma]
are heavier than
\varrho
; in other words,
we have
{\overline{{Q}}}[t+1,\gamma]={\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}\varrho}[t+1,\gamma]
, which together with (
A.3
) implies that

\gamma=\textsf{nextts}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}\varrho},d_{\varrho})
. Then,
from (
A.7
) (that we already established for
\varrho
), we obtain that

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}\varrho},\gamma)={\textsf{pslack}}({\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}\varrho},\gamma)=0
.

In the rest of the third phase, for each packet
j
with
w_{j}<w_{\varrho}
, condition (
A.8
) implies
that if
d_{j}\leq\gamma
then
j
will not be admitted to
{\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
and also to
{Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
.
On the other hand, if
d_{j}>\gamma
, then condition (
A.7
) implies that

j
will be either admitted to both
{\overline{{Q}}}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
and
{Q}_{{\trianglerighteq{\hskip 0.5pt}}j}
or to none.
This completes the proof that the above invariants are preserved, and the proof of (a) as well.

(b)
We now analyze the changes in the values of
{\textsf{pslack}}()
. If
d_{\ell}>t
, it holds that

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)-1
for
\tau\in[t+1,d_{\ell})
, as in the transmission step
the time was incremented and there is no change in the set of packets taken into account.

(c)
For
\tau\in[d_{\ell},\min(d_{\varrho},d_{p}))
we have
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)

as in the transmission step the time was incremented, but
{\ell}
was forced out. (In particular,
since
d_{\ell}\leq\alpha<\min(d_{\varrho},d_{p})
,
\alpha
is a tight slot in
{Q}
, including the special case when
\alpha=t
.)
Similarly, for
\tau\geq\max(d_{\varrho},d_{p})
, it holds that

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)
, since in the transmission step the time was incremented,
{\ell}
was forced out,

p
was transmitted, and
\varrho
appeared in
{Q}
.

(d)
In Case (d1), for
\tau\in[d_{p},d_{\varrho})
, we have

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)+1
as in the transmission step the time was increased,
but
{\ell}
was forced out and
p
was transmitted.
It follows that
{Q}
has no tight slots in
[d_{p},d_{\varrho})
, so

\textsf{prevts}({Q},d_{\varrho})=\beta
and
\textsf{nextts}({Q},d_{p})=\gamma
, which means that
in
{Q}
the interval
(\beta,\gamma]
forms one segment.

In Case (d2), the definitions of
\beta=\textsf{prevts}({P},d_{p})
and
\gamma=\textsf{nextts}({P},d_{\varrho})
, together with

d_{\varrho}<d_{p}
, imply that
(\beta,\gamma]
is indeed a segment of
{P}
. In particular, this means that

{\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)\geq 1
for
\tau\in[d_{\varrho},d_{p})
. For such slots
\tau
we thus obtain that

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)-1
because in the transmission step the time was increased,
{\ell}
was forced out,
and
\varrho
was added to
{Q}
.
∎

Note that after transmitting
p\in{P}(\alpha,{T}]
(without adjusting packet weights or deadlines appropriately),
the slot monotonicity property does not hold. This is because
{\textsf{minwt}}(d_{\varrho})
decreases,
as
{\textsf{minwt}}({P},d_{\varrho})>w_{\varrho}
but
{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},d_{\varrho})=w_{\varrho}
.

Appendix B
Appendix: Leap Step of Algorithm
PlanM

Algorithm
PlanM
artificially adjusts weights and deadlines of some pending packets in leap steps,
and therefore the results from the previous section are not sufficient
to fully characterize how the optimal plan evolves during its computation.
Lemmas
A.2
and
A.4

remain valid for Algorithm
PlanM
, but we still need a variant of
Lemma
A.5
that will characterize the changes in
the optimal plan after a leap step. We provide such a characterization below, see Lemma
B.1
.
Then we use this lemma to prove Lemma
B.2
, the slot-monotonicity property for leap steps.

In the following, similar to the previous section, we use
{P}
for
{P}^{t}
(the optimal
plan after all packets at time
t
have been released) and by
{Q}={\widetilde{{P}}}^{t+1}

we denote the optimal plan just after the event of transmitting
p
, incrementing the time,
and changing weights and deadlines (but before new packets at time
t+1
are released).

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose that
t
is a leap step of Algorithm
PlanM
in which
p
was transmitted,
and let
\varrho=\textsf{sub}({P},p)
be the substitute packet of
p
.
Let packets
h_{0}=p,h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
and tight slots

\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{k}=\gamma=\textsf{nextts}({P},d^{t}_{\varrho})
be as defined in the algorithm.
Furthermore, let
\beta=\tau_{-1}=\textsf{prevts}({P},d^{t}_{p})
.
Then:

(a)

{Q}={P}\setminus{\left\{p,{\ell}\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
; in particular, if
k\geq 1
, all packets
h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{k}
are in
{Q}
.

(b)

w^{t}_{p}=w^{t}_{h_{0}}>w^{t}_{h_{1}}>w^{t}_{h_{2}}>\cdots>w^{t}_{h_{k}}>w^{t}_{\varrho}
.

(c)

h_{k}
’s deadline is in the segment of
{P}
ending at
\gamma
,
that is,
\textsf{prevts}({P},d^{t}_{\varrho})<d^{t}_{h_{k}}\leq\gamma
.

(d)

The
{\textsf{pslack}}()
values change as follows (see Figure
B.1
):

(d.i)

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)-1
for
\tau\in[t+1,d^{t}_{\ell})
.

(d.ii)

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)
for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\ell},\beta]
.

(d.iii)

If
k\geq 1
,
then for
i=0,\dots,k-1
we have the following changes
in
(\tau_{i-1},\tau_{i}]
:

(d.iii.1)

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)
for
\tau\in(\tau_{i-1},d^{t}_{h_{i}})\cup{\left\{\tau_{i}\right\}}
.

(d.iii.2)

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)+1
for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{h_{i}},\tau_{i})
.

(d.iv)

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)
for
\tau\in(\tau_{k-1},\min(d^{t}_{h_{k}},d^{t}_{\varrho}))
.

(d.v)

For
\tau\in[\min(d^{t}_{h_{k}},d^{t}_{\varrho}),\max(d^{t}_{h_{k}},d^{t}_{\varrho}))
, there are two cases:

(d.v.1)

If
d^{t}_{h_{k}}<d^{t}_{\varrho}
, then
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)+1

for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{h_{k}},d^{t}_{\varrho})
.

(d.v.2)

If
d^{t}_{\varrho}<d^{t}_{h_{k}}
, then
{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)-1

for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{k}})
.

(d.vi)

{\textsf{pslack}}({Q},\tau)={\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)
for
\tau\geq\max(d^{t}_{h_{k}},d^{t}_{\varrho})
.

(e)

Any tight slot of
{P}
is a tight slot of
{Q}
, but there might be new tight slots of
{Q}

in
[t+1,d^{t}_{\ell})
and, in Case (d.v.2), also in
[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{h_{k}})
. Thus, in general, we have

\textsf{nextts}({Q},\tau)\leq\textsf{nextts}({P},\tau)
for all
\tau\geq t+1
.

(f)

w^{t+1}_{h_{i}}\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},d^{t+1}_{h_{i}})
for any
i=1,\dots,k
, and
w^{t+1}_{\varrho}\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},d^{t+1}_{\varrho})
.

Proof.

(a) The claim clearly holds for a simple leap step by Lemma
A.5
,
i.e., when
k=0
, because in this case the algorithm does not decrease deadlines, and
increasing the weight of
\varrho
does not change the new optimal plan
{Q}
, by Observation
A.1
(b).

Thus consider an iterated leap step.
Let
\overline{{Q}}
be the optimal plan after
p
is transmitted and the time is increased,
but before the adjustment of weights and deadlines in line
11

of the algorithm is taken into account. From Lemma
A.5
we have

\overline{{Q}}={P}\setminus{\left\{p,{\ell}\right\}}\cup{\left\{\varrho\right\}}
;
in particular, packets
h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{k}
are in
\overline{{Q}}
.
Our goal is to prove that
\overline{{Q}}={Q}
.

By Observation
A.1
(b), increasing the weights of packets in the optimal
plan cannot change the plan, so it is sufficient to show that the feasibility and optimality of the
plan is not affected by the decrease of the deadlines of packets
h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{k}
.

By Lemma
A.5
, all segments of
\overline{{Q}}

between
\beta=\textsf{prevts}({P},d^{t}_{p})
and
\gamma=\textsf{nextts}({P},d^{t}_{\varrho})

get merged into one, which means that
{\textsf{pslack}}(\overline{{Q}},\tau)\geq 1
for any
\tau\in(\beta,\gamma)
.
For
i=1,\dots,k
, decreasing the deadline of
h_{i}
from
d^{t}_{h_{i}}
to
d^{t+1}_{h_{i}}=\tau_{i-1}

decreases
{\textsf{pslack}}(\overline{{Q}},\tau)
by
1
for
\tau\in[\tau_{i-1},d^{t}_{h_{i}})
.
All
k
intervals where these decreases occur
are contained in
(\beta,\gamma)
and, since
d^{t}_{h_{i}}\leq\tau_{i}
for all
i
, these intervals do not overlap.
Thus, after decreasing the deadlines of the
h_{i}
’s,
all values of
{\textsf{pslack}}()
will remain non-negative for
\overline{{Q}}
, so
\overline{{Q}}

remains a feasible set of packets.
Decreasing these deadlines cannot make previously unfeasible sets of packets feasible,
implying that
{Q}
remains optimal. Thus
\overline{{Q}}={Q}
, as claimed.
In particular, packets
h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{k}
remain in
{Q}
.

(b) By the choice of
p
in the algorithm,

p
is the heaviest packet in the segments of
{P}
in
(\beta,\gamma]
, because
\varrho
is the substitute packet
for any packet in
{P}
with deadline in
(\beta,\gamma]
. Thus for each
i=1,\dots,k
,
since
d^{t}_{h_{i}}\in(\beta,\gamma]
, we get
w^{t}_{p}>w^{t}_{h_{i}}
.
The ordering of weights of
h_{i}
’s follows from the definition of
h_{i}
’s in
line
9
of the algorithm’s description.
Finally, for any
i=0,\dots,k
inequality
w^{t}_{h_{i}}>w^{t}_{\varrho}
holds, because
\varrho\notin{P}

and
d^{t}_{h_{i}}<\gamma=\textsf{nextts}({P},d^{t}_{\varrho})
.

(c) This holds by the definition of
h_{i}
’s in line
9

and by the condition of the while loop in line
7
, which stops the loop
when
\tau_{i}=\textsf{nextts}({P},d^{t}_{h_{i}})=\gamma
.

(d) For any slot
\tau\in[t+1,\beta]\cup(\gamma,{T}]

the value of
{\textsf{pslack}}(\tau)
is not affected by the decrease of the deadlines of
h_{i}
’s,
since
h_{i}
’s are both in
{P}
and in
{Q}
and since their old and new deadlines are in
(\beta,\gamma]
.
We thus get exactly the same changes of the
{\textsf{pslack}}()
values outside
(\beta,\gamma]
as
in Lemma
A.5
.
Regarding slots in
(\beta,\gamma]
, Lemma
A.5
(d) shows that
{\textsf{pslack}}(\tau)

decreases by 1 for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{\varrho},d^{t}_{p})
if
d^{t}_{\varrho}<d^{t}_{p}
,
and increases by 1 for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{p},d^{t}_{\varrho})
if
d^{t}_{p}<d^{t}_{\varrho}
.
In the former case, we have
\tau_{0}=\gamma
as
d^{t}_{\varrho}
and
d^{t}_{p}
are in the same segment, that is
k=0
.
In the latter case, we sum the increase of values
{\textsf{pslack}}(\tau)
for
\tau\in[d^{t}_{p},d^{t}_{\varrho})

with the changes of the
{\textsf{pslack}}()
values due to decreasing the deadlines of
h_{i}
’s, analyzed in (a),
and we get the changes summarized in (d); see Figure
B.1
.

Part (e) follows from (c) and (d), which imply
that the value of
{\textsf{pslack}}({P},\tau)
does not increase for any tight slot
\tau
in
{P}
.

Part (f) follows from the way the weights are changed in lines
4
and
11

of the algorithm.
∎

Figure B.1:
An example with
k=3
illustrating the proof of Lemma
B.1
(d), by
showing changes of the
{\textsf{pslack}}()
values in an iterated leap step.
Here, notations
\Delta{\textsf{pslack}}({P}\rightarrow\overline{{Q}})
,

\Delta{\textsf{pslack}}(\overline{{Q}}\rightarrow{{Q}})
, and

\Delta{\textsf{pslack}}({P}\rightarrow{Q})
represent the changes of the
{\textsf{pslack}}()

in the two substeps (from
{P}
to
\overline{{Q}}
and from
\overline{{Q}}
to
{{Q}}
),
and the overall change from
{P}
to
{Q}
, respectively.
Up and down arrows represent increases and decreases of the values of
{\textsf{pslack}}()
in individual slots.

Next, we show that, thanks to the deadline adjustments in Algorithm
PlanM
,
the slot monotonicity property for optimal plans is preserved in leap steps.

Lemma B.2
.

If step
t
is a leap step of Algorithm
PlanM
, then

{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},\tau)\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
for any
\tau\geq t+1
.

Proof.

We use notation from Lemma
B.1
. By Lemma
B.1
(e) all tight slots of
{P}

are tight slots of
{Q}
(in particular,
\beta
and
\gamma
remain tight slots);
thus
\textsf{nextts}({Q},\tau)\leq\textsf{nextts}({P},\tau)
for all
\tau\geq t+1
.
Fix some
\tau\geq t+1
, and let
a
be the packet that realizes
{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},\tau)
, that is
the minimum-weight packet in
{Q}
with
d^{t+1}_{a}\leq\textsf{nextts}({Q},\tau)
.
We need to show that
w^{t+1}_{a}\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
. We have three cases.

Case 1
:

\tau\leq\beta
.
Lemma
B.1
(a) shows that
{\ell}
is forced out of the optimal plan,
thus not in
{Q}
, and otherwise the set of packets in the optimal plan with deadline at most
\beta

does not change, that is,
{Q}[t+1,\beta]={P}[t,\beta]\setminus{\left\{{\ell}\right\}}
.
It follows that
a\in{P}
and its weight and deadline remain the same.
We thus have
d^{t}_{a}=d^{t+1}_{a}\leq\textsf{nextts}({Q},\tau)\leq\textsf{nextts}({P},\tau)
,
so
a
is considered in the definition of
{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
, which implies

w^{t+1}_{a}=w^{t}_{a}\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
.

Case 2
:

\tau\in(\beta,\gamma]
.
If
a\notin{\left\{h_{1},...,h_{k+1}\right\}}
(in particular, this means that
a\neq\varrho=h_{k+1}
),
then
a
is also in
{P}
with the same deadline and weight.
As
\textsf{nextts}({Q},\tau)\leq\textsf{nextts}({P},\tau)
, we get
d^{t}_{a}\leq\textsf{nextts}({P},\tau)
,
hence
w^{t+1}_{a}\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
follows as in Case 1.

Next, suppose that
a=h_{i}
for some
i\in{\left\{1,\dots,k\right\}}
(excluding the case
a=h_{k+1}=\varrho
).
Recall that, as a result of changes in line 11 in the algorithm, we have

w^{t+1}_{a}\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau_{i-1})
and
d^{t+1}_{a}=\tau_{i-1}
.
The definition of
{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},\tau)
gives us that
\tau_{i-1}=d^{t+1}_{a}=\textsf{nextts}({Q},\tau)
,
so
\tau>\textsf{prevts}({Q},\tau_{i-1})
. Thus,
since tight slots of
{P}
are tight also in
{Q}
, implying
\textsf{prevts}({Q},\tau_{i-1})\geq\textsf{prevts}({P},\tau_{i-1})
, we get that
\tau>\textsf{prevts}({P},\tau_{i-1})
.
So
\tau
is in the segment
(\textsf{prevts}({P},\tau_{i-1}),\tau_{i-1}]
of
P
.
By definition, for each segment of
{P}
the value of
{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau^{\prime})
is constant
for all
\tau^{\prime}
in this segment. Therefore
{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau_{i-1})={\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
,
and we conclude that
w^{t+1}_{a}\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau_{i-1})={\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
.

Finally, consider the case when
a=\varrho
. Recall that
w^{t+1}_{\varrho}={\textsf{minwt}}({P},d^{t}_{\varrho})
and
d^{t}_{\varrho}=d^{t+1}_{\varrho}
.
We have
\tau>\textsf{prevts}({Q},d^{t}_{\varrho})
, which implies that
\tau>\textsf{prevts}({P},d^{t}_{\varrho})=\textsf{prevts}({P},\gamma)
.
Thus
\tau
is in the segment
(\textsf{prevts}({P},\gamma),\gamma]
of
P
, just like
\varrho
.
It follows that
w^{t+1}_{\varrho}={\textsf{minwt}}({P},d^{t}_{\varrho})={\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
.

Case 3
:

\tau>\gamma
. The set of packets in the plan with deadline strictly after
\gamma
does not change
and also their weights and deadlines remain the same.
Thus if
d^{t}_{a}>\gamma
, then using
\textsf{nextts}({Q},\tau)\leq\textsf{nextts}({P},\tau)
again,
we obtain that
w^{t+1}_{a}\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
.
Otherwise,
d^{t}_{a}\leq\gamma
, thus
w^{t+1}_{a}\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({Q},\gamma)\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\gamma)\geq{\textsf{minwt}}({P},\tau)
,
where the second inequality follows from Case 2 and the third one from
\gamma<\tau
.
∎

Appendix C
Appendix: Simpler Variants of Algorithm
PlanM

In this appendix, we provide some intuition on why the iterative choice of packets
h_{i}
in Algorithm
PlanM

(line
9
) is essentially needed in an iterated leap step.
We first give an example of an instance showing that a memoryless variant of our algorithm does not achieve ratio
\phi
;
this variant simply transmits packet
p
according to Line 1 of Algorithm
PlanM
(and does not make any
changes of packet weights and deadlines).

This memoryless algorithm does not maintain the slot-monotonicity property, so this still leaves open
the possibility that perhaps some simpler way of maintaining this property is sufficient to
achieve
\phi
-competitiveness. To address this, we further give an instance which
shows a lower bound higher than
\phi
on the competitive ratio
of two simpler variants of Algorithm
PlanM
that maintain the slot-monotonicity property.

C.1
Counterexample for the Memoryless Variant of
PlanM

The memoryless variant of Algorithm
PlanM
, denoted
PlanM-Memoryless
, is very simple:
In each step
t
, transmit packet
p\in P^{t}
that maximizes
w_{p}+\phi\cdot w(\textsf{sub}^{t}(p))
.
That is, it chooses the packet for transmission in the same way as Algorithm
PlanM
, but it does
not make any modifications of the instance. We now show that
PlanM-Memoryless
is not
\phi
-competitive.

Recall that slot-monotonicity is preserved if Algorithm
PlanM

executes a packet from the initial segment in each step (see Lemma
A.4
).
For such instances,
PlanM-Memoryless
is equivalent to Algorithm
PlanM
and thus it achieves ratio
\phi
.
Hence, a hard example for
PlanM-Memoryless
needs to involve some leap steps.

We give an example of an instance for which
PlanM-Memoryless
is not
\phi
-competitive.
Our instance uses three interleaved infinite sequences of packets with weights increasing geometrically by
factor
\phi^{2}
, similar to the examples in Section
4
. These packets will now have span
4
.
The basic idea is that
the competitive ratio in each step (i.e., the ratio of the weights of packets
transmitted by the optimum and by the algorithm) will be
\phi^{2}
on these geometric sequences.
In the example in Section
4
, the online algorithm’s ratio on the geometric sequence is also
\phi^{2}
,
but it can catch up with the optimum at the end, as in the last slots
it still has some heavy packets pending that are not pending for the optimum. The trick behind our construction is to
add several packets near the end that will force
PlanM-Memoryless
to make some leap steps in which these new
packets will be transmitted, while the older packets will expire.

The instance is defined in Figure
C.1
.
To ensure consistent tie-breaking,
we use a small parameter
{\epsilon}>0
in packet weights.
The three sequences of packets with span
4
and geometrically increasing weights are

{\left\{\varrho_{i}\right\}}_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
{\left\{x_{i}\right\}}_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
{\left\{z_{i}\right\}}_{i=1}^{\infty}
.
When estimating profits, we will also include packets
\varrho_{0}=\varrho
,
x_{0}=x
, and
z_{0}=z
in these sequences, even
though the spans of
\varrho
and
z
are not equal to
4
.
This figure also shows the schedule computed by
PlanM-Memoryless

and the optimal schedule. The steps of
PlanM-Memoryless
are explained below:

Steps
t=...,-2,-1,0
:

As in the example in Section
4
, when scheduling
the packets in steps
t=...,-2,-1,0
, the algorithm will always transmit the packet that is tight at the given step.
This is because at each step
t=...,-4,-3
there are four packets pending
a,b,c,a^{\prime}
, with respective deadlines
t
,
t+1
,
t+2
and
t+3
,
and with weights that satisfy
w_{a}<w_{b}<w_{c}<w_{a^{\prime}}=\phi^{2}w_{a}
. Thus, breaking the tie between
w_{a}
and
w_{a^{\prime}}
, the
algorithm will transmit
a
. For times
t=-2,-1,0
the deadlines do not satisfy the above condition; nevertheless, by inspection, the
algorithm will still schedule the tight packet
w_{a}
in these steps. In other words, the three interleaved packet sequences do not interfere with each other.

Step
1
:

The pending packets are
\varrho,x,z,{\dot{z}},s^{\prime},s
and the
optimal plan is
P^{1}={\left\{z,x,s^{\prime},s\right\}}
(with each packet in its own segment); see Figure
C.2
.
We also have
\textsf{sub}^{1}(s)=\textsf{sub}^{1}(s^{\prime})=\textsf{sub}^{1}(x)=\varrho
,
\textsf{sub}^{1}(z)=z
,

w_{s}>w_{s^{\prime}},w_{x}
, and

w_{s}+\phi{\,}w_{\varrho}\;=\;(\phi^{2}+2\epsilon)+\phi(1-\epsilon)\;=\;(1+\phi)w_{z}+(2-\phi)\epsilon\;>\;w_{z}+\phi{\,}w_{z},

so the algorithm will transmit
p=s
.

q

Figure C.2:

Optimal plans of
PlanM-Memoryless
in steps
1
,
2
and
3
.

Step
2
:

The pending packets are
\varrho,x,s^{\prime},{\dot{x}},\varrho^{\prime}
and the optimal plan is

P^{2}={\left\{x,s^{\prime},\varrho\right\}}
. Also,
\textsf{sub}^{2}(s^{\prime})=\varrho^{\prime}
,
\textsf{sub}^{2}(x)=x
,

\textsf{sub}^{2}(\varrho)
is a packet of weight
0
, and

\displaystyle w_{s^{\prime}}+\phi{\,}w_{\varrho^{\prime}}\;

\displaystyle=\;2+2\epsilon+\phi\cdot\phi^{-2}\;=\;(1+\phi)w_{x}+2\epsilon\;>\;w_{x}+\phi{\,}w_{x},

\displaystyle w_{s^{\prime}}+\phi{\,}w_{\varrho^{\prime}}\;

\displaystyle>\;w_{\varrho}+\phi{\,}0,

so the algorithm will transmit
p=s^{\prime}
.

Step
3
:

The pending packets are
\varrho^{\prime},\varrho,e
, the optimal plan is
P^{3}={\left\{\varrho^{\prime},\varrho\right\}}
,

\textsf{sub}^{3}(\varrho)=e
,
\textsf{sub}^{3}(\varrho^{\prime})=\varrho^{\prime}
, and

w_{\varrho}+\phi{\,}w_{e}\;=\;(1+\phi)w_{\varrho^{\prime}}+(\phi-1)\epsilon\;>\;w_{\varrho^{\prime}}+\phi{\,}w_{\varrho^{\prime}},

so the algorithm will transmit
p=\varrho
.

Step
4
:

The only (non-zero weight) pending packet for the algorithm in this step is
e
, so this is the packet transmitted.

It remains to calculate the profits of
PlanM-Memoryless
and of
OPT
. As
{\epsilon}
can be made
arbitrarily small, in these calculations we assume that
{\epsilon}=0
.
We also assume for simplicity that the three interleaved geometric sequences are backwards-infinite.
Recall that
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\phi^{-2i}=\phi
.

For
OPT
, the profit in steps
...-2,-1,0
from the three interleaved geoemtric sequences,
is
\phi(w_{\varrho}+w_{x}+w_{z})=\phi(1+1+\phi)=3\phi+1
.
Its profit in steps
1,\dots,4
is
w_{{\dot{z}}}+w_{{\dot{x}}}+w_{s^{\prime}}+w_{s}=\phi+1+2+\phi^{2}=2\phi+4
.
In total,
w({\textsf{OPT}})=5\phi+5
.

The profit of the algorithm in steps
...,-2,-,1,0
is
\phi^{-2}
times that of
OPT
,
that is,
(3\phi+1)/\phi^{2}=\phi+\phi^{-1}
.
In steps
1,\dots,4
, its profit is
w_{s}+w_{s^{\prime}}+w_{\varrho}+w_{e}=\phi^{2}+2+1+0=\phi+4
.
Thus,
w({\textsf{ALG}})=\phi+\phi^{-1}+\phi+4=3\phi+3
, and thus the ratio
equals
w({\textsf{OPT}})/w({\textsf{ALG}})=5/3>\phi
.
(In fact, a slightly higher ratio can be achieved by having four interleaved geometric sequences,
which would allow the optimal schedule to also include the packet corresponding to
\varrho^{\prime}
.)

Finally, we remark that even if we parameterize
PlanM-Memoryless
by
\alpha

and select a packet
p
maximizing
w_{p}+\alpha\cdot w(\textsf{sub}^{t}(p))
,
we do not obtain a
\phi
-competitive algorithm. It would be interesting to know the competitive ratio
of
PlanM-Memoryless
for the best choice of
\alpha
.
The currently best memoryless deterministic algorithm is
1.893
-competitive
[
14
]
.

Comparison to Algorithm
PlanM
.

For comparison, consider what will our Algorithm
PlanM
do on this instance.
The computation up to and including step 0 will be identical to that of
PlanM-Memoryless
.
In step 1 the same packet
s
will be transmitted, but the weight of its substitute packet
\varrho
will be increased to
1
in Line 4 of the algorithm.
Similarly, in step 2
s^{\prime}
will be transmitted, but the weight of its substitute packet
\varrho^{\prime}
will be increased to
1
.
Then, in step 3
PlanM
will transmit
\varrho^{\prime}
(whose weight is now
1
) and in step 4 it will transmit
\varrho
.
This will increase the profit of the algorithm by
w^{0}_{\varrho^{\prime}}=1/\phi^{2}
(the original weight of
\varrho^{\prime}
), and its competitive ratio on
this instance will be
\frac{5\phi+5}{3\phi+3+1/\phi^{2}}<\phi
.

C.2
Counterexample for Simpler Variants of
PlanM

As explained in Appendix
A
, the value of
{\textsf{minwt}}^{t}(\tau)
may decrease only
if a packet
p
from a non-initial segment of
P^{t}
is transmitted. This is because in such a case

p
is replaced in the optimum plan by a packet
\varrho
with
w_{\varrho}<{\textsf{minwt}}^{t}(d_{\varrho})
.

PlanM
maintains the slot-monotonicity property by adjusting the weights and deadlines of some pending packets.
First, it increases the weight of the substitute packet
\varrho
to
{\textsf{minwt}}^{t}(d_{\varrho})
. The example in the
previous section showed how this weight increase of
\varrho
helps in reducing the overall ratio on some instances.

Second, if
\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d_{p})<\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d_{\varrho})
(i.e., in an iterated leap step),
then
PlanM
also makes an intricate shift of iteratively chosen packets
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
.
This shift is implemented by decreasing the deadlines and it prevents the algorithm from merging segments of
P^{t}
.
In this section we address the question whether this shift process is necessary. To this end,
we consider two simpler variants of
PlanM
that maintain
the slot-monotonicity property as well
3
3

3

The slot-monotonicity property for both variants of
PlanM
follows from arguments similar to the proofs in Appendices
A
and
B
.
We do not include the proofs here as our only aim is to show that these algorithms are not
\phi
-competitive.
,
but do not perform an iterative shift
of packets
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
. Both variants only differ from
PlanM

in iterated leap steps, i.e., they use the same rule for choosing a packet to transmit and
also increase the weight of
\varrho
to
{\textsf{minwt}}^{t}(d_{\varrho})
in each leap step.

In the first variant, called
PlanM-Simpler
and defined in Algorithm
3
,
instead of choosing packets
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
, we identify the heaviest packet
{h^{\ast}}

in the segment of
P^{t}
containing
d^{t}_{\varrho}
, we decrease its deadline to

\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d_{p})
, and, if its weight is too small to preserve slot monotonicity, then we appropriately increase it.
Recall that
\alpha=\textsf{nextts}^{t}(t)
is the first tight slot of
P^{t}
.

Algorithm 3

Algorithm
PlanM-Simpler
(
t
)

1:

transmit packet
p\in{{{P}}^{t}}
that maximizes
w^{t}_{p}+\phi\cdot w^{t}(\textsf{sub}^{t}(p))

2:

if

d^{t}_{p}>\alpha

then

\triangleright
“leap step”

3:

\varrho\,{\leftarrow}\,\textsf{sub}^{t}(p)

4:

w^{t+1}_{\varrho}\,{\leftarrow}\,{\textsf{minwt}}^{t}(d^{t}_{\varrho})

\triangleright
increase
w_{\varrho}

5:

\eta\,{\leftarrow}\,\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d^{t}_{p})
and
\gamma\,{\leftarrow}\,\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d^{t}_{\varrho})

6:

if

\gamma>\eta

then

7:

{h^{\ast}}\,{\leftarrow}\,
heaviest packet in
P^{t}(\textsf{prevts}^{t}(d^{t}_{\varrho}),\gamma]

8:

d^{t+1}_{{h^{\ast}}}\,{\leftarrow}\,\eta
and
w^{t+1}_{{h^{\ast}}}\,{\leftarrow}\,\max\big(w^{t}_{{h^{\ast}}},{\textsf{minwt}}^{t}(\eta)\big)

The second variant is even simpler as we just decrease the deadline of
\varrho

if it is after
\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d^{t}_{p})
and increase
w_{\varrho}
to match the minimum of
{\textsf{minwt}}()

in its new segment. We call this algorithm
PlanM-EvenSimpler
and summarize it in Algorithm
4
.

Algorithm 4

Algorithm
PlanM-EvenSimpler
(
t
)

1:

transmit packet
p\in{{{P}}^{t}}
that maximizes
w^{t}_{p}+\phi\cdot w^{t}(\textsf{sub}^{t}(p))

2:

if

d^{t}_{p}>\alpha

then

\triangleright
“leap step”

3:

\varrho\,{\leftarrow}\,\textsf{sub}^{t}(p)

4:

d^{t+1}_{\varrho}\,{\leftarrow}\,\min\big(\,d^{t}_{\varrho},\textsf{nextts}^{t}(d^{t}_{p})\,\big)

5:

w^{t+1}_{\varrho}\,{\leftarrow}\,{\textsf{minwt}}^{t}(d^{t+1}_{\varrho})

\triangleright
increase
w_{\varrho}

Instance with ratio above
\phi
.

We now present a hard instance, for which the ratio of both algorithms exceeds
\phi
.
The weights of some packets in this instance are parametrized by a parameter
\epsilon>0
, that can be made arbitrarily small.

Our instance is an extension of the lower-bound instance for
PlanM-Memoryless

described in Section
C.1
. In this instance
we will use
five
(instead of just three) backwards-infinite interleaving sequences of packets whose weights increase geometrically
by factor
\phi^{2}
. These packets will have span
6
. The principle is the same as in Section
C.1
:
At each step
t
the algorithm will have six packets
a_{0},a_{1},...,a_{5}
pending, with each
a_{i}
having its deadline at
t+i
.
The weights will satisfy
w_{a_{0}}\leq w_{a_{i}}\leq\phi^{2}w_{a_{0}}
for
i=2,3,4
and
w_{a_{5}}=\phi^{2}w_{a_{0}}
.
Thus, breaking the tie, the algorithm will transmit
a_{0}
at each such step.
This way, at each step the current ratio between the optimum profit and the algorithm’s profit will be
\phi^{2}
,
although the algorithm will always have some remaining heavy packets pending that are not pending in the optimum schedule.
At the end of the instance we release several new packets that will be transmitted by the algorithm instead of
these older pending packets (which will expire).

Figure
C.3
shows the complete instance. It also shows the schedules of
PlanM-Simpler
(also represented by striped yellow rectangles), of
PlanM-EvenSimpler
, and of an optimal solution (pink rectangles).
Note that the schedules of
PlanM-Simpler
and
PlanM-EvenSimpler
are essentially the same, as the only difference is that slots of
h
and
\varrho
are swapped.

We now explain the behavior of
PlanM-Simpler
on instance in Figure
C.3

(for
PlanM-EvenSimpler
, it is nearly the same). As explained earlier, the instance starts with five interleaved sequences of packets

{\left\{\varrho_{i}\right\}}_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
{\left\{x_{i}\right\}}_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
{\left\{h_{i}\right\}}_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
{\left\{r_{i}\right\}}_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
and
{\left\{z_{i}\right\}}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, all of span
6
, with weights forming a geometric sequence with factor
\phi^{2}
.
We think about these as being grouped into batches of
5
.
Both algorithms
PlanM-Simpler
and
PlanM-EvenSimpler
will transmit tight packets in each step, while the adversary will always choose the
heaviest packet to schedule.
Later we have one more batch of five packets. These packets have different spans but their weights extend these geometric sequences,
so we call them
\varrho=\varrho_{0}
,
x=x_{0}
,
h=h_{0}
,
r=r_{0}
and
z=z_{0}
. For these packets the same basic principle applies,
and in steps
-5,-4,-3,-2,-1
the algorithm’s schedule will follow the same pattern.
In step 0, for the same reason again, the tight packet
r
will be transmitted.

An example for algorithms
PlanM-Simpler
and

. The original packet weights are in the table on the left;
packet spans are depicted in the figure, represented by a thick line segment.
At the bottom, the figure shows the schedules of algorithms
PlanM-Simpler
and

, as well as the

The schedules of

are also represented by unit-length thin rectangles (pink for the optimum and yellow and striped for

PlanM-Simpler
) attached to the line segments representing packet spans.

The crucial step is step 1. In this step
PlanM-Simpler
transmits packet
p=s
, since
s
is the heaviest pending packet,

\varrho
is the only substitute packet
available, and
w_{s}+\phi w_{\varrho}>\phi^{2}w_{z}
, which holds for all
\epsilon>0
.

PlanM-Simpler
increases the weight of
\varrho
to
{\textsf{minwt}}(5)=1
,
decreases the deadline of packet
h
to
2
, and increases its weight of
h
to
{\textsf{minwt}}(2)=\phi
.
This is illustrated in Figure
C.4
, that shows the optimal plans of
PlanM-Simpler
in step 1 and step 2.
(
PlanM-EvenSimpler
also transmits
p=s
, decreases the deadline of
\varrho
to
2
, and increases its weight to
\phi
.)

Figure C.4:

Optimal plans of
PlanM-Simpler
in steps
1
and
2
. The tables show packet weights.
Note that the weight of
\varrho
changed, and for
h
both the deadline and weight changed.

After step 1, there are no leap steps in the remaining four steps as there won’t be any non-expiring packet
outside the optimal plan (that could serve as a non-zero weight substitute packet) and at each step the weights of pending packets are within
a factor of
\phi^{2}
. (Note that packet
y
will not appear in the plan in step 2, even though it is
heavier than the
original
weight of
h
.)

Next, we calculate the profits. Since
\epsilon
can be made arbitrarily small, in the calculations we can assume that
\epsilon=0
.
For the optimal schedule, adding up the weights of the five interleaved packet sequences, up to step -1,
their total weight is
\phi(w_{\varrho}+w_{x}+w_{h}+w_{r}+w_{z})=5\phi+1
.
(Recall that
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\phi^{-2i}=\phi
.)
The optimal profit in steps
0,1,...,5
is
w_{s}+w_{z^{\prime}}+w_{y}+w_{g}+w_{g^{\prime}}+w_{\varrho^{\prime}}=\phi^{2}+\phi+\phi+\phi^{2}+\phi^{2}+1=5\phi+4
.
Thus
w({\textsf{OPT}})=10\phi+5
.

The profit of either algorithm before step
0
(i.e., from the five interleaved sequences) is smaller by factor of
\phi^{2}
than the
corresponding profit in the optimum schedule, so it equals
(5\phi+1)/\phi^{2}=4\phi-3
.
The algorithm’s profit in steps
0,1,...,5
is

w_{r}+w_{s}+w_{h}+w_{x}+w_{g}+w_{\varrho}=1+\phi^{2}+1+1+\phi^{2}+1=2\phi+6
.
(Here, of course, we take the original weights into account).
Thus its total profit is
w({\textsf{ALG}})=6\phi+3
, giving us ratio
w({\textsf{OPT}})/w({\textsf{ALG}})=5/3\approx 1.666
.

What goes wrong in the analysis.

We explain where the analysis in the paper fails for Algorithm
PlanM-Simpler
(the reason is basically the same for
PlanM-EvenSimpler
).
Consider step 1, and assume that
\epsilon=0
.
In this step, using the profit of
\phi\cdot w_{s}=\phi^{3}
, the algorithm needs to “pay” for all of the following:
(i) the adversary profit from
z^{\prime}
, equal to
\phi
, (ii) removing
z
and
s
from
{{\mathcal{B}}}
, which costs
1+\phi
,
(iii) increasing the weight of
\varrho
, which is negligible,
and (iv) increasing the weight of
h
, which costs
\phi^{-1}
, as the potential increases as well.
The total cost thus sums to
\phi^{3}+\phi^{-1}
;
it is no coincidence that the excess of
\phi^{-1}
over the profit equals
w({\textsf{OPT}})-\phi\cdot w({\textsf{ALG}})
,
since all other steps are essentially tight (in terms of calculations in the analysis).

Comparison to Algorithm
PlanM
.

For comparison, consider what our Algorithm
PlanM
does on this instance.
The computation up to and including step 0 remains identical to that of
PlanM-Simpler
.
We focus on step 1. In step 1 the same packet
s
gets transmitted, and the weight of
\varrho

is also increased to
1
.
However,
PlanM
further performs its iterative shift, with
k=2
,

h_{1}=g
, and
h_{2}=h
. Figure
C.5
shows the effect of this change on the
plan in step 2, in which
PlanM
transmits
g
, unlike
PlanM-Simpler
which transmits

h
in this step.
